可如今，赋闲给了他认真反省的工夫，尼古拉斯手肘支膝、两手空垂，把这一切翻来覆去地磨。老一套的自我开脱不灵了。指望新的一代能强些，也没有什么像样的指望。博比是个招人疼的孩子，这不假。他那稳当的机智、那照人的笑容、那份对祖父心绪永不错失的体貼，都让尼古拉斯欢喜；可他看不出半点成器的指望。除了钢琴弹得像职业艺术家、交友的本事与留住交情的能力俱属少见、又居然混完了大学，博比丝毫看不出会干出一件值得一想的事来。他将开车与喝酒，赌博与高尔夫，打猎与钓鱼，娶个晕头晕脑、挥霍无度、涂着猩红嘴唇的小摩登女郎再把她腻烦掉；他将在加拿大避暑，在戛纳过冬，剪他的息票，与裁缝磋商，资助交响乐团，在几家慈善机构的信笺上挂名，等共和党的总统候选人进城时上台亮个相；末了，被人推进那座空荡荡、嗡嗡回响的哥特式陵墓，葬在克利福德——那个败家子——的旁边。
哦，希望之光也有过那么一两次；不过是磷火，一闪即灭；只够在火光熄了之后，把黑暗衬得更浓。
孙子毕业那年的最后一天……那是期中毕业班，典礼延到毕业季一并举行……尼古拉斯驱车去州立大学所在的小城，同老友化学系主任共进午餐。加兰（Garland）教授开口时，他直起了腰，满面生光：“不知你是否留意，梅里克，你家那个野小子，有当化学家的坯子。”
“当真？你跟他谈谈；行吗，加兰？这话出你之口，分量不一样！”
加兰把他那杯茶兑热水的功夫拉成了一套冗长的仪式，才答道：“化学是苦差事，老兄！你家小子知道自己用不着吃苦！”
尼古拉斯的脸垮了下来，加兰又宽慰道：“怪不得他。既能从生活里找乐子，何苦围上胶皮围裙，在烂浆糊和毒臭里搅和。”
---
今夜，一副重担从老尼古拉斯肩头卸了下来。这桩天大的好消息来得猝不及防；他心花怒放，可那塌陷的双肩，恰恰量出了他方才甩脱的担子有多沉。他把一只羊皮纸色的棕色手掌，慈爱地搭上博比的胳膊；两人一同从饭厅踱进宽敞的书房。这是老人的圣所。四壁从胡桃木梁的天花板直到中国地毯，密密实实排满了书柜，装着无出其右、也无人问津的典籍。尼古拉斯如今赖以精神食粮的，多半是些情节与手法雷同得出奇的侦探小说。倒不是尼古拉斯无心读更好的书；他只是懒得再想事了。
两人踱进书房，倦乏的老人喟叹一声，陷进他心爱的椅子深处。一本花哨封皮的侦探小说摊开着，脸朝下扣在桌上。博比拿起来，念出书名，咧嘴一笑。
“点火吗，爷爷（Grandpère）？”
他把火苗凑到老人的雪茄头上。
“故事够刺激？”
尼古拉斯像一只漏风的风箱，使劲喷了几口，答道：“探长正盘问厨娘呢，博比；她说她确知枪响正是在十一点十分，因为那正是她照例放猫出门的钟点。”
“这位厨娘的厨房习惯，您这会儿该烂熟了吧，爷爷。这些故事里的厨娘，都是同一位吧？”
“绝非如此，先生。”尼古拉斯抗议，“上一个厨娘是个男的！”
博比心痒着独处，急着把祖父送回小说里，好让自己脱身。过去一个钟头的感情折腾，着实累人。他向老人吐露的心事，凝着许多天的斟酌；还有好些夜晚，他在屋里来回踱上几个钟头，把他那桩草定的决心从各种可能遭驳的角度翻来覆去地掂量。如今主意已定，告知祖父一声，才算公道。他说了。他有意避开了戏剧性的场面。他腻烦场面；他是被场面泡大的。可老尼古拉斯熬过的绝望与忧煎太多了，听这年轻人平平静静宣布出一套计划——一举把自己押上一桩代价惨重的奉献，而且就期限而言遥遥无期——不由得不被托举到一种昂扬的境界里去。
博比把话抛出来之后，有那么一刻，老人坐在那儿，呆若木鸡，疑心听错。他放下了叉子。下巴颏松了，颏骨颤颤地上下磕碰。他隔着桌子眯眼望去，嘴边的深纹与眼角的皱纹连成了一道道半圆。他把疙瘩盘结的老手指抠进雪白的桌布，胳膊肘撑住分量，用嘶哑的假声喝问：“你说什么，罗伯特？我恐怕没听清你方才的话！再说一遍！”
博比又说了一遍，慢慢的，静静的，斩钉截铁。老尼古拉斯沟壑纵横的脸抽搐起来，他用斑驳的手背揉着深眼窝的眼角。
“你是个有胆的孩子！”他说，声音劈了。
随即他又为自己的失态害臊，狠狠清了清嗓子，挺直腰背，郑重宣告：“我向你道贺，先生！我想不起我的族人里，谁做过比你更要紧的决定！愿上帝——保佑你！”这句祝福带着颤音……两人都差点承受不住。
此后一个钟头，博比把自己的前程摊开来讲，眼界之阔、条目之清，足证他在这上头耗了多少时日心血；老人狮鬃似的脑袋跟着他每个字频频点动，不时还一拳砸在桌上，为他的赞许加重分量。“对，先生！”他会炸雷似的吼，“你办得到！你一定办得到！你有这个底子！我从来就说你有！”他那股劲头，恍然回到了当年的好时光——那时候，要说服董事们“不管多大代价、必须立刻大改方针以应变局”，全凭一顿捶桌。
如今第一波热情的大潮头已经碎作浪花退去，博巴巴不得这话题暂且搁下。这一个月，他同他的难题同吃同睡——梦里是它，踱步是它，摔跤是它，哄过它，咒过它；如今总算把它送到一个近乎见分晓的地步，他乐得看它搁置起来。
尼古拉斯觉察出孙子立在旁边摆弄镇纸时的那股心不在焉，便从容地找到书页，细细擦净眼镜，堆出一个再明白不过的告辞的笑。
“您要看书的话，我就出去遛个弯儿，爷爷。”博比说。
尼古拉斯连连点头；心满意足地吧哈着烟，埋进了他的故事里。
然而博比转身刚走，他就撂下书，直勾勾望着那远去的背影；一双老眼里睁着一种他还不曾习惯的、崭新的兴味。博比穿过门口时回头一望，咧嘴一笑。尼古拉斯一把抓起书，对着刚撞见的一段艰深文句紧皱眉头，把长雪茄抽得呼呼作响。
---
博比换下便鞋穿上网球鞋，脱下晚宴上装套了件薄毛衣，从车库门出去，上了车道。晴空里悬着半个月亮，还有几点萤火。他顺着车道漫无目的地走，下了道上草地，沿玫瑰棚架旁的窄径闲逛，不觉踱到大门那对粗大的双柱跟前，又信步上了公路。这不算什么通衢大道，只是一条窄窄的碎石汽车路，主要伺候着湖西岸稀稀落落、正门朝着公路的几处乡间庄园。今夜路上极静。他双手插兜，脑袋前倾着闷头想心事，大步流星，全不计较走到哪里去；两眼渐渐适应了幽暗。
他把话告诉了祖父，心里痛快。明天他要开车去布莱特伍德，告诉南希·阿什福德。这两个人分担了他的秘密，好比迎风一面下了两枚重锚。他盼着南希·阿什福德说一句“很妥当！不出我所料！”便点到为止。他最不擅长应付情感涨满的场面。祖父那里当然不要紧。他上了年纪，心肠软了。可他盼着南希识大体。
博比走出去一英里。前头一百码，路的急拐弯上，一对耀眼的头灯以岌岌可危的角度斜指着，说明有辆车重重地向右侧倾了过去……掉沟里了，他猜。他听见发动机突然的空吼——马力狠狠加在干打转的车轮上。“生手。”他寻思。紧接着又是一声引擎的死命咆哮，宣告有人正把糟糕的事越弄越糟。“蠢货！”他嘟囔一声，加快了脚步。
显然司机看见了他，拿不准他的来意，想赶他到车前之前再搏一把；因为他离车只有几步远时，发动机当真恼羞成怒地嚎起来，大轿车浑身发抖。方向盘后坐着个年轻女子。
“我的天，大姐，”等那阵喧嚣平息，博比喊道，“别再这么干了！”
大姐瞪圆了眼睛领受这番训诫；博比凑近了，饶有兴味地端详这双眼睛。她一笑，他修正了先前对她的估量。她多半是开不惯松软的碎石路；不识这路面的奸诈；换个地方，兴许是位顶呱呱的好司机。
“陷得真有那么深吗？”她焦急地问。
她的嗓音里有一种奇特的沙哑，赋予它一种促膝的、只你我知的、交心的音色。
博比走到车后看了看。
“深得很！”他断言，“到毂了。你的差速器都贴地了。”
她一脸困惑。“我不知道那是什么，”她承认，“可我敢说它不该贴着地。”
“不该，”博比一副长辈口气，“离了地，它们干活儿才像样。”
她叹了口气，用一小片花边按了按温热的脖颈。
“怪我，我猜，”她懊恼地说，“我开得忒快了；拐那个急弯时，对面一辆车冲过来，装的灯跟飞机场用的一样亮。我一让，就滑出去了……”
“于是就成了这副光景！”博比接完，“幸亏没翻。翻了可要重伤的。”
她端详他隐在暗影里的脸，为他话语里那点关切她的安危而微微一动……没这份关切，兴许倒更安全。她看见的那张脸，没叫她不安。
“好了，至少咱们没有断骨头要操心。眼下我只要把车弄回路上。有什么高见？这种事我一窍不通。可别误会，”她赶紧补一句，“我可不是惯把车开沟里的。”
“我信你不是。”博比宽厚地说，“这碎石路滑得很。”
“您说我该怎么办？”她问，那口气干脆把全部责任卸到他手里。
就像她把那一点轻份量靠在了他身上。过去半个钟头，他一直觉得自己是世上最孤独、最彻底无所依附的人。他那桩重大决断，把他从习惯的种种兴味里整个切断了开来，却还没把他接到什么新的兴味上。从没有人这么急需一份友谊。
他把一条胳膊肘支在敞开的车窗沿上，说起俏皮的教书腔。
“碰上这种事，本地动力既然不济，按例该求援。把邻居请出来。邻居们呢，早疑心有朝一日要轮到他们出力，天一擦黑就钻了被窝，非得用大动静的擂门外加一笔国王赎身的谢礼，才擂得出来。等他们沐浴、剃须、更衣、用过早饭，这才骂骂咧咧地来，拖着一台喷鼻息的拖拉机……”
“等他们一切就绪要拉了，拖绳断了；只好再开拖拉机回镇上另买一根。”
“八九不离十。”博比认可。
“您的高见很清楚，”她顺着他的调子说，“第一，去找邻居。”她扳着指头记下这一项，“可是——找哪家？”
“哪家邻居？”博比呵呵一笑，反问，“还是——咱俩谁去？……当然是我去，乐意效劳。不过，”他命令般地添了一句，“你也得来！我不能把你一个人撂在这儿守着一辆趴窝的车！”
这话是脱口而出的。多半不过是一时不自觉的专断，意思是把一个姑娘独自撂在这条僻静路上，他认为不妥。可这话的热度、那声“我不能把你一个人撂在这儿”里透出的占有意味，给了她一种异样的感觉。从没有人用这一模一样的口气对她说過话。她觉得……怎么说呢……仿佛自己正被一点点吸纳……极小极小的一点……只是她极微小的一粒；像沙漏那针一般细的颈口里，第一缕几乎看不见的细沙在泻下；自然没什么可惊的。她什么时候愿意，把漏斗一倒转，便什么都住了。眼前呢，由它流着，倒也不坏；图个新鲜罢了。流不了多少的。这个她自有分寸。再过半个钟头，她和这个讨人喜欢的小伙子——那侧影轮廓利落得配得上希腊古币——便各奔各路。他爱发号施令就发去，她迁就他，立正，磕一下鞋跟，便是。
博比拉开车门，伸出手。她毫不犹豫地握住，跨下车，站到路上。
“我该不该把排挡锁上？”她问。
“不用，”博比慢悠悠地说，“咱们回来它还在。”两人都笑了。
下了公路，他们钻进一条树篱夹峙的窄巷，是从一大片茂密的高冷杉中辟出来的。
“但愿你知道咱们这是往哪儿去。”看博比大步在前，她说。
“说不上。”他老实承认，“我从没进来过；不过这想必是通到福斯特家庄园的私家路。前头总会撞见哪家佃户的农舍。”
姑娘在他旁边跟着走，他一步她得迈两步——脚上那高跟，原就不是为走乡间小道设计的。一头羊从左边的树篱里窜出来，没命地横穿路面，离他们只几步远。她本能地一把抓住博比的袖子。
“哎呀——吓我一跳！”
“来！挽着我的手！”
她递给他的是只小手；他牵着它，像牵着个小孩儿。尽管自己也觉得荒唐，他待她的态度，是护着自己人的那种；而她也明知孟浪，回应却发乎自然。她有一种感觉：自己那小得不能再小的一丝气息，正被这个居高临下的小伙子有力的手指，静静地吸了去。
心里，博比把攥握收紧了些；外表上，他像引着亲妹妹似的引着她。
“咱们没准儿会撞上种种奇遇呢。”她提议，“万一咱们闯进一窝——一窝造假币的！”
“如今可没有造假币的了。”博比嗤之以鼻，“全贩私酒去了……利厚，风险小。”
“哦——我恨透这一行了！”她激动地喊，“这东西如今变得多俗、多恶心！早先与我毫不相干。可如今……它在毁我最好的朋友！”
博比对心里突然那一刺醋意感到恼火。可他有什么资格吃醋？
“我恨它，也有十足的理由。”他悻悻地应道；随即咬牙啐了一句，“可我想我已经把它踩住了！”
“哦，巴不得如此！”她急促地吸了口气，喊道，“那多可惜……”
她的话悬在半空，好一阵子两人都没作声。
“还从没有人对我说过，那会——可惜。”
于是——倒转沙漏的时辰到了。她这就倒……回头就倒……可不能倒得太急……怎样才算把一只沙漏不露痕迹地、慢慢地倒过来呢？她纳闷。也许到时候自然有章法……眼下……沉默越拉越长；而沉默，在这个节骨眼上，最是扰人。
“不用人说，你早就知道了，对不对？”
“说不上我知道。对旁人来说，这算不得什么大事。”
“多糊涂！……竟没有一个人在乎你把自己糟蹋不糟蹋？”
“这话听着是挺耸动的，是不是？……倒像我要演苦命孤女了。”
“你这一向——挺消沉的，是不是？”小心！……小心！……这么着，沙漏可倒不过来！
“消沉透了！……可是——这会儿不了！”
“这就好！……重新攥紧？”
两人谁也说不清，那一瞬间彼此的握手为什么会紧了一紧。自然，是那个字眼引出来的。他手指在她手上骤然一紧，不过是个肯定；或许是对她鼓励的一声领情。而她敏捷的回应，也不过是给一个同类的友好表态——他跌倒过，如今正在往上走。可两人都明白——并且凭直觉明白对方也明白——这是一份默契；一种奇得说不清的归属感……过了一刻，她把手抽了回去，随即立刻醒悟：这一抽，抽错了……这一撤手，倒像是表白之后的退却……还不止呢，这番抽回远不该那么仓促。她的手指是从他掌心里慢慢地滑出去的，还被他流连的压力轻轻挽了一下……这么说，沙漏到底还是倒了，是吗？……
“哦，我看见灯了！”她喊，“一扇窗里有灯！”
两人一路打趣着预测会受怎样的接待，脚下加紧，不多时便敲响了农舍的门。一个农夫开了门，立在悬顶的乙炔灯光里，门框正好把他嵌住。两个小孩一人抱住他一条腿，满脸好奇。
三言两语讲妥，农夫回身取帽子，出来随二人上路，说他随后就到，便去发动拖拉机。
望见农舍而打断的那席话，博比无意续上。只是转身往回走时，他握起这位新朋友的小手，把它掖进自己臂弯。她落落大方地由他握着。
“你这是要回大学了吧，我猜。”他试探道。
“不，今年不回去了……你呢？”
“哦，我念完了。”博比老气横秋地说，“过几天就开始我的专业课程。”
“法律——也许是？”
“你就替我挑这个？”
她笑了。
“要替你选职业，我总得多知道你一点才成。”
“那么……要换成你是个男人……”
“我就干外科。”
“专攻哪一门？”
“专，”她答得干脆，“我就当脑外科医生。”
“真怪！”
“怎么怪？”
她这一问没有下文。轰隆隆的拖拉机追了上来。两人已近公路，谈话让位给正事。
拖拉机折腾半天总算摆好了位。博比坐进轿车的方向盘后；车主站在得体的距离外，等车被拽回路上。事情干脆利落办完了；可轿车的这位临时司机却结结巴巴说，转向装置也许该检查检查。这么一别，有时会伤着转向齿轮，他说。人家也没逼他细讲。他说，或许最好开到村里去，保险起见，查个周全。她若愿意，他乐意奉陪……她求之不得，劳驾他了；可否请他来开？……处此情形，兴许这样更好。
她问农夫要多少工钱，付的钱比他索的还多。农夫道了谢，笨手笨脚，假意推辞不肯多收。她上车，坐进方向盘后以主自居的博比身边；他心跳得飞快；那农夫一心套近乎，说：“这车真是个宝贝！活脱儿像哈德森医生从前在这边开的那辆帕卡德。”
“就是那辆。”姑娘轻声说，“晚安；再谢您。”
博比·梅里克机械地挂上挡——已故哈德森医生的大轿车——两人朝村子驶去。
“车没事了，是吧？”那位黑衣的年轻女子快活地说；哈德森医生从前在这边开的，正是她这辆大轿车。
看样子，她的新朋友还没有十足把握答这话。他两眼盯住前方的路，方向盘攥得指节都发了白。
博比痛切地觉着身旁座位上的她；对她的觉者，胜过他认识过的任何一个女人。两人并无实际的身体接触；可她就是在那儿，铺天盖地地在那儿。
“没事了。”他含混地咕啺了一声。
“反正我正要上村里去，”她接着说，“真巴不得那家小药房还没关门。”
看样子她的司机对药剂师的夜间作息并不知情，因为他没敢搭腔。
就在这时，油门被多踩了一脚，强劲的轿车猛地窜了出去。
“真不知怎么谢你才好，”姑娘说，为他这一阵沉默微微发窘，“你若不是碰巧路过，我可真不知道该怎么办了。”
博比正忙着调点火杆。
“可我怕给你添了好多麻烦。”她又不安地补了一句。
他隔了半天才答，像从很远的地方。
“车况完好。”他说，“我在这儿下车。您不必去车库了。”他果断地刹车，车猛地停住。博比推开左侧车门，跨了出去。
“哎，可你离开你遇着我的地方还有好几英里呢！”她喊道，“务必让我送你到方便的地方。求你了！”
他不敢看她的眼睛。“我就是随便遛遶。”他心不在焉地说，“不要紧。”
她挪到方向盘后，伸出手。他用手指握住时，那手微微有些颤。她惶惑了。她到底说了什么伤了他的话？
“那么，晚安；多谢了！”她的声音发颤。
他握着她的手，多留了一瞬，说了声“晚安”——那腔调也许是疲惫，是沮丧，也许是失望——转过身，一步跨进了黑暗里。她挂上挡。车缓缓地、试探地、迟疑地向前移动。博比望着那盏小小的红色尾灯，直到它在下一个转弯处消没。
半个钟头后，他在温迪米尔坟墓般冷寂的客厅里，在钢琴前坐下，满腹心事，信手试着给舒伯特的《未完成交响曲》续一个实验性的结尾。

## IX / 九
===Original===

“Grandpère,” said Bobby, slowly twirling the stem of his glass, “I wish you would tell Tom about the time you and Mr. Anderson butchered the pigs at the Country Club.”
The talk had been growing too serious. Masterson had an annoying tendency, when exhilarated, to become didactic. It was all clever enough, informative, interesting﻿—but more dogmatic than suited the temper of a Thanksgiving dinner.
By a circuitous route they had arrived at the problem of caste in America, Masterson conducting the excursion and pointing out the essential facts of interest to be noted along the way. Bobby was for a change of topic. Old Nicholas had some convictions on this subject. He would probably not express them to the same degree with which he believed them; but Masterson must not be encouraged to deliver himself of too many sentiments unshared by his genial host.
“Why shouldn’t there be a leisured class? Loafers﻿—if you like that word better? This was about the only sign America had exhibited that she was maturing, ever so little, in a few restricted zones. Wasn’t our brand of democracy dangerous? Referendum﻿—huh! What did the average voter know? Every nation that had become great had achieved its distinction through the leadership of small social and intellectual minorities!”
Masterson was still talking﻿—Hyde Parking, almost﻿—a bit flushed about the temples. Bobby determined upon a change of subject.
Old Nicholas was not in accord with many of young Masterson’s views, but he liked his views better than the manner in which he delivered them, and liked both the views and the delivery better than the way he drank his wine.
Meggs had been told to bring up a long-neglected bottle of choice Burgundy. In the opinion of the elder Merrick, wine like this deserved to be caressed, its colour admired, its fragrance inhaled, its story reconstructed. It was to be sipped, drop by drop. It pleased him that Bobby seemed to know how. Liquid sunshine, it was; sunshine that had warmed the pleasant banks of the Rhone, in tranquil days before the war. One did not toss it off at a draught. Masterson gulped it. It was good to see Bobby close his eyes as his lips touched the rim of the glass, as if he saw merry groups of bare-legged little girls tramping it out in the shade at the corner of some Provençal vineyard. The boy was maturing rapidly, reflected old Nicholas. He had the posture and gesture of a man.
“Bobby occasionally insists upon this story,” said Nicholas obediently, “I have repeated it, at his request, many times.”
“And each time it is better,” commented Bobby, appreciatively.
“Let’s have it then, by all means!” shouted Masterson, much more boisterously than he had intended, an error he instantly endeavoured to correct by bowing very formally and dignifiedly in an affirmative to Meggs’ unspoken query as the bottle was again tilted, tentatively, inquiringly, over his glass. Meggs had tried to put into the gesture just the right degree of reserve, but the finesse was a bit too subtle for his customer.
---
“Well,” said Nicholas, clearing his throat, “it was this way. You see, where Axion now stands, there used to be nothing but pasture fields. Joe Anderson and I, when we were kids, had a contract with most of the village folks to drive their cows out to pasture, every morning, in the summer, and bring them home in the evening to be milked. Each cow was worth a dollar a month to us.
“Out there, in those fields, where we freckled-faced youngsters, with stone bruises on our calloused feet, sat all day, whittling kite sticks, playing mumble-peg, and bragging about the teams we captained at school﻿—three-old-cat; you never played it, either of you﻿—Joe and I later built a couple of big factories which quite changed the look of the landscape.
“These factories also brought changes in most people’s mode of living. Many men, who might have spent all their lives in ordinary financial circumstances, came to have quite a little money. They built big houses, and their children took on airs. It was hard to recognize the place, after a while, and even harder to recognize some of the people.
“Well, no matter how much absorbed we became in our business, Joe and I remained, through the years, just kids﻿—to each other. I suppose it was a subject for many little jokes with other people. We kept up our boyish relations﻿—always bragging about our speed, strength, and endurance, whenever we met, and invariably in the language we had used in the old days. Like ‘Gemunee Crickets!’ and ‘Gosh darn ut!’ and ‘Fer the love o’ Mike!’ It was rather silly, I’ll admit; but we enjoyed it.
“Joe and I used to help our fathers with the hog butchering in November. Every family, in those days, had a few hogs in a pen. The time came, when we were in our late ’teens, that the annual slaughter was left pretty much to our management, and we usually combined forces﻿—pulling it off at the Anderson place, where they had better facilities for such work. We became quite expert butchers, and were very proud of our accomplishment. I dare say our fathers flattered us in the hope of making us too vain to see how glad they were to dodge the job.
“Well, one day, after Joe and I had retired from our business concerns, we were having lunch together at the Country Club. It was brand splinter new; had been open only a week. The only natural hazard, on the golf course, was a little stream where Joe and I used to fish for crawdads and minneys. Nobody ever said ‘minnows.’ On the knoll, where the new club house stood, there had been quite a nice little grove. Joe and I used to sit among those trees, on a log, slapping mosquitoes, and watching for grey squirrels. He owned an old muzzle-loading shotgun. Mighty dangerous toy, too. Wonder we weren’t killed a dozen times.
“We were having a good time reminiscing. It seemed queer to be sitting there, on that spot, lunching at a solid mahogany table sprinkled with Venetian doilies that slid about under crested silver. Everybody belonging to that club had plenty of money, and the club house had been built and equipped without regard for expense. There was a lot of swank too. The servants seemed to have been let into the secret that this was one of our better clubs. The institution was by way of becoming a bit snobbish, we feared. Practically all the natives who belonged to it had been derived from quite humble origins, like Joe’s and mine; and we worried a bit about the decline of the old democracy. It struck us that the mere fact of our having accidentally made a little money didn’t require us to pretend we were of the British peerage.
“At adjoining tables sat some of the second-growth crop of Axionites﻿—male and female﻿—talking about polo and Derbies and regattas and Biarritz and grouse in Scotland; and we thought the general tone of the place should be improved. So we fell to discussing some of the good old times the Axion boys and girls used to have before the automobiles came along and scared the horses off the roads, and drove the cows out of the pastures to make room for the golfers. Then, one of us remembered about the butchering.
“Joe recalled that one time we put on a contest to see which of us could dress a hog in the shortest time. I recalled the incident perfectly, but was sure I had won the small stake we had put up. Joe disputed me so strongly that the neighbouring tables became interested and, presently, alarmed. When we observed that we had collected quite a sizable audience, we obligingly continued our debate chiefly for their benefit. The upshot of the argument was that Joe and I bet a thousand dollars aside that each of us could dress a hog in less time than the other. The old chaps, when they heard of it, insisted that the contest be held in the grillroom, and a book was made which, I learned afterwards, involved bets running into scandalous sums.
“The engagement was put on the club calendar. I’m not sure it was pointed to with much pride by the younger set; but the old fellows seemed to want it there, and, seeing their wishes really had to be consulted, more or less, the polo crowd waived their objections. On the next Tuesday, after luncheon, no man went back to his office﻿—if he had one. Two live pigs﻿—pretty good sized ones, too﻿—were fetched in crated. A tarpaulin was spread on the grillroom floor, and Joe and I stripped to the skin and put on butchers’ togs. We went through it, from squeal to sausage. I was told, afterwards, that the club house had to be gone over, from crypt to spire. They had grease on the stairsteps and bristles in the soup and cracklings in the rugs for days thereafter.”
“Did it improve the democratic tone of the club?” asked Masterson, laughing.
Old Nicholas shook his head and smiled.
“No. I don’t think so. Once you outgrow the simplicities, you can’t recover them or even remember them with any satisfaction. It’s not far, you know, from corn pone to plum pudding, but it’s a long way back!”
“What do you make of our new second generation, Mr. Merrick?” inquired Masterson.
“You mean﻿—yours and Bobby’s, perhaps? Yours is the third, you know﻿—counting from where I am. Well﻿—I presume there’s more to be hoped for from yours than from the one that immediately preceded you.”
Nicholas’ eyes strayed in Bobby’s direction, and he continued, “There’s a youngster, for example, preparing to be a doctor. His father, at his age, was an amateur deer-slayer. When Clif heard about this hog-dressing episode, he was considerably excited. I said, ‘But, Clif, I heard you bragging about skinning and curing a buck.’ ‘Oh, well,’ he said, ‘that was quite a different matter.’﻿ ﻿… And﻿—I ’spect it was,” he added generously, after a pause.
“Our family”﻿—there was a trace of cynicism in Masterson’s tone﻿—“has been curiously undisturbed by the problems incident to the accumulation of large fortune. My father is the editor of a small town paper in Indiana. His father was a country doctor, his grandfather a Methodist circuit rider. All the trouble that money has ever given us has been how to get enough of it to pay our bills﻿ ﻿… But, so far as Bobby is concerned, he isn’t running true to type, at all﻿ ﻿… He’s a biological﻿—or, should one say﻿—a fiscal sport?”
Nicholas was meditative.
“Well, yes﻿—Bobby’s case is, as you say, somewhat unusual.”
Masterson’s corrugated brow signed that he was tuning his kettledrums to this key and would presently be holding forth again unless promptly checked.
“Let’s not bother to diagnose my case,” protested Bobby, with an amicable growl. “Besides, I haven’t done anything yet. And this is no time for serious talk﻿ ﻿… Grandpère﻿—how about telling Tommy the story of the time you and Mr. Anderson bet on which one of you could mow the most hay in an hour.”
Masterson furtively glanced at his watch and was caught at it by old Nicholas who immediately pushed back his chair, ignoring Bobby’s suggestion, and led the way toward the big drawing-room where he paused to toy with the music on the piano desk.
“What’s this *Unfinished Symphony*, Bobby?﻿ ﻿… Mind playing it for us?”
“Not much in a mood for that one, Grandpère﻿ ﻿… Too stuffed. We need something a bit livelier.”
“How about this one﻿—‘Neapolitan Nights’?”
“Pretty good; but it’s rather soft and sticky too for a holiday celebration.”
“Something with a lot of bang in it, then,” said Nicholas, sinking with a satisfied sigh into a deep chair.
Masterson edged up to Bobby and muttered, under his breath, “I say, do you think your grandfather would take it nicely if I ran along? I’ve promised to look in, later, at Gordon’s. There’s a special little party on there tonight﻿ ﻿… a Revue﻿ ﻿… Wouldn’t care to come along, would you?”
“Who will be there?”
“Oh﻿—everybody! The old gang﻿ ﻿… that you’ve snooted so damnably for months!”
Bobby was thoughtful for a moment; then, quite on impulse and greatly to Masterson’s surprise, he said, “I believe I will, Tommy. I’d rather like to see the bright lights again, myself. It’s been a good while.”
Masterson drummed nervously on the top of the piano. Recalling himself suddenly, he said with enthusiasm, “Attaboy, old mole! Let’s tell Grandpère and hop﻿ ﻿… Just make the eight-twenty-five﻿ ﻿… In at midnight. Right time to be there.”
“You weren’t taking anybody, were you?”
“Well﻿—yes,” Tom admitted hesitantly. “That is, I had a tentative arrangement to pick up Joyce Hudson, around midnight, and drive her out﻿—provided it was quite agreeable I should return from here. But there’s no reason why you can’t come with us, is there?”
“Not so good!﻿ ﻿… Don’t care to horn in﻿ ﻿… But﻿—I’ll run down with you and spend the night at Grandpère’s club﻿ ﻿… Have to be there in the morning, anyway﻿ ﻿… Perhaps I’ll drop in for a look at Gordon’s﻿ ﻿… We’ll see.”
Old Nicholas was glad enough to have done with both of them. So long as they stayed, he must be mindful of his sacred obligation as host. Late that afternoon he had arrived at a most perplexing situation in *The Tragedy in Stateroom 33*, and was feverish to discover whether the pilot, whom the count had tied up in the closet, would contrive to release himself and warn the American girl before the motor-launch returned with the conspirators.
“Willing you should go?” rumbled Nicholas, as they scampered upstairs, “Great Snakes!”

===Chinese===

“爷爷，”博比说着，缓缓捻着酒杯的杯脚，“劳您跟汤姆讲讲，您跟安德森先生在乡村俱乐部杀猪的那一回。”
席间的谈话正越谈越沉重。马斯特森有个恼人的积习：三分酒意一上头，就好为人师。他讲的倒也聪明，见闻广，有趣——只是教条气太重，不合感恩节晚餐的脾胃。
他们兜了个大圈子，谈到了美国的门第问题；一路由马斯特森领队兼导游，沿途的名胜一一指点。博比想换个话头。老尼古拉斯对这个题目自有几条坚定的信念。他未必肯把它们和盘托出；可绝不能纵容马斯特森在他这位和气的东道主面前，倾倒太多旁人不与共享的高论。
“为什么不该有个有闲阶级？闲人——爱用这个词也随你！这差不多是美国仅有的征兆，说明她在几片有限的区域里，多少开始成熟了一点点。咱们这一牌子的民主，难道不危险吗？全民公决——哼！选民平头百姓懂什么？凡成了大国的，哪一家不是靠一小撮社会与智识上的精英少数带队，才挣下的功业！”
马斯特森还在滔滔——几乎有点海德公园演讲的架势——鬓角微微见红。博比打定主意换题。
老尼古拉斯对这位小马斯特森的许多见解并不同意；不过比起他发表见解的做派，他倒还更喜欢他的见解；而这见解和做派两样，又都比他喝酒的喝法顺眼。
梅格斯早得了吩咐，去地窖里取来一瓶搁置多年的上等勃艮第。在老梅里克看来，这样的酒，该被爱抚，该被赏色，该被闻香，它的来历该被重新讲述。它该一滴一滴地啜。叫他欢喜的是，博比看着懂这个门道。这是液体的阳光；是战前太平岁月里，晒暖了罗讷河宜人两岸的阳光。这可不是拿来一仰脖干杯的。马斯特森却拿它灌。博比的嘴唇一沾到杯沿便阖上眼，仿佛望见普罗旺斯哪处葡萄园的荫凉角落里，一群光着腿的小姑娘快活地踩着葡萄——老尼古拉斯瞧着，心里熨帖。这孩子长进得快，老尼古拉斯暗想。他已有了成年人的坐相与做派。
“博比偶尔偏要听这段故事，”尼古拉斯从善如流地开了腔，“应他所求，我讲过不知多少遍了。”
“一遍比一遍精彩。”博比赏识地说。
“那就务必讲讲！”马斯特森嚷道，嗓门比他本意闹得大多了；他随即着力找补，在梅格斯又一次试探地、探询地朝他杯上倾瓶、无声地发问时，极郑重极体面地点头示意要。梅格斯原想把这一下倾瓶的分寸拿捏得恰到好处；只是这点细腻，他的客人略嫌消受不起。
---
“喏，”尼古拉斯清了清嗓子，“是这么回事。你们知道，如今阿克西恩立着的地方，从前只有一片片牧场。乔·安德森（Joe Anderson）和我还是半大孩子那阵，跟村里大多数人家订了合同：夏天每天清早把各家的奶牛赶出去吃草，傍晚再赶回来挤奶。一头牛，一个月一块钱。
“就在那片野地里，我们这帮脸上长雀斑、脚底板磨出茧、茧底下硌着石子伤的小子们，整日价坐在那儿削风筝骨、玩钉刀游戏、吹嘘各自在校里领衔的球队——三垒老猫球；你们俩谁都没玩过吧——乔和我，后来就在那儿盖起了两座大工厂，把那片风景彻底改了模样。
“这两座工厂也改了大多数人的活法。许多原可能一辈子安于寻常境况的人，手里渐渐有了几个钱。他们盖起了大宅，儿女们也端起了架子。隔了些年，那地方叫人认不出来了；有些人，更叫人认不出来。
“呢，不管生意上陷得多深，这些年里，乔和我彼此之间始终还是那两个孩子。想必旁人拿这个编了不少小笑话。我们保持着孩子气的交情——每次碰面，照旧吹自己的速度、力气、耐久，一成不变地用当年那套话。什么‘好家伙，蚂蚱精！’‘见鬼去！’‘老天爷发慈悲！’说来犯傻，我认；可我们受用。
“乔和我打小年年十一月帮着各家父亲宰猪。那年月，家家院里都圈着几口猪。等到我们十七八岁，一年一度的宰牲，差不多全交给我们张罗；我们通常合兵一处——在安德森家办，那儿的家伙什齐备些。我们练成了相当在行的屠户，十分得意这门手艺。我料想，父亲们拼命捧我们，是存心把我们夸得晕头转向，好遮掩他们自己躲了这份差事有多乐。
“后来有一天，乔和我都已从各自的买卖上退下来，正在乡村俱乐部共进午餐。那俱乐部崭新锃亮；开张才一个礼拜。球场上的唯一天然障碍，是一条小溪——乔和我从前就在那儿捞蝼蛄、逮小鱼。没人管小鱼叫‘鲦鱼’。新俱乐部会馆立的那座小丘上，原有一片挺不错的小树林。乔和我常坐在林子里一根倒木上，拍蚊子，守着灰松鼠出窝。他有一杆老前膛火铳。也是件要命的玩具。真奇怪我们没叫它送上西天十几回。
“我们叙旧叙得正欢。坐在那个位置上，在一张实心红木餐桌上用饭，桌面铺着满铺打滑的威尼斯小花巾，银器都錾着纹章——这一切透着古怪。那俱乐部的会员个个有钱，会馆造起来置办起来不惜工本。排场也足。仆役们仿佛也被透了底：这是咱们上等俱乐部中的一家。这机构眼看要沾上一股势利气，我们担心。入会的本地人，出身几乎都跟乔和我一样寒微；老派的民主眼看要没落，我们有点儿发愁。我们琢磨着，侥幸挣了几个钱这件事，并不等于必须装出一副英国世袭贵族的做派。
“邻桌坐着阿克西恩的第二茬新贵——男女都有——张口马球、德比、赛船、比亚里茨、苏格兰的松鸡；我们觉得，这地方的风气该整一整了。于是我们谈论起汽车时代之前的那些好日子——那时路上的马还没被惊散，牧场里的牛还没被撵走给高尔夫客腾地。说着说着，我们俩有一个想起了宰猪的事。
“乔忆起有一回我们俩比赛，看谁拾掇一口猪最快。那件事我记得一清二楚，只是笃定赢家是我、注下的彩头也归了我。乔死活不认，嗓门越抬越高，惊动了邻桌，邻桌先是看热闹，继而着了慌。我们见已聚起相当可观的一圈听众，便识趣地继续争下去，主要是争给他们听。争到末了，结果是乔和我一人押下一千块，赌各自拾掇一口猪比对方快。老先生们得了信，非主张这比赛在烧烤厅举行不可；还开了盘口——我事后得知，牵涉的赌注大得骇人。
“这场赛事上了俱乐部的日程。我不敢说年轻一辈引以为荣；可老先生们执意要办，而他们的意愿多少总得依傍，打马球的那一群便撤了异议。到了下星期二，午饭之后，没有一个人回办公室去——若说谁还有办公室的话。两口活猪——个头也都不小——装在木笼里运了来。烧烤厅地板上铺了帆布，乔和我脱得赤条条，换上屠夫的行头。我们从猪叫一路干到香肠。事后听说，整座会馆从地窖到塔尖，统统重洗了一遍。此后好多天，楼梯台阶上有油，汤里漂着鬃毛，地毯里嵌着油渣。”
“俱乐部的民主风气因此改善了吗？”马斯特森笑着问。
老尼古拉斯摇头笑了笑。
“没有。我看没有。人一旦长过了朴素的年纪，就再也要不回来，连回想去都不痛快了。你可知，从玉米饼到李子布丁，路不远；可往回走，路就长了！”
“对我们这新一代的第二代，您怎么看，梅里克先生？”马斯特森探问。
“你是说——你辈和博比辈的吧？要知道，从我数起，你们是第三代了。呢——我料想，你们那一代，比紧挨着你们前头的那一代，可指望的多。”
尼古拉斯的目光溜向博比那头，接着说：“比如眼下有这么个年轻人，正预备当医生。他父亲当年在他这个岁数，是个业余猎鹿人。克利夫听说这桩拾掇猪的把戏，激动得很。我说：‘可是克利夫，我亲耳听你吹过剥鹿皮、腌鹿肉。’‘哦，那码事，’他说，‘另当别论。’……呢——想必也真是另当别论。”他顿了顿，宽厚地补了一句。
“我们这一门，”马斯特森的调子里带一丝挖苦，“对积攒巨财必然附带的种种难题，一向奇异地无所感受。我父亲是印第安纳一家小镇报馆的编辑。他的父亲是乡间医生，祖父是卫理公会的巡回牧师。钱给过我们家的全部烦恼，从来只是怎么挣够付账的数……至于博比，他可是半点不随本家风……他算是一桩生物学上的——或者说，财政上的——变异吧？”
尼古拉斯沉吟着。
“嗯，是——照你说的，博比这孩子的情形，多少有点特别。”
马斯特森额上打起的褶子表明，他正把定音鼓往这个调门上校，眼看又要开讲——除非有人当机立断地拦住。
“就不劳诊断我的病症了。”博比友好地咆哮了一声，抗辩道，“再说，我八字还没一撇呢。眼下不是说正经话的时候……爷爷——给汤米讲讲，您跟安德森先生打赌看谁一钟头割草多的那一段，怎么样？”
马斯特森偷偷瞄了一眼手表，被老尼古拉斯逮了个正着。老人立刻推开椅子，不理博比的提议，领头朝大客厅走去；到了那儿，他停下手，把琴谱架上摊着的乐谱拨弄着玩。
“这《未完成交响曲》是怎么回事，博比？……弹给我们听听，肯吗？”
“今儿没那个心情，爷爷……吃得太饱。得来点热闹的。”
“那这个怎么样——《那不勒斯之夜》？”
“不赖；可逢年过节，它也软腻了点。”
“那就来个响亮的。”尼古拉斯说着，心满意足地喟叹一声，陷进深深的椅子里。
马斯特森凑到博比跟前，压着嗓子嘀咕：“我说，要是我先走一步，你祖父会不会见怪？我答应了回头去戈登家打个照面。今晚那儿有个小型专场……一场时事讽刺剧……你不想一起去？”
“都有谁去？”
“哦——全伙儿都到！老班底……你他妈给蹬了好几个月的那帮！”
博比出了会儿神；随后，全凭一时冲动，大出马斯特森意料，他说：“我看我去，汤米。我自己也想再见见那花花世界了。好些日子了。”
马斯特森在琴顶上不安地敲着鼓点。猛然回过味来，他热乎地说：“好小子，老地龙！这就去禀报爷爷，撒腿……赶八点二十五那班……半夜进场。正是时候。”
“你原不是要带谁同去吧？”
“呢——带。”汤姆迟疑着招认，“就是说，我有个草约，半夜前后去接乔伊斯·哈德森，送她出城——前提是我从这儿脱得了身，她也乐意。可你一块儿去也没什么不妥，不是吗？”
“不大好！……我不想横插一腿……不过——我随你进城，上爷爷的俱乐部过夜……反正明早我得在那边……没准儿也去戈登家瞅一眼……再说。”
老尼古拉斯巴不得打发掉他们俩。他们多待一刻，他就得多惦记一分东道主的神圣义务。今天傍晚，他在《三十三号特等舱惨案》里刚读到最悬的一节，正心急火燎，想知道伯爵捆在壁橱里的那个飞行员，能不能挣脱出来，赶在摩托艇载着同谋回来之前，向美国姑娘报信。
“去便去吧！”尼古拉斯在他们蹬蹬蹬奔上楼去时瓮声瓮气地说，“好家伙！”

## V / 五
===Original===

It was late afternoon of the last Sunday in September. Nancy Ashford’s snug retreat, immediately adjacent to the general administrative offices in Brightwood Hospital, had proved too small to house the radiant spirits of herself and her guest. She had gratefully accepted Bobby’s suggestion that they take a drive. Sitting close to him in the big, gaudy, rakish roadster, her elbow touching his, it pleased Nancy to indulge the fancy that passersby might think him her son. Her life had been filled and emptied twice. It was brimming again.
Nothing could have been more clear to the white-haired hospital superintendent, as she greeted her expected visitor, at the door of her little office, that afternoon, than that he was hopeful of stating his errand in a manner to insure against its reception with surprise or emotion. He had a very businesslike air, and she determined to match it.
He had blurted out his story immediately upon arrival. Tossing his hat upon her desk, and seating himself by her on the little divan, he had said brusquely, “Well, I’m resolved to do it. It will be no surprise to you; for it was really your idea in the first place, even if you didn’t specify the details. It’s all arranged now. I am entering the Medical School at the University, a week from Thursday﻿ ﻿… Are you glad?”
Nancy had reached out a hand for his, winked back the sudden tears and bit her lip in an effort at control. Her eyes shone. But she did not speak.
“Of course,” continued Bobby hurriedly, as if reciting lines, “I have no illusions in this matter. It means a long, hard grind of drudgery, and I am not naturally industrious. It will be five years, at least, before I can even guess whether I am likely to succeed, or have only been making myself ridiculous. I take chances of becoming an obscure second-rater. In that case, I shall have become merely absurd at the cost of a very great deal of time and trouble. People would grin. They would say﻿—I can hear them﻿—‘Yeah; he’s the fellow that thought he’d be another Doctor Hudson.’ But maybe the threat of that will put a little more fight into me. It’s foolish, I suppose, to hope that I might sometime be even a halfway substitute for him; but﻿—I can move in that direction, anyway.”
“I felt confident you would come to some such decision as this, Bobby,” commented Nancy quietly, “and I feel even more sure﻿—now you have decided﻿—that you will succeed.”
“Your hope will help﻿—a lot!”
“Now you will be wanting to learn all you can find out about Doctor Hudson, won’t you?”
---
At this juncture, the tiny office had seemed stifling. They would ride. Bobby would drive and listen; Nancy would do the talking. For fully twenty miles they had been weaving swiftly in and out of the Sunday traffic on crowded boulevards; and, now, with speed reduced, were traversing a more quiet suburban street. Nancy had been recalling some of the more singular facts in the life of her hero, especially relating to his wide diversity of philanthropic interests and his odd whim of keeping them a secret.
“Did his family know?”
“I doubt it. Joyce was a mere baby when he began doing these queer things for people, and it is unlikely that he ever told her. And Mrs. Hudson, during a call she made at the hospital only last Thursday, asked some questions indicating he had not confided that feature of his life to her. Many of his wards and beneficiaries have been coming to see her with expressions of sympathy, and some with proffers of assistance if she needed it. She was naturally curious.”
“Yes; I know just a little about that. Learned it only today. Tom Masterson dropped in, as I was preparing to leave Windymere, at noon. He had driven Joyce up to the Hudson cottage, where Mrs. Hudson is spending a few days. He came over to inquire if I would join them there for the afternoon. I told him of my engagement in town.”
“Perhaps you should have gone. You could have telephoned to me. Have you seen Joyce at all?”
“Not since I’m back from France.”
“And I presume you never met Mrs. Hudson.”
Bobby wished that question had not been raised. Perhaps it would be near enough the truth to reply in the negative. On second thought, absolute sincerity with Nancy Ashford was but her rightful due. He decided to be honest.
“Yes,” he answered reluctantly, “I spent a whole hour with her last night, on a country road”﻿—adding after a considerable pause, “But she doesn’t know it.”
“Meaning what?” demanded Nancy, in amazement.
Briefly he narrated the whole circumstance of their meeting. Nancy Ashford’s blue eyes widened. Underneath the boyish recital ran a strong current of unmistakable personal interest which Bobby’s attempted tone of casualness failed to conceal.
“I think you liked her, Bobby. Didn’t you?”
He essayed a smile of indifference. He might even have been able to delude himself into the belief that Nancy accepted the smile as a sufficient answer to her query, had he not been so audacious with his little deceit as to look her full in the face. What he saw there of incredulity and disappointment instantly sobered him. There was no use trying to keep anything from this woman.
“My dear,” he confessed, with an unsteady voice, “I like her so much I’d rather we didn’t talk about it.”
His big car had idled to a full stop alongside the kerb flanking a little park. They were both silent for a while. At length, Nancy said mechanically, “Well, of all things!”
“Yes,” agreed Bobby abstractedly, “something like that.” There was another protracted pause.
“And she did not know who you were?”
“I couldn’t tell her.”
“How long do you think you can maintain your﻿—incognito?”
“Oh, that should be simple enough,” Bobby declared, in a tone of self-deprecation. “I took pains to invent an alibi for the evening, when I talked to Masterson, in case some inquiries might be made. But Mrs. Hudson has probably forgotten all about the little episode by this time.”
Nancy laughed.
“Bobby Merrick, do you really believe that a young woman of Helen Hudson’s temperament could produce the impression she made on you without being fully aware of it? You confessed how acutely conscious you were of her﻿—that was your phrase, wasn’t it?﻿—as you sat together in her car. Do you think you would have had that sensation had she not shared it?”
“Of course; why not? See here﻿—you’re taking altogether too much for granted in this case. Mrs. Hudson was no more than courteous, friendly, appreciative of a little favour. She had no reason to think me interested in her. In fact, I was almost rude to her when we parted.” He did not feel it necessary to add that her car had crept along, in low gear, for fully two hundred yards, apparently reluctant to leave.
“Yes,” said Nancy significantly, “she would notice that!”
“And she would know﻿—by my abruptness﻿—wouldn’t she?﻿ ﻿…”
“Know what?” persisted Nancy ruthlessly.
“Why﻿—that I was not interested.”
“Dear boy, how very little you know about her!”
“Meaning that she has unusual gifts for interpreting other people’s private thoughts?”
“No﻿—Foolish! Meaning only that she is a woman!”
They strolled under the elms, stopping to watch some small boys sailing toy boats on the little lagoon, dappled with lily-pads. A bench was found unoccupied. By common consent, their discussion was resumed of Doctor Hudson’s queer penchant for concerning himself with the private perplexities of nobody knew how many people, and the thick wall of secrecy with which these strange negotiations were surrounded.
---
“You may as well put it down as a fact,” Nancy was saying, with strong conviction, “that the curious manner of Wayne Hudson’s costly investments in these cases, from which he never expected or accepted any reimbursement, was occasioned by no mere whim. He was not given to whims. He was not an eccentric. I never knew him to do anything without an adequate motive. Nobody could have said that he was reckless with his money or incompetent in business. He could drive a shrewd bargain. He knew when to buy and when to sell. Plenty of business men, with more commercial experience, asked his advice on probable trends in the real estate market and took his judgment about industrial stocks. I am convinced he did these strange things for certain people, in this furtive way, with a definite motive. In some fashion, which I don’t pretend to understand, his professional success was involved in it. When you find out what that motive was, you’ll know why Wayne Hudson was a great surgeon!”
“Do you know any more about it than you have told me?” regarding her searchingly.
“There is a little book﻿—a sort of journal﻿—I think you have a right to know about it. He kept it in the office safe, along with valuable records; some relating to professional matters, some to private business affairs. The book was there when I took over the management of Brightwood. Once﻿—we were looking for some insurance papers﻿—I asked Doctor Hudson whether the little book concerned hospital business﻿ ﻿…”
“Couldn’t you tell?” interrupted Bobby.
“It was not written in English, nor in any other language I ever saw.”
“What did it look like﻿—Spanish, German, Greek?”
She shook her head, and resumed her story.
“I asked him what the little book was. I vividly remember how earnestly thoughtful he grew, and how he stood, for many minutes, rubbing his temple with the tips of his fingers﻿—a trick of his when trying to arrive at an important decision﻿—saying, after a long wait, ‘It’s just a personal record.’ And then he added, smiling, ‘You are at liberty to read it, if you can.’ ”
“Did you ever try?”
“*Did* I?” she echoed. “Hours and hours﻿—lately.”
“Get anything out of it?”
“Headache!”
“I wish I could see it!”
“I’ll show it to you! Nobody has a better right to it. I told Mrs. Hudson there were many valuable documents of the doctor’s in the hospital safe, and she insisted that we keep them until she was up to looking them over with me; so the book is still there.”
“Let’s go back,” he said impetuously.
Darkness had fallen before they arrived at Brightwood. Nancy brought the book from the safe, and laid it on the desk before him. He sat down, and took it in his hands﻿—a plain, black, leather-bound journal, eight inches by five, and more than an inch thick. On the flyleaf was written, in Doctor Hudson’s quite distinctive hand, the single sentence.

===Chinese===

那是九月最后一个星期天的傍晚。南希·阿什福德那间紧挨着布莱特伍德医院总办公区的舒适小屋，已容不下她和客人两下里涨满的兴致。博比提议出去兜风，她欣然从命。她紧挨着他坐在那辆花哨剽悍的大跑车里，胳膊肘贴着他的胳膊肘；南希不由得放任一个遐想：过路人兴许会把这小伙子当成她的儿子。她的生命盛满过两回，又空过两回。此刻，它又一次盈盈欲溢。
那天下午，这位白发的医院院长在小办公室门口迎候她等候着的来客时，看得再明白不过的便是：他满心想把来意讲得四平八稳，保着它不受惊讶或激动的接待。他一副十足的公事派头，她便也拿出对等的架势。
他人一到，就把事情倒了出来。把帽子朝她桌上一丢，在她身旁的小长沙发上落座，他开门见山：“呢，我拿定主意干了。你不必惊讶；说起来这原是你的主意，只不过你没细说章程罢了。如今全都安排妥了。下下个星期四，我进大学医学院……你高兴吗？”
南希伸手握住他的手，把夺眶的泪硬眨了回去，咬住嘴唇强自镇定。她两眼放光。却没说话。
“当然，”博比急急地接下去，像在背台词，“这件事我不抱幻想。这意味着一场漫长艰苦的苦役，而我天生不是个勤快人。至少得五年，我才能猜出自己是可能成器呢，还是一直在出自己的洋相。我冒着沦为无名二流货的风险。果真那样，我不过是拿大量的光阴和麻烦，换来一场荒唐。人们会咧嘴笑。他们会说——我听得见——‘嘿，就那小子，还想着当第二个哈德森医生呢。’可没准儿正是这层威胁，能逼我多几分斗志。指望哪天能顶他半个缺，我承认是傻；可是——反正我能朝那个方向走。”
“我本就笃定，你迟早会定下这样一个决心，博比。”南希平静地说，“如今你既定了——我就更笃定你会成。”
“你这份指望会帮我大忙。”
“这下你该想把哈德森医生的事，能打听的都打听了吧？”
---
到这个节骨眼上，那间小办公室显得憋闷起来。出去兜风吧。博比开车、听；南希讲。足有二十英里，他们在大街的车流里穿进穿出；此刻车速放缓，正行在一条清静些的近郊街道上。南希一直在追述她那位英雄生平中几桩较奇特的事，尤其关于他名目繁多的慈善兴味，和他将它们瞒得铁严的古怪癖性。
“他家里人知道吗？”
“难说。他开始替人干这些怪事的时候，乔伊斯还是个吃奶的娃儿，他多半不曾对她讲过。而哈德森太太——上星期四她来医院，问的几个问题表明，他生前也从未向她交过这层底。他的许多被保护人、受惠人，一直络绎登门吊唁，有的还表示若有难处愿效力。她自然纳闷。”
“是；这事我略知一二。还是今天才知道的。中午我正要离开温迪米尔，汤姆·马斯特森来了。他把乔伊斯送到湖边小屋去了，哈德森太太正在那边住几天。他过来问我去不去那边消磨一下午。我跟他说了我在城里有约。”
“你兴许还是该去的。可以打个电话给我嘛。乔伊斯你见过吗？”
“打法国回来就没见过。”
“那我料想，哈德森太太你也没见过。”
博比巴不得这问题没被提出来。答一声“没见过”，或许也算离真相不远。转念一想，对南希·阿什福德，百分之百的坦诚才偿得起她的情分。他决意说实话。
“见过。”他勉强承认，“昨儿晚上，在一条乡间路上，我陪了她整整一个钟头”——顿了好一会儿，又添上一句，“可她不知道。”
“这话怎么讲？”南希惊问。
他把两人相遇的始末约略讲了。南希·阿什福德的蓝眼睛越睁越大。这孩子气的叙述底下，淌着一股掩不住的个人兴味；博比刻意拿出的散淡腔调，一点也没能盖住它。
“我看你是喜欢她了，博比。是不是？”
他试着装出一丝淡然的笑。倘若他做这个小手脚时不那么大胆、不直直地看她的脸，他原本兴许还能骗过自己，以为南希把这一笑当成了足够的回答。他在她脸上看到的怀疑与失望，顿时让他清醒。在这女人面前，什么都瞒不住。
“亲爱的，”他招了，嗓音发颤，“我喜欢她喜欢到了——宁可咱们不谈这一节。”
他的大车已经怠速滑停在小公园旁的路缘边。两人都沉默了一阵。末了，南希机械地说：“嗬，天下竟有这样的事！”
“是啊，”博比心不在焉地附和，“差不多这样的事。”又是一场长长的静默。
“她当真不知道你是谁？”
“我不能告诉她。”
“你想你那副——隐姓埋名——还能维持多久？”
“哦，那该不难。”博比的口吻带着自嘲，“我跟马斯特森说话时，还特意给那晚编好了一套不在场证明，以防有人查问。再说，哈德森太太这会儿多半早把那段小插曲忘干净了。”
南希笑了。
“博比·梅里克，你当真以为，海伦·哈德森这样性情的年轻女子，给你留下那样的印象，而她自己会浑然不觉？你自己招的，坐在她车里那阵，你对她的‘觉着’多敏锐——这是你的原话吧？她若不曾同样觉着，你以为你还会有那份感觉？”
“当然会有；怎么不会？听我说——这案子你可想当然得太远了。哈德森太太不过是有礼、友好、承人家小忙的情分。她没有理由认为我对她有意思。其实，分手时我对她近乎无礼。”他觉得没必要再补一句：她的车当时挂着低挡，足足蠕行了两百码，像是不大舍得走。
“是啊，”南希意味深长地说，“这个她会留意到的！”
“那她也便知道——从我那份唐突上——是不是？……”
“知道什么？”南希毫不留情地追。
“喏——知道我没有意思。”
“傻孩子，你对她懂得多么少啊！”
“您是说，她猜透旁人私心思想的本事异乎寻常？”
“不——傻瓜！只是说：她是个女人！”
两人在榆树底下散步，停下来看几个小男孩在小湖上放玩具船——湖面浮着点点睡莲叶。寻着一条空着的长凳坐下。两人不约而同，把话头又接回哈德森医生那桩怪癖：操心着不知多少人的私事烦恼，还偏用一堵密不透风的高墙，把这一干古怪交易团团围住。
---
“你不妨把它当事实记下，”南希正说着，语气斩钉截铁，“韦恩·哈德森在这些案子上的那种古怪投法——投资巨大，从不指望也从不接受分文偿还——绝非一时兴起。他不是个兴之所至的人。他不是怪人。我从不曾见他没有充分动机地做过一件事。谁也不能说他挥霍钱财或者不通生意。杀价他能杀得精明。他知道何时买进、何时卖出。多少生意经比他老到的人，都要来请教他对地产行情的见解，听他对工业股的判断。我坚信，他用这种偷偷摸摸的方式替某些人做这些怪事，是有明确动机的。他的事业成功，以某种我不敢说懂的方式，牵在其中。等你查出那动机是什么，你就会明白韦恩·哈德森何以是个了不起的外科医生！”
“除了讲过的，你还知道些什么？”他端详着她，目光探问。
“有一本小书——像一本日记——我认为你有权知道。他锁在办公室的保险柜里，同贵重文件放在一起；有些关乎医务，有些关乎私人财务。我接手布莱特伍德时，那本书就在。有一回——我们找保险单来着——我问哈德森医生，那本小书是不是医院的事……”
“您看不出来？”博比插嘴。
“它不是用英文写的，也不是我见过的任何一种文字。”
“它像什么——西班牙文、德文、希腊文？”
她摇头，接着讲。
“我问过他那小书是什么。我清清楚楚记得，他一下子变得何等郑重，站着好几分钟，用指尖揉着太阳穴——他琢磨重大决断时的老习惯——隔了好久才说：‘不过是一份私人记事。’然后他又笑着添了一句：‘你要是能读懂，尽管读去。’”
“您试过吗？”
“还用问？”她应道，“几个钟头几个钟头地——就在最近。”
“读出什么了？”
“读出个头疼！”
“真想亲眼看看！”
“我拿给你看！谁的资格都越不过你。我跟哈德逊太太说，医生的许多贵重文件还在医院保险柜里；她坚持由我们保管着，等她哪天有精神同我一起清理；所以那本书还在。”
“那咱们这就回去。”他按捺不住地说。
他们回到布莱特伍德时，天已擦黑。南希从保险柜里取出那本书，摊在他面前的桌上。他坐下，把它捧在手里——一本素净的、黑色皮面日记，八乘五英寸见方，一英寸多厚。扉页上，以哈德森医生极有特点的笔迹，写着孤零零的一句话。

===Original===

> To whom this may concern

===Chinese===

> 致关心此书的人

===Original===

“Nobody could be any more concerned than I am!” He glanced up at Nancy for approval. She nodded.
“Now turn over to the next page, and see what you make of that!”
Bobby stared long and hard.
“It’s in code!”
“You will find it difficult,” said Nancy. “The way I have it figured is this: Doctor Hudson purposed not to have the matters which he kept secret divulged during his lifetime. The fact that so many of these odd wards of his are bobbing up now, ready to tell their stories of his strange dealings with them, convinces me that they were all virtually sworn to secrecy while he lived. Now that he is gone, they tell. I think the mystery is all contained in this book. Whoever reads it, knows the real story. Perhaps the doctor was willing it should be known after he was done with it, but made it inaccessible to anyone who might chance upon it while he lived.”
“He’s made it inaccessible enough﻿—I’ll say!” Bobby growled. “Did you ever see the like?”
“This first page,” explained Nancy, “is unquestionably a sort of preface. You notice that all the other pages are completely filled. This one has only ten lines. It must be a foreword; an explanation; dedication, perhaps﻿ ﻿… You take it along with you﻿ ﻿… Know any Greek?”
Bobby shook his head.
“Oh, I know the alphabet,” he qualified, smiling.
“That’s enough﻿ ﻿… What’s the last letter?”
“Omega,” recited Bobby glibly.
“And omega is a sort of stop-signal, isn’t it? A sign for the end of something?”
He nodded.
“How many letters are there in the Greek alphabet?”
Bobby closed his eyes and counted on his fingers.
“Twenty-four.”
“*Omega* being the twenty-fourth﻿—and signifying the end!”
“Right!”
“Now, what is the twelfth letter?”
“*Mu*,” answered Bobby, after another calculation.
“Well, if *omega* means ‘finished,’ what do you suppose *mu* means?”
“ ‘Half finished’﻿—I suppose.”
Bobby soberly returned to the preface of the little book and found, at almost regular intervals, the letters *μ* (*mu*) and *ω* (*omega*).
“Is that a clue?”
“I think so,” said Nancy, “but it did me no good. I just offer it to you for what it seems to be worth.”
---
Not at any given moment of his long drive home did Bobby Merrick realize exactly where he was, as he swiftly covered the familiar road to Windymere. At midnight, he put up his car; went to his room; sat down before his desk, with Doctor Hudson’s private journal, a pencil, and a thick pad of paper; and at dawn was still experimenting without a glimmer of encouragement.
Meggs, opening the door to call him to breakfast, found him sitting fully dressed, with his head on his arms, asleep, and tiptoed downstairs, his eyes shining.
A few minutes later he whispered to the cook, in a tone of victory, “You lost your bet!”
“Drunk again?”
“Quite!”

===Chinese===

“再没有谁比我更关心了！”他抬眼望南希，求个认可。她点了点头。
“再翻到下一页，看你读得出什么名堂！”
博比盯了半天，盯得很苦。
“是密码！”
“你会觉得难的。”南希说，“我琢磨出来的门道是这样的：哈德森医生存心不让他秘而不宣的这些事，在他在世之日泄露出去。如今他那些古怪的被保护人纷纷冒头，都准备讲出他同他们那桩桩件件奇怪的往来——这说明他们当年个个都等于发过守口如瓶的誓。如今人去了，他们才开口。我想，全部的秘密都收在这本书里。谁读懂了它，谁就知道真情。兴许医生也乐见它在他身后大白于天下，只是生前谁碰巧翻着，也休想读得进去。”
“他把门槛抬得也够高了——我说！”博比咆哮，“您见过这路数吗？”
“这头一页，”南希解释，“毫无疑问是篇序。你注意到没有，其余的页页写得满满当当。独这页只有十行。想必是前言；是说明；兴许还是献辞……你带回去琢磨……希腊文你懂吗？”
博比摇头。
“哦，字母表我还认得。”他笑着补了一句。
“那就够用……最后一个字母是什么？”
“欧米伽。”博比脱口而出。
“欧米伽是个停车信号，是不是？一样东西到了头的记号？”
他点头。
“希腊字母表共多少个字母？”
博比闭上眼，扳着指头数。
“二十四个。”
“欧米伽是第二十四个——表示终结！”
“对！”
“那么，第十二个是什么？”
“穆（mu）。”又算了一遍，博比答道。
“好，欧米伽若表示‘完结’，你猜穆表示什么？”
“‘半完结’——我猜。”
博比郑重地回到小书的序页，发现字母 μ（穆）和 ω（欧米伽）以近乎规整的间隔布满其上。
“这算条线索？”
“我想是，”南希说，“可它帮不了我。值多少，你自己掂量。”
---
在长长的归途上，博比·梅里克没有哪一刻清楚自己身在何处；他风驰电掣，覆着那条回温迪米尔的熟路。半夜，他停好车；回房；在书桌前坐下，面前摆着哈德森医生的私人日记、一支铅笔和一沓厚厚的白纸；到破晓时分，仍在摸索，毫无一丝光亮。
梅格斯推门请他早饭，见他衣着齐整地坐着，头枕着胳膊睡着了，便蹑手蹑脚退下楼去，两眼放光。
几分钟以后，他压着嗓子对厨娘说，一派得胜的腔调：“你的赌输啦！”
“又喝醉了？”
“可不！”

## VI / 六
===Original===

The glowing ellipse cast by the heavily shaded lamp at the head of her bed shone upon the weapons with which young Mrs. Hudson had armed herself against drowsiness when, at midnight, she had retired with a novel, two magazines, and a leather writing case embarrassingly stuffed with unanswered letters, resolved to stay awake until Joyce returned.
Shortly before two, she had lost the battle and now slept with the light shining into her troubled face. Not that the trifling incident of dropping off could have worried her; but it was all of a piece with the general failure.
Had the late Doctor Hudson been subpoenaed to the witness-box on the day of his death to report on the success of the marriage he had contracted on behalf of his daughter, he, being unusually fastidious about truth-telling, might have found it an awkward question.
The three of them had stood up, that crisp January morning at St. Andrew’s, in the presence of Uncle Percival and Monty﻿—and the Senator Byrnes, who had run over from Washington﻿—not so much as a bride and groom, attended by a grown daughter, cordially consenting; rather as a pair of strikingly lovely young women, attended by an elderly, distinguished guardian, invoking Church and State to legalize and bless their comradeship and give them a deed to it.
That it was candidly a mariage de convenance (the French for it was more euphonious than the English equivalent) caused Doctor Hudson no serious misgivings. He nursed no illusions on that subject, aware that if all the world’s weddings were limited to romantic attachments in which no material advantages were at issue on either side, the human race would long since have been exterminated; and that if it were unethical for a young woman to marry with the knowledge that she could not immediately give herself with unqualified devotion to her husband, the Holy Virgin herself was at fault.
Immediately upon the consummation of his wedding, however, Doctor Hudson had found himself falling sincerely in love with his girl-bride, and the comradeship he had sought to insure between Helen and Joyce was put in jeopardy.
Joyce was a poor sailor, and the first three days were rough.
“No, darling; not a thing﻿ ﻿… Please run along. I’d much rather you did.”
So when, on Sunday noon, she had been persuaded to come down to the blanketed chair they had prepared for her on B-Deck, she realized that she was being solicitously attended, on either side, not by an indulgent father and a devoted college chum, but by a man and his wife﻿—good friends of hers, unquestionably, but, well﻿—there you were!
All three of them tried to twist the contemplated relationship back into focus, but it wouldn’t go. Perhaps their very efforts to do so made it impossible. Hudson, in Paris, had enthusiastically encouraged Joyce in her shopping extravagances; and Helen, eager to join in her husband’s approval, may have overdone it.
“What an exquisite coat! So glad you found it, Joyce! How very becoming!”
It was the right attitude without a doubt, but it hadn’t the right inflection. It wasn’t as one girl to another, but as an ingratiating stepmother overanxious to be generous, affectionate.
There were no disagreements. Perhaps it might have cleared the air if there had been. Nor was there any constraint on the surface. It was too deep to come to the surface. That was the difficulty. Everything they said to each other was amplified﻿—as through a loud speaker﻿—intensifying their mutual assurances of devotion until it took on a tone of unreality. Each knew the other was trying hard to be natural. Each knew the other was playing a difficult part. Almost desperately, they scrambled for the old position; but they had lost the way to it.
Shortly after the death of Doctor Hudson, the distance between them increased markedly. For the first few days, they had clung to each other with a rededicated bond that promised to be, if not a restoration of their earlier comradeship, at least an earnest of future indispensability﻿ ﻿… possibly to become a more valuable relation than any they had heretofore sustained. But it was not for long.
Joyce’s grief, inconsolable for a week, quickly exhausted itself. It was only common honesty in her when she declared one day that she wasn’t going to sit moping any longer, for, volatile as she was, any protracted mourning would have been mere affectation, even if she were capable of it, which was doubtful.
Presently there came a disquieting furtiveness in her explanation of late hours. Helen’s gently tactful queries, implying an uneasiness about her social programme, were either dismissed lightly with a chaffing reply, or met with a brief﻿—albeit friendly﻿—hint that one was quite old enough now to know where one wanted to go, and with whom, and until when. She was not to trouble her pretty head about her flighty Joyce. She was to live her own life and stop worrying about trifles.
“Don’t sit up, darling!” Joyce would protest, at nine.
“I’m going out with Ned.” (Or Tom, or Pat, or Phil.) “We may be late, you know. Where? Oh﻿—I’m not sure﻿ ﻿… to dance, some place, I suppose﻿ ﻿… and a bite of supper, later﻿ ﻿… Crystal Palace, maybe﻿ ﻿… Gordon’s, perhaps.”
“I don’t like your going out to Gordon’s, Joyce. Really﻿—that’s not a nice place. Tell me you won’t﻿ ﻿… please!”
“Oh, very well! Only one can’t do all the deciding. One’s escort has something to say about that too.”
---
On the rose counterpane lay several letters; one of them from Montgomery Brent, brief, brotherly, suggesting that his counsel would be at his “little sister’s” disposal should she be troubled about business matters, now that her responsibilities were increased.
“Only too glad, you know, to straighten out the kinks for you when it comes to income tax, investments, and the safeguarding of your estate. That sort of thing is my daily work.”
“The dear boy!” she murmured half aloud, as she reread it, “How decent of him; and not a bit bad idea, either. I wonder does he really know anything about business. He ought to by this time.”
Montgomery, whom it pleased her to think of as her brother Monty (never more affectionately than now that she felt so desolated), was five years her senior. College not agreeing with him after his sophomore year, Monty had brought his saxophone to the jazz-market. Not quite satisfied to make a permanent profession of bouncing and writhing and puffing himself purple every night from eight until two in the front row of a dance orchestra, he had made some friends among the chalky-fingered youngsters who posted the board at a down town brokerage office. In a few months he had a desk and a small salaried position in the organization.
“I’m a broker!” he would reply maturely, when some young thing coyly inquired, while they danced, what college he attended.
Doubtless he was doing well enough now. He had not borrowed from Helen for more than a year; had given her an expensive silver vase for a wedding present, appearing on that occasion in a morning coat, striped trousers, and spats, the only man present who had given no quarter to the request for informal dress﻿ ﻿… It wasn’t a half-bad idea. There was one friend left, anyhow﻿—good old Monty!
Another letter was a brief note, written to Joyce, by the young Merrick person, dated from Ann Arbor; evidently composed with much care. It left many things unsaid which its author assumed would be read into it. He had entered the Medical School, hopeful of becoming of some use, eventually, to the profession which her father had so conspicuously adorned.
“I never would have picked him for the part,” Joyce had interpolated, as she read that much of the letter to Helen. “Rather quixotic, don’t you think?”
“Did it on impulse, likely,” Helen had remarked.
“Rather fine of him, though﻿—at that! Wouldn’t you say?” championed Joyce.
“I’ll tell you a year from now,” Helen had replied, half-audibly.
Joyce had continued reading to herself. With a gesture of impatience, upon finishing the letter, she tossed it across the table, and savagely dug into her grapefruit.
“I think you had better answer it, Helen. The only human, personal line in it is for you. Write and tell him you hope he gets on well with his teachers, and makes A in everything,” she snapped, with frank asperity.
The only human, personal line read, “Please convey my sympathy and regards to Mrs. Hudson, whom I hope I may soon have a chance to know.”
Helen had made no further comment. Her opinion of the Merrick person was based solely upon Joyce’s disquieting references to him﻿—a spoiled youngster with loose habits and too much money. It was clear enough that the girl entertained an infatuation for him which he had made no effort to capitalize. Helen was glad of it. She had no wish to meet him﻿—had dreaded the day when he might turn up. It was enough that he had been the cause of her husband’s tragedy﻿ ﻿… Not his fault, of course; but she hoped he would not make it necessary for her to try to be nice to him. It was a closed incident. She was relieved.
The letter had remained in her pile of morning mail, beside her plate. Later, it had been tucked into her portfolio. She had read it again, in bed. Well, it was no concern of hers, whether he made good or not. At least, he was out of Joyce’s calculations﻿ ﻿… One questionable friend less to worry about.
---
Helen was awakened with a start, as the door of her room was cautiously opened far enough to admit the flushed face of Joyce.
“You Joyce?”
The door was closing, slowly, cautiously.
“Sorry, darling﻿ ﻿… Good night!” came in muffled tones from beyond the door.
“Come in, dear!” called Helen.
There was a considerable delay before the door opened again. Joyce came in, jerkily, all but asleep on her feet, dazedly rubbing her forehead with the back of the hand in which she clutched her crumpled hat; the other groping, uncertainly, for something to support her. She leaned heavily against the foot of the bed, swaying dizzily.
“Oh!﻿—but, my dear!” cried Helen, in consternation, propping herself up suddenly on her elbows. “What in the world﻿—! Where have you been?”
“Gordon’s!”
Joyce ground out the guttural between tensely locked jaws, and smiled fatuously.
Helen sat up and peered incredulously, silently, at her dishevelled stepdaughter, who grew restive under the inspection.
“Been﻿—been writin’ letters?” Joyce surveyed the litter on the bed with a pitiable attempt at casualness.
Helen nodded briefly, and pressed both hands tightly against her forehead in a gesture of despair, which Joyce decided to ignore.
“And if there isn’t ickle Bobby?” She leaned far forward over the foot of the bed and with elaborate precision flicked the letter contemptuously with her finger. “That!﻿—for you!﻿—Doctor Merrick!”
“Why﻿—Joyce Hudson!﻿—you’re drunk!”
Helen stepped out into her slippers, caught up a kimono, and put her arm about the girl’s sagging shoulders.
“Who? Me?” giggled Joyce amiably. “Me﻿—drunk? You should shee Tommy!”
“Let me help you to bed,” pleaded Helen brokenly. “No﻿—here! You may sleep in my room.”
Joyce fumbled helplessly at her buttons with one hand, the other clumsily mopping her dripping brow with her little hat. Presently she crumpled on the bed, and Helen tugged off her dress and laid a cold towel across her eyes.
“Thanks, darling!” mumbled Joyce, between heavy sighs. “Much bother﻿ ﻿… too bad﻿ ﻿… all my fault﻿ ﻿… Don’t blame Tommy. Tommy nice boy! Goin’﻿—goin’ to marry Tommy﻿ ﻿… Well﻿—can’t you congrat﻿—can’t you felic﻿—I’m afraid I can’t say it very distinc’ly﻿—but aren’t you glad﻿—about Tommy and me?”
“Let’s wait and talk it over in the morning, dear,” soothed Helen, turning the towel and patting it about the flushed temples.
“No﻿—sir!” babbled Joyce, with an expansive gesture, “We’re goin’ talk about it﻿—” she brought a slim, lovely hand down with a clumsy slap on the pillow﻿—“ri’ now!” The towel was brushed aside, and she gazed up militantly with swollen eyes. “Tha’s just like you! You cry! I come home﻿—all happy﻿—to announce my engagesh to Tommy﻿—and you cry! What’s the big idea? Do you want him?”
Bridling her impatience, Helen urged the drunken girl to leave off and go to sleep. Maudlin tears of self-pity bathed Joyce’s face.
“Nobody loves me!” she wailed. “Nobody but good old Tommy!﻿ ﻿… But I won’t marry him!﻿ ﻿… I won’t!”
Presently she relaxed, licked her stiff lips, sighed deeply, and slept. Helen knelt by the bed, her face buried in the covers to escape the heavy fumes, and wept piteously. Wayne Hudson had left her with a responsibility quite too serious to be met. Only one thing had he expected of her. She had failed him.
---
At length, rousing stiffly from the cramped position in which she had fallen asleep through sheer fatigue and nervous exhaustion, she mechanically collected the scattered letters from the floor, turned out the light, and went dejectedly to Joyce’s bedroom. She put down the letters on the little vanity table and bathed her face with cologne.
Bobby Merrick’s rather stilted note lay open before her as if inviting attention. Between the lines it announced that he considered himself under a moral obligation to Doctor Hudson﻿ ﻿… She had been disposed to waive that implication aside as a mere pose﻿ ﻿… a bit of ephemeral martyrdom to be toyed with until he tired of it﻿ ﻿… a pretence of gallantry. She averted her eyes from the letter, perplexed, accused by it. Was she too under a certain moral obligation to Doctor Hudson? Young Merrick was trying to discharge his! How about hers?
She carefully folded the letter, and stood for a long time preoccupiedly deepening its creases with nervous fingers. It occurred to her that she would like to have a long, confidential talk with Bobby Merrick. Perhaps he might have something to suggest﻿ ﻿… She stared at her haggard reflection in the mirror and shook her head. No﻿—the way out did not lie in that direction. She tucked the letter into her writing-case, and tumbled wearily into bed.

===Chinese===

她床头那盏罩得严严实实的灯投下一圈暖光，照在年轻的哈德森太太午夜就寝时为自己备下的抗瞌睡的家什上：一本小说、两本杂志，还有一只皮面文具盒，盒里塞满未回的信，塞得叫人难为情——她打定主意要熬到乔伊斯回来。
两点差一刻，她败下阵来；此刻睡着了，灯光正照着她眉头不展的脸。倒不是打盹这桩小事本身叫她烦心；而是这与全线溃败一脉相承。
倘若已故的哈德森医生在亡故当天被传上证人席，要他汇报那桩代女儿缔结的婚姻成败如何——他这个对说真话格外讲究的人，怕会觉出这问题棘手。
那个一月清冽的早晨，在圣安德鲁教堂，他们三个并排起立；珀西瓦尔伯伯和蒙蒂在场——还有专程从华盛顿赶来的伯恩参议员——与其说是新郎新娘由一位成年的女儿热诚见证，倒不如说是两位明艳照人的少妇，由一位年高德劭的监护人陪同，吁请教会与国家为她们的情谊作证加冕，发给一张契据。
这桩婚事明明白白是一门权宜婚姻（法语的说法比英语的对等词悦耳），哈德森医生对此倒无甚不安。他在这一点上不抱幻想：他明白，倘若天下所有的婚礼都限定于双方均无物质实惠可言的浪漫结合，人类早绝种了；他也明白，倘若一位姑娘明知自己一时拿不出毫无保留的挚爱还要出嫁就算不道德，那么连圣母马利亚也有过失了。
然而婚礼一成，哈德森医生便发觉自己当真心爱上了他的新娘子；而他想在海伦与乔伊斯之间确立的那份情谊，反倒悬了。
乔伊斯晕船，头三天浪又大。
“不用，亲爱的；什么都不要……你去吧。你去我反而安心。”
于是，星期天中午，她好歹被劝下到 B 甲板上他们替她铺好的圈椅里时，她醒悟到：左右殷勤伺候她的，已不是一个惯她的父亲加一个贴心的大学姐妹，而是一位先生和他的太太——毫无疑问也是她的好朋友，可是，唉——就是这么回事了！
他们三个都想把那层预设的关系扭回原先的焦点，可它怎么也回不去。也许正是这份强扭，坏了事。在巴黎，哈德森起劲地纵容乔伊斯挥霍购物；海伦急于附和丈夫的赞许，兴许又过了头。
“多精致的大衣！真高兴你淘到了它，乔伊斯！你穿着多合衬！”
态度无疑是对的，可惜腔调不对。不是姐妹间的口吻，倒像一位急于显得宽厚亲热的继母。
他们之间没有龃龉。若有，倒兴许能把空气抖开。表面上也毫无拘束。症结太深，浮不到表面上。难就难在这里。他们彼此说的每一句话都被放大了——像过了扩音喇叭——把相互的亲爱保证放大再放大，直到带上一种不真实的调子。各自都晓得对方在拼命做得自然。各自都晓得对方在演一折难戏。他们几乎是拼了命去摸原先的位置；可回去的路已经丢了。
哈德森医生死后不久，两人之间的距离显著拉开了。头几天，她们彼此偎依，重续的那条纽带倘若算不得旧谊的复原，至少也是往后相依为命的凭据……兴许会成为比她们以往任何一种关系更可贵的关系。可惜没维持多久。
乔伊斯的悲恸，不可慰藉了一个礼拜，很快自行耗尽。有一天她宣布再不闷坐着发愁了，这在她只是本分的老实话：她天性多变，拖长的哀悼纵然装得出来——还未必装得出来——也不过是做戏。
不久，她解释晚归时添了一种叫人心神不安的遮掩。海伦婉转的探问——话里带着对她社交安排的不安——要么被一句玩笑轻轻岔开，要么换来一句简短的、虽然友好的提示：自己如今岁数不小了，想去哪儿、想跟谁、想待到几点，自己做得了主。她那轻浮的乔伊斯，就不劳她的俏脑袋费心了。她自己过自己的日子、少为鸡毛蒜皮操心，这还用说。
“别等我，亲爱的！”九点来钟，乔伊斯总要这样说。
“我跟内德出去。”（或者汤姆，或者帕特，或者菲尔）“兴许晚呢，你知道。去哪儿？哦——说不准……跳舞吧，反正找个地方……回头再吃点夜宵……水晶宫，没准儿……戈登家，也兴许。”
“我不爱你去戈登家，乔伊斯。说真的——那不是个正经地方。答应我不去……求你！”
“哦，行了吧！总不能事事都由着我呀。人家陪着的先生，在这上头也有发言权呢。”
---
玫瑰色的床罩上摊着几封信；其中一封是蒙哥马利·布伦特写来的，短短几行，一副兄长口吻，大意说：如今她肩上的担子重了，生意上若有烦难，他这个做哥哥的随时效劳。
“乐意效劳，你知道，替你把所得税、投资、产业保全这些疙疙瘩瘩的都捋顺了。这一行是我的日常功课。”
“这孩子真好！”她重读那封信时半出声地喃喃道，“多仗义；这主意也不赖。就是不知他到底懂不懂生意。到这年头，总该懂了。”
蒙哥马利——她乐意把他想成哥哥蒙蒂（从没像如今这样亲热过，如今她正觉着凄惶）——比她大五岁。大学跟他不合，读到二年级他便抱着萨克斯管下了爵士乐的集市。每晚八点到凌晨两点，在舞池乐队前排蹦高、扭摆、把自己吹成猪肝色，他不愿拿这个当终身职业；于是结交了几个手指粉白的年轻人——他们在市中心一家经纪行里管写行情板。不出几个月，他在那机构里谋得一张桌子和一份小薪水的差事。
“我是经纪人！”两人共舞时，有哪个小妞儿娇滴滴问他念的哪所大学，他便这样老成作答。
如今想必混得不坏。他已一年多没向海伦伸过手；结婚时还送了她一只贵重的银花瓶；那天的场合他穿晨礼服、条纹裤、鞋罩，满场男士中，唯独他对“服装随意”的请求寸步不让……这主意不坏。总算还剩下一个朋友——好样的老蒙蒂！
另一封是那个梅里克家小子写给乔伊斯的短信，落款安娜堡；显然字斟句酌过。许多话他没写出来，只当读的人自然读得出。他已进了医学院，指望有朝一日，能对岳丈大人光辉装点过的那门职业，略尽绵薄。
“打死我也想不到他挑这角色，”乔伊斯把信读到这儿，插嘴对海伦说，“挺堂吉诃德的，你说呢？”
“多半是一时冲动。”海伦评了一句。
“不过，就冲这个，倒也够意思！你说是不是？”乔伊斯护上了。
“到明年的今天，我告诉你。”海伦半出声地答。
乔伊斯自顾自读完了。信一完，她不耐烦地一挥手，把它撂过桌面，狠狠戳向她的西柚。
“我看你还是回他一封的好，海伦。全信里唯一一句人味儿的话，是给你的。写信告诉他，盼他跟老师们处得好，门门功课都得甲。”她尖利利地抢白。
那唯一一句人味儿的话是：“请代我向哈德森太太致以慰问与敬意；我盼着不久能有机会认识她。”
海伦没再说什么。她对那位梅里克小子的看法，全凭乔伊斯那些叫人心惊的念叨——一个惯坏了的、手脚不干净又有的是钱的少爷。明摆着，这姑娘对他一片痴心，而他压根没想领受。海伦正求之不得。她不愿见他——一直怕着哪天他冒出来。他既然是她丈夫那场惨祸的因由，这就够了……当然不是他的过错；可她盼着别弄到非要她对他客气的那一步。这段公案已经了结。她乐得轻省。
那封信留在早邮件那摞里，就在她的盘子旁边。后来，被收进了她的文件夹。她在床上又读了一遍。呢，他成不成器，与她无干。至少，他从乔伊斯的盘算里出局了……少操一份可疑朋友的心。
---
海伦惊醒过来：房门被小心翼翼地推开一条缝，刚够探进乔伊斯那张通红的脸。
“是你吗，乔伊斯？”
门正缓缓地、小心地合上。
“对不起，亲爱的……晚安！”门那边传来含混的声音。
“进来，亲爱的！”海伦唤道。
隔了好一会儿，门才又开。乔伊斯踉跄进来，站着都快睡着了，一只手攥着揉皱的帽子、用手背发怔地揉着额头；另一只手没着没落地乱摸，想找个依靠。她重重倚上床脚，晕晕地晃。
“哎呀！——我的老天！”海伦慌得一声喊，猛地用胳膊肘撑起身，“究竟——！你上哪儿去了？”
“戈登家！”
乔伊斯从咬紧的牙关里磨出这声喉音，傻乎乎地笑了。
海伦坐直了，无言地、难以置信地端详着蓬头散发的继女；继女被看得老大不自在。
“写——写信来着？”乔伊斯扫了一眼床上摊的一片，可怜巴巴地想装出随意的样子。
海伦简短地点了点头，双手紧紧抵住前额，一副绝望的姿势；乔伊斯决定装没看见。
“这不是小博比的信嘛！”她朝床脚俯下身去，手指头煞有介事地、轻蔑地把那封信一弹。“这封！——给你的！——梅里克大夫！”
“哎呀——乔伊斯·哈德森！——你喝醉了！”
海伦趿下床穿进拖鞋，抓过一件晨衣，一把揽住姑娘瘫软的肩头。
“谁？我？”乔伊斯和和气气地咯咯笑，“我——醉了？你该瞧瞧汤米！”
“让我扶你上床。”海伦哽着声求，“不——就在这儿！你在我屋里睡吧。”
乔伊斯一只手笨拙地摸着纽扣，另一只手拿她那顶小帽子胡乱擦着淋漓的额头。不多时她整个人瘫倒在床上，海伦拽下她的裙子，把一条冷毛巾敷上她的眼睛。
“多谢，亲爱的！”乔伊斯在沉沉的叹息之间咕哝，“太麻烦你了……真不好……全怪我……别怪汤米。汤米是好孩子！要——要嫁给汤米了……呢——你就不恭喜——你就不贺喜——我怕我说不利索——可你不高兴吗——为汤米和我？”
“等到明儿早上再说，好吗，亲爱的。”海伦温声哄着，翻过毛巾，轻轻拍着她滚烫的太阳穴。
“不——行！”乔伊斯手一挥，嚷嚷道，“咱们这就说——”她把一只纤细好看的手笨拙地一巴掌拍在枕头上——“现在就说！”毛巾被挥开，她肿着眼睛，气势汹汹地瞪上来。“你就这德行！你哭！我回家来——欢天喜地——来宣布我跟汤米订婚——你倒哭！搞什么名堂？你想要他？”
海伦按住性子，劝这醉姑娘别闹了、睡吧。乔伊斯满脸淌下感伤的自怜之泪。
“没人疼我！”她哀哀地哭，“除了好老汤米没人疼我！……可我偏不嫁他！……偏不！”
不多时她松软下来，舔了舔僵硬的嘴唇，长叹一声，睡着了。海伦跪在床边，把脸埋进被褥，躲开那沉沉的酒气，哭得可怜。韦恩·哈德森留给她的这副担子，重得她挑不动。他对她只指望一件事。她辜负了他。
---
末了，她从那个蜷缩的睡姿里僵硬地醒转——她实在是累到神经枯竭才睡过去的——机械地从地板上拾起散落的信，关了灯，蔫头耷脑地去乔伊斯的卧室。她把信撂在小梳妆台上，拿古龙水擦了擦脸。
博比·梅里克那封略显拘谨的短信摊在她面前，像在讨个注目。字里行间透露着：他认定自己对哈德森医生负着一笔道义的债……她本想把这层意思挥到一边去，只当是摆姿态……一时的殉道瘾，玩腻了自然丢开……一场骑士风的做戏。她把眼光从信上挪开，心乱，又被它声讨着。她自己对哈德森医生，难道不负着几分道义吗？小梅里克正想法子清偿他那份！她那份呢？
她仔细把信折好，久久站着，用不安的手指一遍遍加深那道折痕。她忽然起念，想同博比·梅里克来一场长长的、交心的谈话。兴许他能出点什么主意……她盯着镜中自己憔悴的影子，摇头。不——出路不在那个方向。她把信塞进文具盒，疲惫地一头栽进床里。

## VII / 七
===Original===

At one o’clock on the morning of Thanksgiving Day, young Merrick solved the riddle to which he had devoted much of his spare time for nearly two months.
After only a week’s sporadic work on it, he had written to Nancy Ashford:
“When the light breaks on this it will not come like a valley dawn. The book is going to be pitch dark up to a certain moment, and after that it will be clear and bright as a June morning. There’s no halfway business about a job like this. Either you can read it all, with ease and understanding; or you can’t read a syllable!”
The Thanksgiving recess having begun at noon on Wednesday, Bobby determined to spend the brief vacation on the cryptic journal. It would be a good time. Distractions and interruptions would be reduced to a minimum, for no sooner had the noon whistles blown than the student quarter was as deserted as if warned of an impending epidemic of the Black Plague. He had made a tentative promise to have evening dinner on Thanksgiving Day with old Nicholas “provided I finish a problem it is important to complete at once.”
All Wednesday afternoon, he turned from one futile experiment to another, most of them being variants of schemes already tried. It annoyed him that he seemed unable to bring his attention to a sharp focus. Now he would sit at his desk for an hour, sharpening pencils and gazing glassily at the mocking script. Now he would fling himself across his bed and, supporting himself on an aching elbow, set down columns of letters in every manner of eccentric sequence. Oblivious of the passing time, his own discomfort, his need of exercise, he continued to lay out diagrams and tear up paper. Night came down and he turned on the lights. Midnight found him very weary, his brain operating mechanically, sluggishly. He tapped his teeth with his pencil, and went woolgathering in spite of himself. He even made a brief mental excursion on foot along the highway to the north of Windymere and assisted a motorist in distress. Recovering himself, impatiently, he pursued his endless diagrams.
Then a fresh idea occurred to him. Here was something he had not yet tried. He wrote the first few words of the unintelligible script in a running line, utterly disregarding the spaces between them. (It had long been his practice to reproduce the letters in capitals, thinking this might help to simplify the words.)
Now he broke in two what was obviously the first sentence, at the point where the Greek letter “μ” indicated a half-stop, and set down the remainder of that obvious division immediately below it:
R
A
E
I
O
S
D
R
O
M
F
I
N
E
D
R
C
N

E

Y
R
E
D
Moved by sheer caprice, he wrote the lines again, the second division lacking one space of meeting the left margin:
R
A
E
I
O

R
O

R

D
For full five minutes he stared at this combination until the lines blurred and blended. Of a sudden, his heart speeded up. His pencil shook as he rapidly rewrote the letters, mortising the lines:

===Chinese===

感恩节凌晨一点钟，小梅里克破开了那则谜；近两个月来，他把大半的闲工夫都押在了它上头。
断断续续只摸了一个礼拜，他就写信给南希·阿什福德：“天光在这本书上破晓时，不会像山谷里的黎明。它将一路漆黑，直到某一刻；过了那一刻，它便清亮如六月的早晨。这种事没有半吊子的名堂。要么你能通读全篇，轻松自如，心领神会；要么你连一个音节也读不出！”
星期三中午，感恩节假期开始了；博比打定主意，拿这短短的假全耗在那本密码日记上。时机正好。分心和打扪将减到最低，因为午班的汽笛一响，学生区便空得像得了黑死病瘟警。他对老尼古拉斯有个口头的约：感恩节晚上去他那儿吃饭——“只要我赶完一道必须立刻赶完的难题”。
星期三整整一下午，他从一桩徒劳的试验转向另一桩，多半是旧方案的花样翻新。叫他恼火的是，注意力怎么也聚不成焦点。一时，他坐在桌前削铅笔，眼神发直地瞪着那行揶揄人的怪字；一时，他横倒在床上，撑着一条酸痛的胳膊肘，以种种古怪的次序一列一列写字母。忘了时辰，忘了自己身上的不适，忘了该活动活动，他只管画图样、擑纸头。夜幕落下来，他开了灯。午夜时分，他已疲惫不堪，脑子机械地、滞涩地转。他拿铅笔敲着牙，不由自主地走起神来。他甚至神游了一小遭：徒步沿温迪米尔以北的公路，去救助一位抛锚的驾车人。他回过神来，不耐烦地继续他那些画不完的图样。
忽然，一个新念头闪出来。这一路他还没试过。他把那行读不通的字开头几个词连成一串写下，全然不管词与词的空隙。（他早已习惯把字母一律录成大写，以为这样兴许能把词化简。）
如今他把显然是第一句的那一串，在希腊字母“μ”标出半停的地方断成两截，把后半段紧接着写在下面：
R
A
