First honours now carry a prize offered by Mr. Owen Simmons (Simmons Turbine Co.)﻿—a year in Vienna, all expenses, scholarship, liberal allowance, etc﻿ ﻿… The Dawsons were here last Thursday﻿—the day your letter came. She was going to New York to see her husband off.
“Actually, the three of them were like brothers and sister. I couldn’t help being jealous. They were closeted here in my office for a whole hour, and when they came out, and I met them in the hall, Mrs. Dawson was all excited. She seemed so happy that I said, ‘You look like Christmas morning!’ ‘Why shouldn’t I?’ she replied, laughing. ‘I’m going along!’﻿ ﻿… ’To Europe?’﻿ ﻿… ’Absolutely!’
“They have a dear little boy﻿—going on four. I never saw a child so beautifully trained. Doctor Merrick went along with them to New York and brought little Jack back with him to stay at Windymere until Mrs. Dawson returns. I dare say old Mr. Merrick can be found, at this minute, leading the pony he has bought for him.”﻿ ﻿…
---
There was a letter from Joyce:
“No, darling, I’m sure you will take no pleasure in thinking or saying ‘I told you so.’ For that wouldn’t be you﻿ ﻿… Never a day passes that I do not scourge myself for the abominable way I used you that winter when you were trying so hard to step in between me and myself.
“I wasn’t fair to anybody through those days. Not even to poor Bobby. I never told you the straight of that. I meant to; but, you see, I telephoned to him the next morning and asked him to come and see me. I’d a hazy notion that he was﻿—well﻿—kind to me the night before, and I thought he might renew our friendship. He made some excuse. I was hurt and humiliated. I knew something had happened to cause an estrangement between you and Bobby, for﻿—can you forgive me, darling?﻿—I noticed a letter you had from him shortly afterward, and peeked into it. He wanted to be forgiven for causing you a lot of suffering. I knew that meant he hadn’t told you all the truth. You thought he was in that souse party at Gordon’s, and I just let you think so﻿—after he refused to see me again. The honest fact was that he went there sober and fought everybody in the place to get me out when I was so blind drunk I didn’t really learn what it was all about for three days. I know Bobby means nothing to you and that you despise him, but it’s only fair to him that you should know how he happened to bring me home, that Thanksgiving night.
“No, darling, it’s not any better. It’s never going to be any better. I know that now. Tommy can’t help it. One night he comes home drunk, surly and obstinate; the next night drunk, silly and sentimental; the next night drunk, argumentative and critical; the next night drunk, savage and abusive﻿—but always drunk. I can definitely count on that! I never know what mood he will be in﻿—whether I am to be upbraided for imagined indifference to him and his work (little enough he does of it!) or pestered with pretences of an affection neither of us feels﻿—but I can always be sure of one thing: he will be drunk!
“At first, he claimed he wrote better when stimulated, and I believed him; drank with him﻿—all times of the day, in all sorts of places, with all kinds of people. He said it helped him to the local colour necessary to his story writing. I took his word for it. Then I saw that it was rapidly doing him up. He was losing his magazine market. His things began to come back with curt little notes. I hated to nag him, but the time came when I had to rebel against being dragged about with his greasy crowd of drunken pretenders to some sort of literary or artistic talent﻿—would-be’s and has-beens!
“Now he is out on his own, doing little or nothing. Thank God, we don’t have to worry about the rent or where the next meal is coming from. Dear old dad saw to that. So long as they continue to mine copper in the Upper Peninsula, Tommy and I can go through the motions of living, but it’s a dull business and life has gone flat. I would leave him tomorrow if it were not that I feel under a sort of obligation. I’m as much responsible for his habits as anyone. What would you do in my place?”
---
So﻿—nothing whatever ailed Northwestern Copper or Joyce would be having trouble with her dividends. She would write to Monty and press some serious questions. Had Monty volunteered to look after her business with the purpose of appropriating her income? Why had she not sought counsel before putting herself at his mercy? There were plenty of reliable trust companies﻿ ﻿… Perhaps there was an explanation﻿ ﻿… Well, he should have his chance to offer it﻿ ﻿… very soon, too! She would write today!﻿ ﻿… No﻿—tomorrow. She wasn’t up to it today﻿ ﻿… So he was taking Brightwood by storm, was he?﻿ ﻿… Glass-blowing!﻿ ﻿… Whatever for?﻿ ﻿… What had surgery to do with glass-blowing?﻿ ﻿… Why hadn’t he told the truth about that episode at Gordon’s!﻿ ﻿… She was glad his letter of apology had been too vague for Joyce to understand﻿ ﻿… Poor Joyce!
Gathering up her mail, she arose from the table, smiled at old Martino as he drew back her chair, and strolled slowly down the winding carriage-drive. The gay little parasol refused to share her trouble, and brightened her face. She descended the narrow flight of steps to the next level of the mountain road that spiralled up from the village; followed a graceful arc of it to the second long flight which widened to a street flanked by picturesque little shops.
Yesterday she had promised herself that this morning she would make an excursion to the Villa Carlotta. Diagonally across the bay, on a heavily wooded shore of indescribable beauty, the famous home of an absentee prince was open to tourists﻿ ﻿… Some important Canovas, some rare orchids, a wide variety of exotics﻿ ﻿… She must see them. Everyone did.
There was no lack of attentive service as she stepped down into the red-and-blue canopied motorboat﻿ ﻿… It required many assistants at the little wharf in front of the Villa Carlotta to attend to her disembarkation. The diminutive parasol was handed up to the nearest member of the reception committee, the Baedeker in its leather cover was passed to another, the field-glasses on a strap were unslung from her shoulder; and, regretting with a gesture of dismay that there was nothing further to take off, she reached up both hands and permitted the envied pair who remained uncommissioned to help her over the gunwale.
Smilingly collecting her possessions, she ascended to the huge iron gates and into the coolness of the great hall﻿—ceiled, walled, floored with white marble.
A young American woman, about her own age﻿—possibly a little older﻿—was seated on a graceful marble bench﻿—the only place in the room where one might sit﻿—intent upon the famous Cupid and Psyche. She was modish in a grey tailored suit with a close fitting grey hat fringed with tawny curls.
With a brief glance they took each other’s measure, nodded, smiled. Helen sat down beside her.
“It’s the best thing he did, don’t you think?” ventured the girl in grey.
“Exquisite!”
Well﻿—she ought to know what things were exquisite, reflected Marion. The word described herself﻿ ﻿… Somewhere in this vicinity she expected to meet a young woman with blue eyes, long lashes, blue-black hair probably coiffed in what was known as a windblown bob, a smile as tantalizing as Mona Lisa’s, and a voice that made you think of a cello. (“Bobby﻿—for Heaven’s sake!” she had protested. “Are you sure it isn’t like a heavenly harp?”﻿ ﻿… “Well﻿—something like that,” he had agreed.)
“What else is there to see?” she inquired, after a long silence.
“I never was here before,” Helen replied. “The gardens, I think, and some foreign trees and ferns. Shall we look? You’re alone, aren’t you?”
“Quite﻿—alone and lonesome.”
The patter of their heels echoed through the spacious corridor as they sought the autumn sunshine. On the terrace they hesitated, inquired of an attendant, and took the broad path northward through the artfully landscaped gardens.
“You came over from Bellagio?”
She had. Last night she had arrived from Lugano, and was stopping at a little hotel in the village﻿ ﻿… Thought she’d stay a week, perhaps.
“Oh﻿—then you must move up to the Villa Serbelloni. I’d be so happy if you did!”
Immediately they had disclosed their identities, the budding friendship developed with all the rapidity natural to a meeting of two lonely fellow-countrymen in a foreign land. Young Mrs. Dawson’s story was quickly told.
“He’ll be all the better for not having me to bother with until he settles into his routine,” she explained. “And, anyway, it’s my first experience of Europe. I want to ramble about and see things.”
“Queer you should have come to Bellagio direct from Paris. I’m glad you did, of course; but there’s nothing here but an amazingly fine view﻿ ﻿… People don’t, you know. They go to﻿—oh, down into the château country, or along the Riviera; Rome, Naples, Florence﻿ ﻿… How did you happen to come here?”
“I read about it in a book﻿ ﻿… long time ago﻿ ﻿… Always wanted to come here!”
It was fun to search for mutual interests. Doctor Dawson had just finished the Medical School in June. His first honours had taken him to Vienna. Brain surgery﻿—that was his speciality﻿ ﻿… Helen had had a letter, just this morning, from a friend in Detroit who knew intimately the young doctor taking second honours in that class. Without doubt, Doctor Dawson knew him well.
“Merrick?” Marion’s brows wrinkled in an attempt at remembrance. “Oh, yes﻿—tall, serious chap, wasn’t he? But, you never met him﻿—”
“I’ve seen him﻿ ﻿… I think that describes him﻿—pretty well.”
“What a duck of a grotto!﻿ ﻿… Let’s go down!”
They descended into the mossy, fern-fringed enclosure and rested on the circular stone seat, facing a stucco Pan on a graceful pedestal.
“What did he have to be so serious about?” queried Helen. “Surely things should have come easy enough for him!”
“Do you think he looks serious?﻿ ﻿… Why﻿—he’s the most roguish thing I ever saw!﻿ ﻿… Serious?﻿ ﻿… With that impish grin?”
“Oh﻿—you mean Pan!﻿ ﻿… He’s a little devil!”
“And you were still thinking about young Doctor Merrick.” Marion pinioned her lower lip in an understanding smile and mysteriously half closed one eye. “Maybe he wasn’t serious, at all. He wouldn’t need to be. Awfully rich, isn’t he?”
They wandered on, occasionally coming upon delicious surprises﻿—a short flight of worn steps by a wall mantled with Banksian roses descending to a shady water-gate﻿—a little classic pavilion, the flagging strewn with fugitive yellow leaves. Marion loosed her imagination, prattling of romances and intrigues sheltered by these sequestered nooks, through the years.
“He’s at Brightwood now,” observed Helen, at the first full stop in her new friend’s rhapsody. “That was Doctor Hudson’s hospital; so﻿—naturally﻿—I’m interested.”
“Yes﻿—you would be.” Marion smiled, cryptically.
It was far past noon now. The little boat that had brought Helen over was moored at the wharf. They were helped into it. Neither spoke for full five minutes﻿—Helen regarding their silver trail on the placid water, Marion’s eyes held by the lovely terraces and gates of the Villa.
“I think,” said Marion dreamily, “that is the most wonderfully beautiful place I ever saw!”
“What I can’t understand”﻿—Helen’s face was a study in perplexity﻿—“is how they could have helped knowing each other﻿—intimately﻿ ﻿… Taking the honours together﻿ ﻿… and specializing in the same thing﻿ ﻿… a very restricted field, too!”
Marion turned and regarded her with a slow smile.
“If I’d known I was ever to find somebody who was so interested in him, I’d have made it my business to get acquainted﻿ ﻿… Let’s poke about in some of those funny little shops before we go up﻿ ﻿… Want to?”
---
Marion Dawson went to her room that night﻿—she had moved up to the Villa﻿—with many troublesome misgivings. So far as the success of her mission was concerned, it was assured. Bobby had sent her to find out the whole truth about Helen’s financial misfortunes and to put him on the track of their possible remedy. He had confided that his interest was in the main philanthropic. Had Doctor Hudson lived, she would not have met this disaster; and Doctor Hudson’s death was more or less chargeable to him. At least, he admitted a heavy responsibility. Her welfare was his concern.
“Sure there isn’t any more to it than that, Bobby?” she had teased.
“I wish there was more to it,” he had confessed, “but there can’t be﻿ ﻿… I’ve quite put that idea out of my mind﻿ ﻿… Frankly﻿—she hates the sight of me!”
It hadn’t required much feminine intuition to discover that Bobby’s estimate of Helen Hudson’s attitude toward him was exactly wrong﻿ ﻿… How she could delight him, if she wished, with an impressionistic report of today’s conversation﻿ ﻿… But that wouldn’t be fair﻿ ﻿… Precisely where was her allegiance in this matter?﻿ ﻿… It was traitorous enough to extract Helen’s confidence about her money difficulties﻿—but that would be ultimately to her advantage. When she learned﻿—if she ever did﻿—how her financial anxieties were relieved, she would not question the method﻿ ﻿… But she would never forgive a breach of confidence about her interest in Bobby﻿ ﻿… Really, it was most unpleasant﻿—being a spy.
All afternoon they had been together, rambling in and out of the crooked little streets; at four, grinding laboriously up the hill in the ancient fiacre with the high, steel-tired wheels; at seven, tarrying over their dinner in the arbour﻿—each conscious of a friendship destined, they felt, to become very valuable. Helen had insisted upon her having a room next her own, on the south side where the big balconied windows looked out upon the bay﻿ ﻿… Tomorrow they were having an early breakfast so they could catch the little steamer on its first trip of the day to Como.
“I’d flick this job tonight,” wrote Marion, “and come straight home, if it wasn’t that I knew my detective work would benefit her. She’s been terribly lonely, awfully troubled; and she’s going to tell me all about it in the next few days. I won’t have to ask her a single question. She’s going to tell me of her own accord. But I do feel so mean, Bobby, with this deception. What an adorable creature she is! I never met anybody to whom I was so quickly drawn. Please don’t ever let her find out about my part in this. I don’t believe I could bear it if she learned I had cultivated her for a purpose!”
---
The shopkeepers in little Bellagio became quite accustomed to the sight of two remarkably attractive young American women on their streets, and the skippers of the pleasure craft, plying Lake Como, were proud to have them for frequent passengers. Every morning they breakfasted together in the arbour, every evening they strolled, arm in arm, through the lovingly tended gardens of the hotel.
There was very little about each other’s story that they did not know now. Their confidences had been tender, girlish, unreserved. It was no ordinary friendship. From the first moment, they had been irresistibly attracted, and made no effort to sustain the reticence each would have felt, naturally, toward a stranger.
All forenoon of that tragic Tuesday, which they were to remember with agony, they had hiked along a tortuous mountain road above Menaggio. Helen had laid bare her whole dilemma in the case of her business dealings with Monty, and was strongly counselled to wait a while and do nothing until her return to the States, seeing that her income was assured for the present﻿ ﻿… With the bars all down, she talked freely about Bobby too, confessing by her tone all that she hesitated to put into words.
It began to rain after luncheon. They agreed upon a siesta, and went to their rooms﻿ ﻿… An hour later, Helen, having wakened, decided to write some letters. She remembered she had left her guidebook in Marion’s room. Quietly turning the knob and finding the door unlocked, she tiptoed across the room, smiling at the sleeping face on the pillow, and took up her Baedeker from the writing-desk. Beside it, stamped and ready for mailing, was a bulky letter addressed to Dr. Robert Merrick, Brightwood Hospital, Detroit, Michigan, U.S.A.
She was stunned﻿ ﻿… as if someone had dealt her a blow over the heart. Scarcely able to breathe, she groped her way blindly out of the room, so fearful of rousing Marion that she left the door ajar rather than risk rattling the latch. For a long time she sat on the edge of her bed, shoulders bowed, hands listless on her knees. The world had caved in. With hot cheeks, she recalled some of the things she had whispered to Marion Dawson﻿—confidences no Inquisition machinery could have twisted out of her﻿ ﻿… Doubtless all these impulsive confessions had been spread on paper to satisfy the curiosity of Bobby Merrick. It was clear enough now!﻿ ﻿… What an odd coincidence they had thought it﻿—she and Marion﻿—that they had been brought together in this accidental way, and found the dearest friendship either of them had ever known!﻿ ﻿… Coincidence﻿—indeed!
---
Marion slept heavily until five and roused with the uneasy sensation that something unpleasant had occurred. It was raining torrents. The room was dark. A strong draught was blowing through. The door was open. She distinctly remembered having closed it.
Suddenly she gasped and clutched at her throat with both hands. She walked with short, reluctant steps to the desk. Helen’s Baedeker was gone! It would have been impossible for her to recover it without noticing the letter. She flung herself across the bed, swept with remorse.
A half hour later, frantically drumming on her temples with her fingers, she resolved to take the letter to Helen and beg her to read it. She would tell the whole story, and try to explain how she came to be involved in this benevolent treachery.
Heart pounding, face flushed, she tapped gently at Helen’s door and received no response; tried the latch and found it locked.
Returning to her room, she nervously dressed for dinner, and went slowly down the winding staircase; searched the lounge, glanced into the dining-room; finally summoned her courage to approach the desk of the concierge.
“Has Mrs. Hudson come down?” she asked, with a dry throat.
“She is gone, madame﻿ ﻿… You did not know?”
“Gone?﻿ ﻿… You mean she has left the hotel?”
“About four o’clock, madame.”
“But﻿—where?”
“She left no address, madame﻿ ﻿… She said she would send for her trunks, later.”
Marion turned slowly away and retraced her steps spiritlessly to the foot of the stairs; then, after some hesitation, came again to the desk.
“Will you see if there is a message for me?”
Obediently he went through the motions of inspecting several pigeonholes on the wall behind him and thumbed a pack of letters, looking for something neither of them expected he would find.
It poured hard all night, and the Villa Serbelloni﻿—if not the loneliest place in the world﻿—was a close runner-up to the Continental Hotel in Milan, for that distinction.

===Chinese===

早晨九点钟——九月初。
夏日游客中的最后一批走了，依依不舍，舍不得把这一片美景撇在身后，个个发誓早早回来……他们手里捧着大束大束的园中鲜花——老仆们殷勤相赠，仿佛真心惋惜他们的离去——在车站巴士溜滑的座位上挪动着让出位置，给那些匆匆赶来的、刚从露台矮墙上望了蓝湖最后一眼的人。他们将下山去贝拉焦，渡湖到梅纳焦，乘一段好笑的小登山铁路翻过山，一路向西——回家去。
塞尔贝洛尼别墅今晨很静……它本就不是个喧哗的地方，即便客满也只那么几个人。这座老宅那股说不出的宁静，有一种东西，能把人拖慢到它从容的节拍里；把嗓音泡软，把视线泡花。
这里的气氛像一剂奇异的镇静药，给整个地区罩上一层虚幻。人觉得自己正走在一张柯罗的画里。山与湖上流转的云影，应和着一缕缕转暖的秋风，把人对距离与时辰的估算捉弄得莫名其妙。谁也说不准今天是星期二还是星期四——也没人在乎。
有人说过一句：这里像一幅模糊的画……失了焦。紫色的山峦和下面绿松石色的湖，都没有棱角分明的轮廓。连车道上硌脚的卵石都不真实，每一颗都裹着一圈淡淡的蛋白石色的微光晕……藤架遮着早餐座，那一串串匀称的葡萄，每一粒都围着一圈琥珀的光环，仿佛内里透着光……一个做白日梦的绝妙去处。
不过，要把此地领略到位，客人须得带一颗安静的、无心事的头脑来这小凉亭，否则这地方那股超越时间的安宁，只会把内心的动荡衬得更凶……在这里，人若不能与自己讲和，会比在沙漠里更绝望地孤单、消沉。
凉亭里几乎没人了。除了贴着露台矮墙那一排最末一桌的一对英国老夫妇——埋头看信，时不时摸一摸咖啡杯的把手——海伦·哈德森独占了这地方。她孤单得只好满怀同志式的关切，看一只雄心勃勃的蜜蜂跟她争夺那一小罐蜂蜜的滑稽表演。
世界上第二可爱的地方究竟在哪里，恐怕永远争不出个名堂……拉霍亚？……路易斯湖？……哥伦比亚河公路？……罗亚尔峡谷？……大峡谷？
旅欧三年，海伦的忠心辗转易主：从满月下的威尼斯大运河，到上科尼切大道、阿玛尔菲车道、从海德堡城堡破败的阳台上透过树梢望见的内卡河。
可世上最可爱的一片风景，从来没有疑问。她此刻正对着它——科莫湖——从塞尔贝洛尼别墅东侧的小凉亭里，在俯瞰贝拉焦的山顶上……只是今日她看着它，却视而不见；她的眼睛，另有所属。
---
早间邮件差不多坐实了几桩折磨人的疑心。如今可以十拿九稳：蒙蒂一直在操纵她的产业，让她大吃其亏。怎样保全自己、躲过一场大祸——倘若大祸还没有被铸成的话——又不把娘家拖进一场丢脸的风波，这道题太绕，解不开。
自从一年前，经蒙蒂再度劝说，她把全部事务交给他以来，他汇来的钱就从没有一次如她的指望。
一月里他来信说，西北铜业正处在“重组”之中，股价一时受压，股息削减；她虽然纳闷，也还是打算信他的话不错。他随信附上的解释，细节无穷无尽，满纸行话，全然不知所云，她根本不装懂。这局面叫她又烦又不便，可她努力相信蒙蒂。身边无人可问；通信请教的，也既没有她愿问的、也没有她敢问的。她给了蒙蒂全权委托，使他成了她的商务代理。她在他手里，任他摆布。这太叫人心神不宁了。
七月中，他又写来长信，深表遗憾与失望：西北铜业恢复得如此缓慢；仍缠在“重组”困难、“再融资”问题和“无谓官司的无聊拖延”里——蒙蒂天生一支笔，写得皮里阳秋、废话成山。总而言之，西北铜业的半年结算期过去了，没有向股东宣布分文股息……他比说得出的更抱歉——当然，这不是他的错。
她这才惊醒：自己孤身在国外，没有进项，也没人担保进项会回来；为此她整日整日发愁，盘算这尴尬处境的下一步。
她想起她在布莱特伍德的持股兴许可以变现。她知道她继承的医院股份没有市面。它同寻常的工商证券全然两样。收益微薄且不定；那股票本身，就看哪位慈善的买主肯出多少。
何况在她心里，它还系着一份情分。若非真正迫在眉睫的急需，她决不肯脱手。可情分归情分，日子总要过。她的现款所余无几了。
愁了一个月，她写信给南希·阿什福德。要不要和盘托出实情，她掂量了一阵，到底没提；只问：她布莱特伍德的股份，可否尽早寻个买主折成现钱。阿什福德太太想必又要惊又要失望，随她怎么想吧。真正的缘由，说不得。
---
“呢——我倒纳闷，她到底为什么要这样？”收到信后，南希对梅里克医生说，“她的日子明明过得下去……她那西北铜业的进项，一年怎么也不会低于六千块……优先股……坚实得跟直布罗陀一样……你说会不会有人坑她？她对生意一窍不通。”
“她不在的这段，谁替她管事？”
“没人——据我所知……她那摊子事不复杂……我好像听她说过，她哥哥——该说是堂兄——会帮她填所得税申报表。”
“这人你了解吗？”
“一无所知……会不会是他在替她管钱？”
“不像……他凭什么？……除非她给了他全权买卖；她不会的。”
“那我跟她说什么，博比？”
“她布莱特伍德的股份值多少？”
“往保守里说？”南希咧嘴一笑。
“不——往乐观里说！——按密考伯的估价法！”
“我看两万上下……你要它？”
 “要……你去告诉她，你已经谈妥了买主，肯出——两万五千块买下她布莱特伍德的股份……告诉她，那人希望分二十五个月按月付清……这样她在那边就不愁断粮；同时嘛……”
“同时怎么？……”
梅里克医生朝门口走去。
“哦——我也不知道——说不好……那是她的事。”
---
海伦又一次拿起阿什福德太太刚寄到的长信，连同那救命的汇票，和日后按月照付的确实许诺。拿着它，她心里却七上八下。她剪断了自己与哈德森医生最珍视的事业之间一条要紧的纽带。这等于朝他毕生的念想，关上了一扇门。
信里没有责备，明里暗里都没有……倒不难听出一层担忧。
“但愿你这番脱手布莱特伍德的股份，并不是因为其他来源的进项有所亏空……有什么要我们替你照看的吗？”
在简明交代了成交的买卖和付款条件之后，南希·阿什福德又端上一盆本地新闻大杂烩……底特律你都要认不得了！……新俱乐部、新剧院、气派的旅馆、大都会式的铺子！……连医院都在添新楼，图纸都画好了。
“年轻的梅里克医生如今在我们这儿了。或许你已知道。眼看他进步得这样快，我自然欢喜，因为我一向关心他……不用说，比起寻常实习医生，他占着大便宜：从医学院头一天起，他就知道要专攻脑外科，所以他来时，专门知识与经验就比进过本院的哪个年轻医生都多……如今连派尔医生待他都是一副客气劲儿，看得我直乐。（派尔医生对雏儿们可凶呢。）……博比获准布置了一间自己的小实验室。顶楼大日光疗养室旁边原有个小凹室——你也许记得——我们把它隔了出去。他那套设备你真该看看。我看与其说是化学实验室，不如说是物理实验室……吹玻璃！……一座锻炉！……一座熔炉！……五花八门的电器！……你休想从他嘴里套出什么……昨天我去那儿，问他是不是在装一台新收音机，他说：‘差不多。’……这话从他嘴里出来，意思是：请你别搅我。
“哦——我这辈子没有比他毕业那天更激动过！我一直对他抱着好大的指望……等到大日子来了……他的名字带着星号印在毕业典礼的节目单上（第二名荣誉他对我只字未提！）我坐在那儿，傻傻地直掉泪……他母亲没能来，我就冒充了一回他的母亲……他祖父那样子你真该瞧瞧！得意吧？全班上台领文凭的时候，梅里克老先生站到椅子上，又想挥手绢又想用它擤鼻涕，两个动作打架，直到人家扯着他的下摆把他拽下来。”
读到这儿，信纸在海伦手里发颤。
那页——编到第八页——最末一行被划掉了，但仍认得出：
“获得——的那位年轻医生”
下一页原先编到第十二页，也照样划掉，改成了第九。
南希·阿什福德转念之间，抽掉了信里相当大的一截。
---
“这是一千块的汇票。”博比·梅里克立在南希桌旁说。
“谢了。信刚写完。今天就寄。”
“好！……对了——道森夫妇的事，你一个字没提吧？”
“提了呀——怎么……不该提？”
“删掉……我看她不会有多大兴趣……再说——呢，我有我的道理。”
“随你。”南希说着，急急抽掉她写过字的几页：
“——头名荣誉是梅里克医生最要好的朋友……达蒙与皮西阿斯……书里才读得到的交情！整个医学院两人同窗——同攻一个专科。今年的头名荣誉附带一份奖金，欧文·西蒙斯先生（西蒙斯透平公司）所赠——维也纳留学一年，全部费用、奖学金、宽裕的津贴，等等……道森夫妇上星期四来过——你信到的那天。她正要去纽约送她丈夫启程。
“说真的，他们三个处得跟亲兄妹一样。我都忍不住眼热。他们在我办公室里关起门谈了整整一个钟头，出来时我在走廊撞见，道森太太兴奋得满面红光。她那高兴劲儿，我脱口说：‘你活像圣诞节的早晨！’‘可不是嘛？’她笑着答，‘我也去！’……‘去欧洲？’……‘千真万确！’
“他们有个可爱的小男孩——快四岁了。我从没见过教养这么好的孩子。梅里克医生陪他们去了纽约，把小杰克带回了温迪米尔，等道森太太回来。我敢说，这会儿梅里克老先生正牵着给他新买的小马，陪他玩呢。”……
---
还有一封乔伊斯的信：
“不会的，亲爱的，我知道你决不会说‘我早跟你说过’这种话寻快活。那不是你……无一日不自责，那年冬天，你拼命想拦在我和我自己中间，我那样作践你，真是天理难容。
“那些日子我对谁都不公道。连对博比也不。这一层我从没跟你交过底。本想说的；可是，你看，第二天一早我给他打了电话，请他来见我。我迷糊记得他前一夜——呢——对我不错，我想我们的交情兴许能续上。他推托了。我又伤又辱。我早看出你跟博比之间生了嫌隙，因为——亲爱的，你能原谅我吗？——我注意到他随后写给你的一封信，偷看了一眼。他求你原谅他害你受了许多苦。我便知道，他没把真相全告诉你。你以为那晚他也在戈登家那群醉鬼里，我便由你那么想着——自打他拒绝再见我之后。老实说，他去那儿时是清醒的，为了把我弄出来，跟满场的人动了手；而我醉得不省人事，隔了三天才弄明白那一晚到底出了什么事。我知道博比在你心里一文不值，你看他不起；可他那一感恩节之夜怎么会送我回家的，你理当知道，这对他才公道。
“不，亲爱的，一点也没见好。永远也不会好了。这我现在懂了。汤米管不住自己。今夜他醉醺醺回来，乖戾，绷着脸；明夜醉醺醺回来，傻笑，伤感；后夜醉醺醺回来，好辩，挑剔；再后夜醉醺醺回来，凶暴，骂人——回回都醉。这一点我倒拿得稳！我永远猜不透他今晚是哪一种——是骂我对他、对他的作品漠不关心呢（他正经写的也没多少！），还是拿我们俩谁都不信的假恩爱来缠磨我——可有一样我永远拿得稳：他一定醉着！
“起初他说，微醺时写得更好，我信了；陪他喝——一天到晚，什么场合，跟什么人都喝。他说这有助于他写小说搜罗地方色彩。我信了他的话。后来眼看酒快把他毁了。他在杂志上的路子越走越窄。稿子开始夹着三言两语的退条回来。我不愿唠叨他，可到后来，我实在受不了被拖着他那帮油腔滑调、假装有什么文学或艺术才分的酒鬼——一群不成器的和过了气的——四处打转！
“如今他单干，几乎什么都不做。谢天谢地，我们不用愁房租愁下一顿饭。老爸早安排好了。只要上半岛还挖铜，汤米和我就能把日子这么演下去；可这日子乏味，生活早走了气。若不是觉得自己多少欠着他，我明天就离开他。他这习惯，我跟谁一样有责任。换了你在我这地步，你会怎么办？”
---
呢——西北铜业一点毛病也没有，不然乔伊斯的股息早出乱子了。她要写信给蒙蒂，好好追问几句。蒙蒂当年毛遂自荐替她管产业，莫非就是存心吞她的进项？她当初为什么不清教个明白人，就把自己交到他手心里？靠得住的信托公司有的是……兴许另有解释……呢，也该给他个机会解释……而且要快！今天就写！……不——明天。今天她提不起这精神……哦，他正横扫布莱特伍德呢，是不是？……吹玻璃！……吹玻璃做什么？……外科跟吹玻璃有什么相干？……戈登家那一段，他当年为什么不讲实话！……幸亏他那封道歉信写得含糊，乔伊斯才没能看懂……可怜的乔伊斯！
她收起信件，离了桌，老马尔蒂诺替她拉椅时她报以一笑，缓缓踱下蜿蜒的车道。那柄俏丽的小阳伞不肯分担她的愁，反倒照亮了她的脸。她下了一段窄台阶，到上一层山路——那路从村里螺旋般绕上来；又顺着一段优美的弧线走到第二道长台阶，台阶尽头展开成一条街，两旁是些如画的小铺。
昨天她就跟自己约好，今早去卡洛塔别墅一游。湖对岸斜对面，一片美得无法言喻的茂林岸边，一位常年不在家的亲王的著名府邸向游人开放……几尊重要的卡诺瓦，几种珍稀兰花，各式各样的异卉……她非看不可。谁都看的。
她步下红蓝篷顶的摩托快艇时，殷勤的伺候一样不缺……在卡洛塔别墅门前的小码头上，光伺候她下船就得好几个帮手。小阳伞递给迎宾委员会最靠前的一位，皮套里的导游手册递给第二位，肩上挎带的望远镜也卸了下来；她两手一摊，懊恼再没有东西可脱，便伸出双手，由那两位没领到差事、正眼红的伙计扶她跨过船舷。
她笑盈盈收回随身物件，登上大铁门，走进大厅的凉意里——顶、墙、地，一色白大理石。
一位年轻的美国女子，年纪与她相仿——兴许略长——坐在一张秀气的大理石长凳上——全厅唯一可坐的地方——专心致志地看那尊著名的《丘比特与普赛克》。她一身灰色定制套装，式样时新，灰色的窄帽檐下压着一圈黄褐色的鬈发。
两人目光一碰，彼此掂了掂，点头，微笑。海伦在她身旁坐下。
“这是他最好的一件，你说呢？”灰衣姑娘搭话。
“美极了！”
呢——什么东西美，她该最有发言权，玛丽恩心想。这个词正说的是她自己……大约就在这一带，她预计要遇见一位蓝眼睛、长睫毛、蓝黑头发大约剪成所谓“风吹式”、笑起来跟蒙娜丽莎一样挑人、嗓音让人想起大提琴的年轻女子。（“博比——求你了！”她当时抗议，“你敢肯定不是像天国的竖琴？”……“呢——大概那么回事。”他认了。）
“还有什么可看的？”静了半晌，她问。
“我也头一回来，”海伦答，“花园吧，还有些外国的树木蕨类。一起逛逛？你是一个人吧？”
“可不——一个人，还孤单。”
两人的鞋跟声在大回廊里清脆地回响，一路去寻秋日的太阳。到了露台，两人踌躇片刻，问了侍者，便取大路向北，穿过人工经营的园林。
“你是从贝拉焦过来的？”
正是。她昨夜从卢加诺到，住在村里一家小旅店……打算住一个礼拜，兴许。
“哦——那你务必搬上塞尔贝洛尼别墅来。你搬来我才高兴呢！”
两人随即互通了姓名身家；这段新交情，以两个孤单的同乡在异国他乡相逢所特有的速度，飞快地生长。小道森太太的经历三言两语就讲完了。
“他得先安下心来做他的事，没我打搅，反倒好。”她解释，“再说，欧洲我头一回来。我要四处逛逛，见见世面。”
“你从巴黎直奔贝拉焦，倒怪有意思。当然，我高兴你来；可这儿除了一幅好得惊人的风景，什么也没有……人们不这么走的。他们去——哦——去城堡区，或者里维埃拉；罗马、那不勒斯、佛罗伦萨……你怎么会到这儿来？”
“我在一本书里读到过……好久以前……就一直想来！”
两人发掘共同兴味，其乐无穷。道森医生六月刚从医学院毕业。他的头名荣誉带他去了维也纳。脑外科——那是他的专科……海伦今早刚收到底特律一位朋友的信；那位朋友，同届拿第二名荣誉的年轻医生，她再熟不过。道森医生无疑跟他熟得很。
“梅里克？”玛丽恩皱眉回忆，“哦，想起来了——高个子，挺严肃的那位，对吧？不过，你没见过他——”
“见过……我想你这四个字形容他——倒挺准。”
“多可人的洞窟！……咱们下去吧！”
两人下进那座苔痕斑斑、蕨叶镶边的洞府，在环形石凳上歇脚，对面是一座秀气的座子，上头立着一尊粉饰的潘神。
“他有什么可那么严肃的？”海伦问，“他的日子还不顺顺当当！”
“你觉得他严肃？……嗐——他是我见过最淘气的一个！……严肃？……带着那副顽童的笑？”
“哦——你说的是潘！……这小鬼头！”
“你还想着年轻的梅里克医生呢。”玛丽恩会心地一笑，抿住下唇，神秘地阖上一只眼。“没准儿他根本不严肃。他也不需要严肃。富得流油，对吧？”
两人继续逛，不时撞见可喜的惊喜——一段贴墙的旧石阶，墙上披着班克西亚蔷薇，下到一处荫凉的水埠——一座古典小亭，石板上散着几片逃亡的黄叶。玛丽恩放开了想象，喋喋讲起这些幽僻角落历年来荫庇过的风流韵事。
“他如今在布莱特伍德。”海伦趁新朋友的兴会暂歇，插了一句，“那是哈德森医生的医院；所以——自然——我关心。”
“是——你会关心的。”玛丽恩莫测地一笑。
此刻早过了正午。载海伦过来的小船泊在码头。两人被扶上船。足足五分钟谁也没说话——海伦望着船尾在平静湖面上犁出的银痕，玛丽恩的眼睛离不开别墅那可爱的露台与门扉。
“我想，”玛丽恩梦呓般地说，“这是我这辈子见过的最美的地方！”
“我不明白的，”海伦一脸的困惑，“是这两个人怎么可能不熟——亲密地熟……荣誉一并拿……专科又相同……那么窄的一个行当！”
玛丽恩转过身，慢慢地朝她笑。
“早知道我会遇上一位这么关心他的人，我当年说什么也要跟他混熟……上去之前，咱们再钻几家好玩的小铺子……去不去？”
---
当夜，玛丽恩·道森回到房里——她已搬进别墅——心事重重。就她此行的使命而论，已成定局。博比派她来，是要查清海伦财务厄运的全部真相，好让他摸到解救的门径。他坦白说，他的关心大体是慈善性质的。哈德森医生若在，她必不会遭此难；而哈德森医生之死，多少要记在他账上。至少，他认下一份重责。她的福祉，就是他的心事。
“就这么多，再没别的了，博比？”她当时打趣。
“我巴不得还有别的，”他招了，“可是不能有了……那个念头我早撂开了……老实说——她一见我就烦！”
用不着多少女人的直觉便能发现：博比对海伦·哈德森待他态度的估计，错得一丝不差……只要她愿意，今天这场谈话，她大可以给他写一份多么叫人心花的印象派汇报……可那不公道……在这桩事里，她的忠心到底站在哪一边？……套出海伦对钱荒的心里话，已经够得上背叛——可那终究是为她好。等她知晓——如果她终有知晓的一天——她的财务焦虑是怎么解的，她不会追问手法……可她决不会原谅有人拿她对博比的那点心思做了背信……说真的，这差事真难受——当密探。
一整个下午，两人形影不离，在那弯弯曲曲的小街里钻进钻出；四点钟，坐着那辆钢箍轮子的高座老马车，吭哧吭哧上了山；七点，在凉亭里磨蹭晚餐——两人都觉得，这场友谊注定要变得极其珍贵。海伦坚持让她住进自己隔壁的房间，朝南，大阳台窗正对着湖湾……明天她们要早早用早餐，好赶上去科莫的小轮船的头一班。
“今夜我就想撂了这差事，”玛丽恩写道，“直接回家——若不是我知道我这侦探活儿到头来是为她好。她一直孤单得厉害，愁得厉害；这几天她就要把一切告诉我了。我一个字都用不着问。她自己会说的。可是博比，连着这场骗局，我实在觉得自己卑鄙。她真是个可人儿！我从没这么快喜欢上过谁。千万别让她查到我在里头的角色。她若知道我接近她是别有目的，我怕我受不住！”
---
贝拉焦小村的店主们渐渐看惯了街上这两位标致得出奇的美国年轻女子；科莫湖上跑生意的船老大们，也以她们常乘自家的船为荣。每天清晨，她们在凉亭共进早餐；每天傍晚，她们挎着胳膊，在旅馆那备受爱怜的园林里散步。
如今，两人彼此的经历，再没什么不知道的了。她们的知心话，温软、少女气、毫无保留。这不是一场寻常的友谊。从第一刻起，她们便身不由己地相互吸引，谁也没打算维持彼此面对生人时自然会有的那点矜持。
在那个悲惨的星期二——她们日后将痛彻地记住它——的整个上午，她们徒步走在梅纳焦上方一条曲折的山路上。海伦把她同蒙蒂生意往来的困境和盘托出；玛丽恩力劝她暂且按兵不动，等回美国再说，眼下进项既已无忧……门户洞开，她谈起博比来也就没了遮拦；言语上说不出口的，那声气全招了。
午饭后天落起雨来。两人说好各睡各的午觉，回房去了……一个钟头后，海伦醒了，想起写几封信。她记起导游手册落在玛丽恩房里。她轻轻拧动门把，门没锁；她踮着脚尖穿过房间，对着枕上那张睡脸笑了笑，从写字台上拿起她的导游书。书旁边，放着一封厚厚的、已贴好邮票待寄的信，收信人是：美利坚合众国密歇根州底特律市布莱特伍德医院罗伯特·梅里克医生。
她懵了……仿佛有人照着她的心口捣了一拳。她几乎喘不上气，跌跌撞撞摸出门去；生怕惊醒玛丽恩，门都不敢带上锁舌响，只留了条缝。她在床沿坐了许久，肩膀塌着，两手没精打采地摊在膝上。天塌了。她两颊发烧，记起自己对着玛丽恩·道森低声讲过的那些话——那些连宗教裁判所的刑具都撬不出的心腹话……想必这一切口没遮拦的自供，都摊在纸上，去满足博比·梅里克的好奇了。如今全明白了！……她跟玛丽恩还当它是多妙的巧遇——在异乡偶然相逢，各自得了平生最亲的朋友！……巧遇——亏想得出！
---
玛丽恩沉沉睡到五点，醒来时心口发闷，总觉得出了什么不快的事。大雨倾盆。屋里昏暗。一股穿堂风正灌进来。门开着。她分明记得自己关了门。
她猛地抽了口气，双手攉住喉咙。她迈着细碎的、不情愿的步子走到书桌前。海伦的导游书不见了！取书的人不可能看不见那封信。她一头扑在床上，悔恨如潮。
半个钟头后，她发疯似的拿手指敲着太阳穴，拿定主意：把信给海伦送去，求她读。她要和盘托出，尽力解释自己怎么会卷进这场“善意的背叛”。
她心口擂鼓，满脸通红，轻轻叩海伦的门，没有应声；试试门把，锁了。
她回房胡乱换上晚餐的衣裳，慢慢走下旋转楼梯；找过休息厅，望过饭厅；末了鼓起勇气，凑到门房柜台前。
“哈德森太太下来了吗？”她嗓音发干地问。
“她走了，夫人……您不知道？”
“走了？……您是说，她离开旅馆了？”
“四点钟光景，夫人。”
“可——去哪儿了？”
“她没留地址，夫人……她说回头会派人来取行李。”
玛丽恩缓缓转身，失魂落魄地走回楼梯口；迟疑片刻，又踅回柜台。
“劳驾看看，有没有留给我的条子？”
门房依言做了一通翻检——把身后墙上几格信件格一一查看，又把一沓信捻了捻——找一件谁都不指望找到的东西。
大雨泼了一整夜；塞尔贝洛尼别墅——就算不是世上最孤单的地方——论这一名号，也紧紧咬着米兰大陆饭店，屈居第二。

## XIII / 十三
===Original===

The girl with the pink hair, languorous lashes gummy with an overdose of mascara, and ugly black muffins over her ears, was informing Mr. Brent that a Doctor Merrick was in the lobby and wished to see him﻿ ﻿… No, he hadn’t said.
“Mr. Brent is dressing for dinner and can’t come down,” she said coyly, with one hand over the mouthpiece. “He says can you tell him on the phone what you want. Just step into booth number two, please.”
“Tell him I’ll be right up.”
She relayed the message, while the tall young man with the shoulders of a discus-thrower and the tapering waist of a fencer tapped the high counter in front of the switchboard with slender, impatient fingers.
“He says it won’t be convenient,” she reported, visibly reluctant to transmit the blunt discourtesy.
“What’s his room number?” inquired Doctor Merrick, unruffled.
“Three hundred and seventy-eight; but﻿—he said he couldn’t see you, you know.”
“Here, boy,” to the bellhop at his elbow, “put those bags in check, and then show me up to three hundred and seventy-eight.”
Mr. Brent was not dressing for dinner. He was packing a trunk; and chairs, bed and table were littered with clothing, papers, books, toilet articles and mussy linen. The room was in great disorder. It was with a very surly scowl that he opened the door to inspect his visitor.
“Merrick﻿—you say? Never heard of you. What’s wanted?” He planted his short, stocky bulk defiantly in the doorway, hands on hips.
“Ask me in and I’ll tell you,” said Merrick quietly.
Brent reluctantly stepped back.
“Oh, very well,” he snapped, peevishly. “But make it peppy. I’m busy﻿—as you see﻿ ﻿… Thought I’d sent word down that I didn’t care to be bothered.”
The loud-checked coat that matched the trousers he had on was lifted from the back of a chair and tossed on the bed.
“Sit down﻿—if you want to.”
Young Merrick ignored the sour invitation and proceeded to state his errand.
“I live in Detroit where I am associated with Brightwood Hospital.”
Brent’s face, pallid, and bristly with two days’ beard, went a shade paler.
“Yeah?”
“You may recall that Brightwood Hospital was brought into prominence by your cousin’s late husband﻿—Doctor Hudson.”
“Well﻿—and then what?” growled Brent, insolently.
“It came to our attention, about thirty days ago, that Mrs. Hudson﻿—now in Italy, as you know﻿—was obliged to dispose of her interest in the hospital.”
“And how’s that any of your damn business?” demanded Brent, stepping toward Merrick belligerently. “You’re just a doctor, aren’t you? Couldn’t she sell her hospital stock, if she wanted to, without consulting you?”
“Quite true,” replied Merrick, determined not to lose his temper. “She had no occasion to confide in me, and didn’t. But if we are interested in her welfare, I should think that might meet with your approval. You’ve been managing her affairs, haven’t you?”
“Yeah! And I don’t need any help!”
“I happen to know that you do. That’s what I came here to talk about.”
“And what makes you so powerfully interested in my cousin?” Brent sneered. “Trying to get your fingers on her money?”
The fingers were restless.
“I advise you not to presume too far on my patience, Brent.”
“When you get too impatient, you can leave!﻿ ﻿… You want to marry my cousin, I suppose﻿—but have to make sure, first, that she has plenty to keep you!”
“Just for the moment,” cautioned Merrick, “we’ll not be talking about Mrs. Hudson. We’re going to talk about you!﻿ ﻿… And that Northwestern Copper stock!”
“What do you mean, you low-down, sneaking spy?”
“I mean that in the last twelve months you’ve lost upwards of one hundred thousand dollars on the ticker and the ponies﻿ ﻿… That last big flyer in oil﻿—along in May, wasn’t it?﻿—wiped out the Northwestern Copper completely!﻿ ﻿… I’m here, as Mrs. Hudson’s friend, to find out exactly what you propose to do about it.”
Brent’s face was livid. He stamped to the door and threw it open.
“Now﻿—out you go, damn you!﻿—or I’ll call the house detective!”
Merrick turned to the untidy desk and took up the telephone.
“I’ll save you the trouble,” he said quietly, lifting the receiver.
“Put that down!” screamed Brent, slamming the door.
Merrick smiled and obeyed.
“You’re not anxious to talk to a detective, Brent. But you’re going to talk to me! Do you want to come clean on all this, now, and tell me about it﻿—or do I have to break you in two over that table? I can do it, you know; and I’d like to!”
Blind with fury, Brent lunged savagely with his fist. Stepping aside to let it go by, Merrick caught the shaking wrist in a vice-like grip of his left hand. With the other, he gathered up a large handful of Brent’s throat, pushed him to the table, and bent him over it﻿—back﻿—back farther, until the purple neck was corded with distended veins, and his laboured breathing signed it was a good time to ease up.
“Like to talk business now?”
Brent raised heavily to one elbow, and his hand fumbled in the desk drawer.
“Drop that gun!” Merrick closed down on the wrist until Brent’s fingers released their hold on the automatic. It fell to the floor. “That shooting project costs you extra.” Once more Brent’s Adam’s apple and environs were compressed until his breath came in agonizing little whistles.
Merrick stooped and picked up the gun, emptied it, pocketed the cartridges, and waited for his host to recover.
After some minutes he sat up, rubbed his bloodshot eyes clumsily with his fists, and felt gingerly of his neck.
“Well,” he croaked, “now you’ve proved you’re bigger than I am, what do you want?”
“I’m no bigger than you are, Brent. Your trouble was that you rushed me with your eyes shut. Just took a long chance that maybe, somehow, your blow would land. That’s probably what ails you, all along the line. You close your eyes and whang away, hoping you’ll strike something by accident﻿ ﻿… That afterthought of yours about the gun﻿ ﻿… you’d be in a nice mess by now if I had let you shoot me﻿ ﻿… Some little gambler, you are!”
“I’d rather take a chance on the chair than the pen!”
“That proves what I’m saying﻿ ﻿… And﻿—speaking of chairs and pens, crawl over there to your desk and sit down. I’m going to have you write something for me.”
“If you think you’re going to extort a confession from me, Merrick, you’ve another think coming.”
“Confession? Nonsense! I’ve enough evidence in my pocket to send you up for twenty years﻿ ﻿… I want you to write a letter to your cousin. I’ll dictate it. Don’t make any mistakes in it, for I’m going to mail it myself.”
Brent slumped off the table and sweeping the desk clear of its litter took up a pen.
“Begin with her address and your customary salutation. Continue as follows: In view of the uncertainty of your income from your Northwestern Copper stock, I have disposed of it﻿—”
Flinging the pen down, Brent shouted, “Do you realize you’re forcing me to write this letter?”
“Oh, rather! That’s why you’re writing it! Proceed, please.”
“Do you know what that is?﻿ ﻿… compelling me to write this?”
“What is it?”
“Why﻿—it’s﻿—it’s﻿—”
“You think up the name for it after I’m gone﻿ ﻿… Pick up that pen!﻿ ﻿… You’ve got that about your disposal of her Northwestern Copper? Now﻿—go on﻿ ﻿… I have taken its equivalent for you in Axion Motor Corporation, preferred﻿—”
Brent hesitated and glanced up mystifiedly.
“I don’t understand!”
“That’s unimportant,” said Merrick crisply. “It’s none of your business, in fact. You’re just acting as my clerk for the moment. You have nothing further to do in your cousin’s business affairs after writing this note:﻿—‘Axion Motor Corporation, preferred; five hundred shares listed today at two hundred and twenty-six. This stock is held by the Trust Department of the Fourth National Bank of Detroit from where your dividends will hereafter be remitted to you regularly.’ ”
“How do I know that’s true?” growled Brent.
“You don’t; but﻿—as I said﻿—it’s no affair of yours. Have you written it? Now﻿—one more paragraph:﻿—I find that an important business errand requires me to go immediately to Buenos Aires﻿—”
“But﻿—I’m not going to Buenos Aires!”
“Oh, yes, you are!﻿ ﻿… Sailing on Saturday﻿ ﻿… You’re just packing up, now, to leave for Washington to get the passport I’ve arranged for you. From there you go to New York where you sail on the *Vigo*﻿ ﻿… Get on now with your correspondence:﻿—‘I do not know how long I shall be away; so I am transferring my entire responsibility for your business to the Trust Department of the Fourth National Bank of Detroit. Mr. T. P. Randall will verify this and give you a clear statement of your affairs in a short time﻿ ﻿…’ Now add whatever pleasant amenities you may have the crust to write to a woman you have robbed, and sign your name to it﻿ ﻿… and address an envelope.”
While he wrote, Merrick took out his wallet, extracted a steamship ticket, and counted two thousand dollars in bills of large denomination.
“There.” He pointed to it, as Brent finished. “That is for you. Take it﻿—and be off! And if you’re in the market for any advice, I’d suggest that you quit trying to be a sport, which you most certainly aren’t; make some new connections; find a few honest friends; get yourself a respectable job; buck up﻿—and be a man!”
His face distorted, Brent fumbled with the money, and blindly groped out with his hand. Merrick ignored the gesture. He was not fond of the movies.
“You can get out of the country, can’t you?﻿ ﻿… I’ve fixed things for you so you’ll have no trouble about the passport, unless you’re wanted for some other crime﻿ ﻿… Sure you’ll not be stopped?﻿ ﻿… Haven’t been robbing anybody else besides your little cousin, have you?”
“No! No!﻿ ﻿… God﻿—but I’ve been a rotter!” Collapsing in his chair, Monty buried his tousled head in his arms.
“You don’t have to remain a rotter, Brent﻿ ﻿… Start fresh!﻿ ﻿… Go straight!﻿ ﻿… You can do it!﻿ ﻿… I’m going now. If you have any trouble getting off, wire me. There’s my card. And I presume I needn’t tell you that Mrs. Hudson is never to know anything about this little transaction of ours.”
“You mean,” said Brent, looking up perplexed, “that she’s not going to know you’ve given her all this money? What do you expect to get out of it?”
“That’s my business!﻿ ﻿… No﻿—I think I’ll tell you﻿ ﻿… just to clear your mind of any nasty suspicion that my obligation to her may be somehow to her discredit﻿ ﻿… Do you recall the story of her husband’s death?”
Brent nodded.
“Do you remember that a young man was resuscitated, while Doctor Hudson was drowning?”
“Yeah﻿—I remember﻿ ﻿… some rich guy﻿ ﻿… By God﻿—it was *you*?”
“Yes! My life was saved, that day, by a machine which might have brought Doctor Hudson around, if he’d had a chance at it﻿ ﻿… Had Hudson lived, you would not have squandered Mrs. Hudson’s money﻿ ﻿… Do you understand now?”
“Was that why you became a doctor too?” inquired Brent, wide-eyed.
“That would be a good enough reason﻿ ﻿… but﻿—no matter about that﻿ ﻿… I just wanted you to know the nature of my interest in your cousin﻿ ﻿… I don’t give a damn what you think about me, but I’d prefer you’d think straight about her!”
“So﻿—you figured he had handed your life back to you﻿—sort of﻿—and you had to make good with it; is that it?”
“Something like that.”
For a long time Brent sat staring up glassily, and when he spoke his voice came from a distance.
“God!﻿—I never heard of such a thing!﻿ ﻿… And here you’ve just handed my life back to me!﻿ ﻿… If I were the kind of a person you are, I suppose I’d have to do something about it, wouldn’t I?”
“Oh﻿—not necessarily﻿ ﻿… I was a good while debating whether to do anything about my affair﻿ ﻿… I was a pretty rummy lot, Brent﻿ ﻿… I’d have ducked it if I could.”
“How do you mean﻿—rummy?﻿ ﻿… Hadn’t stolen anything, had you?”
“Never needed to steal anything﻿ ﻿… Had everything﻿ ﻿… Had too much!﻿ ﻿… Corrupted a lot of people with it!﻿ ﻿… You’ve had too little. I suppose that’s the main difference between us.”
“D’you think there’s anything I could do﻿—to square up for what you’ve done for me?”
“Perhaps﻿—if that sort of thing interests you?”
“What﻿—for instance?”
Merrick rose and took up his coat.
“You’ll have to figure that out for yourself.”
“I’d like to, you know,” said Brent earnestly.
“You mean that?” challenged Merrick, putting both hands on Monty’s shoulders and looking him squarely in the eyes.
“Yes﻿—more than I ever meant anything in my life!”
It seemed to Monty that his athletic young benefactor would never emerge from the brown study into which his words had driven him. He stood, leaning against the table, hands deep in his pockets, oblivious to his surroundings.
“In that case,” he said slowly, “I presume I’ll have to help you. I don’t want to! But﻿—there’s just a slim chance that﻿ ﻿… See here! You shave and dress for dinner. I’ll be in the lobby when you come down. We’ll eat﻿—and I’ll tell you all I know about it. After that﻿—it will be up to you!﻿ ﻿… Better put some iodine on those scratches﻿ ﻿… Sorry I hurt you.”
---
They went in a taxi to a down town restaurant. It was somewhat after the dinner hour and their table in the corner permitted them to talk undisturbed. Counselled to forget the experience of the past two hours, Monty had regained his self-confidence and listened with rapt attention to his host. If it was not good soil for the reception of a new idea, it was for no lack of sufficient plowing and harrowing.
“I am going to tell you a strange story, Brent, about a sculptor named Randolph. If it seems incredible to you, I shall not be surprised. It was to me when I first heard it.”
It was a long story. Dinner courses came and went. In the hour, Monty had spoken but once.
“He’d quite lost his mind, hadn’t he?”
Dessert glasses had now been pushed aside and cigars lighted.
“Of course,” Merrick was saying, “if you should decide to experiment with this projection of yourself through investments in other people, you must be prepared for all manner of failures, disillusionments, disgusts. You will frequently go to no end of trouble and expense for somebody who turns out to be a pest and a piker. You will be imposed upon, lied to, lied about! You will run into cases of ingratitude so rank that it will sicken you! But﻿—now and again, you will manage to put the thing over﻿ ﻿… and when you do you will discover it has squared for all the failures you’ve had!”
He paused, and his mood seemed reminiscent.
“I expect you’re wondering,” risked Monty, “whether you’ve been wasting your time and money on me.”
“So far as the money’s concerned, I’m afraid that wasn’t an investment in you. I just wanted to be rid of you. But﻿—you’re quite right about my wondering whether it’s really been worth while to confide to you this theory of personality projection.”
“Do you mind if I inquire whether you think it would give you additional personal power in your undertakings, if I managed to make good?”
“Oh, unquestionably!”
“And﻿—if it ever got out﻿—that you had started me on the way up﻿—then this whole investment of yours in my behalf would be a total loss to you?”
Merrick smiled and toyed preoccupiedly with his ashtray.
“Well﻿—no!﻿ ﻿… Randolph was a bit obsessed, you know. He was for breaking people’s necks if they told of his investments in them. That was going it rather stronger than necessary﻿ ﻿… Hudson was careful to caution his beneficiaries against telling, and threw every possible safeguard around his investments to protect them from discovery; but I don’t believe he felt they hadn’t been worth doing if the facts leaked out﻿ ﻿… The really important feature of it is here: if you succeed in expanding your personality, I shall come in on the reward by just as much as I’ve been responsible for it﻿ ﻿… In the process of expanding yourself, you are almost sure to help somebody else make himself bigger. He, in turn, energizes other people﻿ ﻿… If you know anything about chemistry, you will be helped by considering this as a process of catalysis﻿ ﻿… Personality projection is like any other investment. The thing goes on! It earns compound interest. If you were the agent that set it going, the credit’s yours﻿ ﻿… Some of it will be going at full blast long after you have been declared dead. In actual fact, the real *you* may be more alive, as to personality energy, fifty years after you’re gone, than when you seem to be at the top of your power!”
“But﻿—if your beneficiary does not succeed in making good on your investment?﻿ ﻿…”
Merrick shrugged.
“In that case, you can see for yourself that nothing comes of it.”
“But﻿—you tried! Isn’t there any satisfaction to be had of that?”
“Oh, it’s good practice, I suppose﻿ ﻿… But﻿—if you had spent twenty thousand dollars and six months’ time sinking an oil-shaft, and got nothing but a dry hole, would you have much satisfaction in reflecting that﻿—at all events﻿—you’d tried?”
When they parted at the kerb, Brent said, “May I write to you, sometimes, and report?”
“Glad to have you﻿ ﻿… But you needn’t try to tell me what you’re doing for anybody else. That’s your affair. Write and tell me if it works﻿—but not what you did to make it work. Do you get me?﻿ ﻿… Good luck!﻿ ﻿… Goodbye!”
---
Bobby went to the Ritz-Carlton for the night, stood for a long time at the counter of the Western Union office in the lobby trying to compose a cable to Vienna, gave it up, bought a few magazines and took the elevator.
Having made himself comfortable in a dressing-gown, he drew up to his desk and wrote:
“Dear Marion: I’ve been racking my brain for a solution to your problem, but nothing comes. You have lost a friend and I see no way for you to regain her at present. I learned tonight that she is in Nice. But I don’t want you to go there seeking a reconciliation, for you wouldn’t do it without making things bad all around. You would be obliged to say that you went to Bellagio as my agent to discover what had happened to her money. I’ve arranged﻿—guided by your report﻿—for the complete untangling of her affairs. She has recovered in full. But the machinery devised to effect this restoration of her property without offence to her is frail enough. I believe and hope it will go, but it won’t stand any wrenches being tossed into it.
“The postscript to your letter broke me all up. She left Bellagio about four, you said﻿ ﻿… drenching rain﻿ ﻿… heartsick and betrayed﻿ ﻿… by you and me﻿—who would have laid down and died for her!﻿ ﻿… Stuffy little steamer to Como﻿ ﻿… Probably spent the night there﻿—or maybe caught a train over to Milan﻿ ﻿… wondering where to go﻿ ﻿… what to do next! My dear﻿—was there ever a more pitiful state of things?﻿ ﻿… Consult Jack about this. Ask him if he sees any other way out. If neither you nor he can think of a plan by which you can communicate with her without jeopardizing everything we have tried to do for her, better keep away! I’m devastated over the situation, but﻿—there you are!”
Then he wrote a letter to Helen which he had no intention of mailing, tore it into small bits, undressed, went to bed, tried to read, turned out the light, relaxed.
For a long time it had been his custom, just before dropping off, to attempt an inward look. His corridor﻿—as he called it﻿—was, of course, a mere hallucination developed and encouraged by his own quest of it. He had long since decided that the corridor was but an eccentric property of his own imagination, located somewhere in that No-Man’s-Land between fading consciousness and sleep.
It amused him to search for it, and, by practice, he had been able to arrest the clouding of his consciousness at the exact phase where his curious phantom resided.
The clearness of it depended upon his mood; and his mood﻿—in respect to the corridor﻿—was determined by the projects he happened to be working on in the field of personality projection.
Usually a very thin, faint pencil of promising yellow light streaked down the middle of the corridor’s rough flagging﻿—the flagging was always rough as if paved with cobblestones. There would be but an instant of it. The big doors would part, and the light would shine through﻿ ﻿… just enough to nourish a great hope.
Tonight﻿—perhaps because of the investment he had made, his intense concentration upon the subject he had endeavoured to make clear to Brent, and the emotional strain incident to both﻿—his mood, he found, was unusually conducive to a materialization of the corridor.
As he neared that grey twilight of consciousness, it came sharply into focus. The doors, instead of parting a little, slowly, tentatively, were opening! The corridor was flooded with a shimmering radiance.
After that, events moved with bewildering swiftness. The corridor suddenly seemed objectified﻿—a thing apart from himself﻿—and he walked into it! A terrific roar deafened him﻿ ﻿… Finding the blinding glare at the big doors too painful to face, he turned his attention to the objects against the wall, blinking in his effort to accommodate his eyes to the dazzling light.
All endeavours to recall, afterwards, exactly what he saw there were futile. They belonged to a narrowly restricted phase of half-consciousness, and were not to be reconstructed elsewhere. He was left only with a very hazy impression that he had seen his own laboratory﻿—the oven, the black switchboard, the little vice screwed to the table. His diminutive blast-furnace was at top heat. White flames jutted out about the hinges. Doubtless that accounted for the roar. There was also a nebulous recollection left that the door of the five-foot cabinet, containing all the apparatus he had been at such pains to manufacture over a period of many months, stood wide open﻿ ﻿… He had almost decided, a few days earlier, to dismantle it and have it carted off before some inquisitive colleague in the hospital discovered what an audacious thing he had had in mind, and chaffed him about﻿ ﻿…
Well﻿—be he waking or sleeping, sane or crazy﻿—there it was!
In the lowest compartment there was a box containing the vacuum tubes; *but they were not arranged in the order of the tubes in his cabinet!*
He had summoned all his efforts to concentrate on that illusory tube-box, and the exertion aroused him to full consciousness.
Tossing aside the bedclothes, he leaped out drenched with perspiration and trembling so he could barely stand. For an hour he sat at his desk, drawing diagrams of another experimental hookup of his tubes. He was unable to shake off the impression that he was on the edge of discovery. A strange sensation of exultancy possessed him.
Mechanically putting on his street clothes, he went down into the deserted lobby and sought the outer air. For miles and miles he walked, neither knowing nor caring where he went; walked with long strides, seeing nothing; utterly absorbed by the curious experience that still clung to him like a garment﻿ ﻿… When dawn broke, he found himself down at the ferry-docks.
Returning to the hotel, he bathed, breakfasted, and drove to the station. Securing a compartment, he went to bed and slept dreamlessly all day. When he awoke it was dark, and for a moment he was unable to recall where he was. Then remembrance came, and he smiled broadly. A strange sense of mastery exalted him. He laughed, and recalled Randolph. Randolph had laughed. Randolph had found the grass greener; everything tuned up to a higher key; every sensation more intense. He laughed as Randolph had laughed!
“And once I thought him crazy!”
He sat on the edge of his berth and stared hard at the shiny mahogany walls of his compartment, his eyes wide with the interest of his pleasurable self-analysis. He laughed.
“And once I thought Hudson was crazy!”
The jolting of the trucks over the rail-ends, the clank of chains, and the wail of flanges tortured by a sharp curve, stirred him out of his rhapsody. The sound of his own laughter still echoed in his brain. He rubbed his forehead roughly with the back of his wrist, and swallowed with a dry throat.
“My God!” he groaned. “I wonder if I’m going mad!”

===Chinese===

那个粉头发的姑娘——惺忪的睫毛被过量的睫毛膏粘在一起，耳朵上捂着两只难看的黑色耳套——正通知布伦特先生：一位梅里克医生在大堂，想见他……不，他没说什么事。
“布伦特先生正换晚饭的衣裳，下不来。”她一只手捂着话筒，娇滴滴地说，“他问您能不能在电话里讲讲来意。请到二号电话间。”
“告诉他我马上上去。”
她转达了；与此同时，那高个子年轻人——掷铁饼运动员的肩膀、击剑手的收腰——用纤细而不耐烦的手指，敲着话务台前高高的柜台。
“他说不方便。”她回话，明显不情愿转达这句直通通的怠慢。
“他几号房？”梅里克医生问，不动声色。
“三百七十八号；可是——他说过不见您的，您知道。”
“喂，小子，”他招呼肘边的行李员，“把这几个包存了，然后带我去三百七十八。”
布伦特先生并不是在换晚饭的衣裳。他在收拾一只大衣箱；椅子、床、桌上摊满了衣裳、文件、书、盥洗用具和皱巴巴的床单。屋里乱作一团。他开门打量来客时，一脸恶声恶气的怒容。
“梅里克——你叫？没听说过。什么事？”他把矮壮的身板往门框里一横，双手叉腰，一副挑衅的架势。
“请我进去，我就告诉你。”梅里克平平静静地说。
布伦特老大不情愿地退了一步。
“哦，行吧。”他焦躁地喝道，“可长话短说。我忙着呢——你也看见了……我以为我早捎话下来，说别拿事烦我。”
那件与他身上裤子同料的花格子西服上装，从椅背上拎起来，撂到了床上。
“坐吧——想坐就坐。”
小梅里克没理这酸溜溜的邀请，径自说明来意。
“我住在底特律，在布莱特伍德医院任职。”
布伦特那张灰白的、扎着两天胡茬的脸，又白了一层。
“是吗？”
“您也许记得，布莱特伍德医院是您堂妹的亡夫——哈德森医生——带出名的。”
“呢——那又怎样？”布伦特无礼地咆哮。
“大约三十天前，我们注意到，哈德森太太——如您所知，如今在意大利——不得已出让了她在医院的股份。”
“这关你屁事？”布伦特逼上一步，“你不过是个医生，是不是？她要卖她的医院股票，还得跟你商量？”
“一点不错，”梅里克答，决意不动气，“她无需向我交底，也确实没有。可我们关心她的福祉，我想这一点总该合您的心意吧。她的事务一直是您在经手，对不对？”
“对！而且我不需要任何人帮忙！”
“我恰好知道您需要。这正是我来要谈的。”
 “你对我堂妹哪来这么大的兴头？”布伦特讥笑，“想把手伸到她的钱上？”
那双手不安地动起来。
“我奉劝你，别把我的耐性试探得太狠，布伦特。”
“你不耐烦了，可以走！……我看你是想娶我堂妹——先得掂掂清楚，她养不养得起你！”
“眼下这个时刻，”梅里克警告道，“我们不谈哈德森太太。我们要谈你！……还有西北铜业那笔股票！”
“你什么意思，你这卑鄙的探子？”
“我的意思是：过去十二个月里，你在行情机和赛马上赔掉了十万美元开外……五月里在石油上豪赌的那最后一把——没记错吧？——把西北铜业赔了个精光！……我作为哈德森太太的朋友来这儿，就是要弄明白：这笔账，你打算怎么办。”
布伦特脸色发青。他咚咚地走到门边，一把拽开门。
“滚——你他妈滚出去！——不然我叫旅馆侦探了！”
梅里克转身走向那张乱糟糟的写字台，拿起电话。
“省您一趟腿。”他轻轻拿起听筒。
“放下！”布伦特尖叫，砰地摔上门。
梅里克一笑，照办了。
“你不急着见侦探，布伦特。可你得跟我谈！你现在就痛痛快快全招了呢，还是等我把你从那张桌子上折成两截？我做得到，你知道；而且我很乐意！”
布伦特怒不可遏，抡拳狠狠捣来。梅里克侧身让过，左手一把攥住那直哆嗦的手腕，虎钳似的。右手抄起布伦特喉咙的一大把，把他推到桌前，摁着往桌上折——往后——再往后，直到那紫胀的脖子上暴起一条条青筋，那副费劲的喘息表明：该松一松了。
“现在乐意谈生意了？”
布伦特沉重地撑起一边胳膊肘，那只手在抽屉里摸索。
“把枪放下！”梅里克往那手腕上一扣，布伦特的手指只得松开那把自动手枪。枪落在地板上。“开枪这一项，另收费。”布伦特的喉结及其周边又被攥住，直到他的呼吸变成一阵阵惨急的哨音。
梅里克俯身拾枪，退了弹，把子弹揣进口袋，等主人回魂。
过了几分钟他坐起来，拿拳头笨拙地揉着充血的眼睛，小心翼翼地摸了摸脖子。
“好吧，”他喑道，“如今你证明了你比我大个儿，你要什么？”
“我不比你大个儿，布伦特。你的毛病是闭着眼睛朝我扑。只赌一把侥幸，指望你那一拳兴许能沾着边。你这一辈子的毛病，多半全在这上头。闭上眼乱挥，指望瞎猫碰上死耗子……你那手补枪的妙着……真由着你开了枪，你这会儿可就体面了……好一个赌徒！”
“宁可赌电椅，也不蹲大狱！”
“这正好证明我说的……说到电椅和大狱——爬到你的书桌那儿，坐下。我要你给我写点东西。”
“你想逼我写自供状，梅里克，那你打错了算盘。”
“自供状？扯淡！我口袋里的证据，够送你进去二十年……我要你给你堂妹写封信。我念，你写。一个字也别写错，信我自己去寄。”
布伦特从桌上滑下来，把桌面上的乱七八糟一扫而空，拿起笔。
“先写她的地址和你惯用的称呼。接着写：鉴于您西北铜业股票的收益已无保障，我已将其脱手——”
布伦特把笔一摔，喊道：“你明白不明白，你这是在逼我写这封信？”
“哦，当然！不然要你写什么？请继续。”
 “你知道这叫什么吗？……逼我写这个？”
“叫什么？”
“这——这是——”
“等我走了你再想名目……捡起笔！……‘脱手西北铜业’那句写上了？好——接着……我已为您将等值调换成阿克西恩汽车公司优先股——”
布伦特迟疑着，莫名其妙地抬眼。
“我不懂！”
“这不打紧，”梅里克干脆地说，“其实与你无关。你此刻不过当我的文书。写完这张条子，你与你堂妹的商务就再无干系：‘阿克西恩汽车公司优先股五百股，今日牌价二百二十六。该股票由底特律第四国民银行信托部持有，今后您的股息将按期由该行汇奉。’”
“我怎么知道这不是假话？”布伦特咆哮。
“你不知道；不过——我说过——这不关你的事。写上了？好——再一段：‘我因一项紧急商务，须即刻前往布宜诺斯艾利斯——’”
“可——我不去布宜诺斯艾利斯！”
“哦，你去！……星期六启航……你这收拾行装，正是要动身去华盛顿，取我替你办好的护照。从那儿去纽约，乘‘维戈号’……接着写：‘此去不知多久；故此，我将您全部事务的责任，移交底特律第四国民银行信托部。T·P·兰道尔先生将予证实，并于短期内向您提供您事务的清晰明细……’现在，添上几句你能厚下脸皮写给一位被你掏空了的女人的客气话，签上名……再写个信封。”
他写的当儿，梅里克掏出皮夹，抽出一张船票，又数出两千块钱的大票。
“喏。”布伦特写完，他一指。“这是给你的。拿去——滚吧！你要是还想讨一句忠告：别再充好汉了，你根本不是那块料；重新搭些人脉；交几个正经朋友；找个体面的营生；振作起来——做个男人！”
布伦特脸都扭曲了，抖抖索索摆弄那沓钱，盲着眼睛伸手乱摸。梅里克没理会这个手势。他不看好这种电影桥段。
“你出得了境，对吧？……我都替你打点好了，护照不会再有麻烦——除非你还背着别的案子……确定没人拦你？……除了你那位小堂妹，没再拈别人的钱吧？”
“没有！没有！……上帝——我真是个人渣！”蒙蒂瘫在椅子里，把乱蓬蓬的头埋进臂弯。
“你不必一直是个人渣，布伦特……重新来！……走正路！……你做得到！……我走了。动身若有麻烦，给我来电。这是我的名片。还有，想必不用我说：咱们这桩小交易，哈德森太太永远不能知道一个字。”
“你是说，”布伦特茫然抬眼，“她不会知道这些钱全是你给的？那你图什么？”
“那是我的事！……不——我看还是告诉你吧……省得你心里存什么肮脏念头，以为我对她负的义务，反倒于她的名声有碍……你还记得她丈夫的死吗？”
布伦特点头。
“你可记得，哈德森医生淹死的时候，有一个年轻人被救活了？”
“记得——记得……哪个阔少爷……天哪——那人就是你？”
“是！那天救我性命的那台机器，若轮得到哈德森医生用，他兴许就缓过来了……他若活着，你也就糟蹋不了哈德森太太的钱……现在明白了吗？”
“你后来学医，也是为了这个？”布伦特瞪圆了眼。
“那倒是个足够好的理由……不过——这不打紧……我只想让你知道，我关心你堂妹的性质……你怎么看我，我他妈毫不在乎；可我巴望你把她看得清清楚楚！”
“这么说——你认定他把命还给了你——也算还了——你就得拿它干出个名堂；是这么回事吧？”
“差不多。”
布伦特直勾勾向上望了许久；再开口时，嗓音像从远处来。
“上帝！——我从没听过这种事！……而你这会儿，恰恰把命还给了我！……我若是你这种人，想必也得为此做点什么，对不对？”
“哦——那倒未必……我当年为我自己那档子事要不要有所作为，也琢磨了好久……我从前是摊烂泥，布伦特……能躲我早躲了。”
“烂泥——怎么说？……没偷过东西吧？”
“从不需要偷……什么都有……有的是过头的！……拿它带坏了多少人！……你是什么都缺。咱俩的分别，多半就在这儿。”
 “你看——我能做点什么——把你为我做的抵回去吗？”
“也许——若是你对那一路事有兴趣？”
“比如——什么？”
梅里克起身取衣。
“这得你自己想。”
“我是真想，你知道。”布伦特恳切地说。
“当真？”梅里克追问，两手按住蒙蒂的双肩，端端正正视进他眼里。
“当真——比我这辈子当过的任何真都真！”
在蒙蒂看来，他这位运动健将般的年轻恩人，被自己方才那句话驱进了深思，半天出不来。他倚着桌子站着，两手深深插在兜里，浑忘了四周。
“既然如此，”他缓缓地说，“我料想我得帮你。我不愿意！可是——偏偏有那么一线机会……听我说！你去刮脸，换上晚饭的衣裳。你下来时我在大堂。咱们吃饭——我把我知道的都告诉你。之后——就看你自己的了！……那些抓痕上点些碘酒……伤了惊，对不住。”
---
两人乘出租车进城，进了一家馆子。晚饭钟点已过，角落里那张桌子正好容他们不受打扰地说话。经劝忘掉方才两个钟头的经历，蒙蒂重拾了自信，听得入神。这块地若说不是接纳新观念的好土，那绝不是犁得不够、耙得不勤。
“我要给你讲一个怪故事，布伦特，关于一位雕塑家，兰道夫。你若觉得难以置信，我不奇怪。我头一回听，也不信。”
故事很长。一道道菜来了又撤。这一个钟头里，蒙蒂只开过一次口。
“他脑子彻底坏掉了，对吧？”
餐后酒的杯子如今推开了，雪茄点上了。
“当然，”梅里克正说着，“你若当真决定试一试这条通过向旁人投资来投射自己的路，就得备好了迎接种种失败、幻灭与恶心。你会屡屡为一个到头来既讨厌又没出息的家伙，赔上无穷的工夫和金钱。你会被利用，被骗，被造谣！你会撞上恶劣到叫你作呕的忘恩负义！可是——隔三差五，你总能把事情办成……而一旦办成，你会发现，它把你从前所有的失败都抵平了！”
他顿住，神往起来。
“我猜你正在嘀咕，”蒙蒂试探道，“时间和钱花在我身上，是不是白扔了。”
“钱那一笔，恐怕算不得投在你身上。我只想摆脱你。不过——你猜得不错，我确实在嘀咕：把人格投射这套理论向你交底，究竟值不值。”
“我能不能问一句：倘若我果真成器了，您觉得它会给您的事业添一分个人力量吗？”
“哦，毫无疑问！”
 “而——万一泄露出去——说我是您扶上道的——那您在我身上这笔投资，对您就全盘打了水漂？”
梅里克笑了，心不在焉地拨弄着烟灰缸。
“呢——不！……兰道夫有点走火入魔，你知道。谁泄露了他的投资，他就要拧断谁的脖子。那手未免下得太重……哈德森则小心嘱咐受惠人不可外传，又给他每笔投资围上一切可能的护栏，防人发现；可我不信他会觉得事情一旦漏了，就算白干……真正要紧的一层在这儿：你若成功地扩张了自己的人格，我便按我出的那份力，分到同份的回报……你在扩张自己的过程中，几乎注定会帮着另一个人也长大。那人再去激发旁人……你若懂点化学，不妨把这看作一场催化……人格投射同任何投资一样。它自己会走！它生复利。你若是那启动它的一剂，功劳便记你一份……在你被宣布死亡之后很久，它还会有一部分轰轰烈烈地跑着。实打实说：真个的你，论人格能量，在你身后五十年，可能比你在鼎盛之年看起来还要活得旺！”
“可是——您的受惠人若没能成器，辜负了您的投资呢？……”
梅里克耸耸肩。
“那种情形，你自己看得出，一无所出。”
“可是——您试过了！这里头就没有一点可满足的？”
“哦，算是练手吧，我想……可是——你若花了两万块、六个月，打了一口油井，得到的只是一个干窟窿，你回想‘不管怎样——我试过了’，能得着多少欣慰？”
路缘分手时，布伦特说：“我能不时写信向您汇报吗？”
“欢迎……但不必试图告诉我你为谁做了什么。那是你的事。写信告诉我灵不灵——别写你做了什么才灵的。听懂了吗？……祝好运！……再见！”
---
博比当夜宿在丽思卡尔顿，在大堂西联电报柜台前站了好半天，想拟一封发往维也纳的电报，终归作罢；买了几本杂志，上了电梯。
他换上晨衣，舒舒服服在书桌前坐定，写道：
“亲爱的玛丽恩：为你的难题，我把脑子都想穿了，没有出路。你丢了一位朋友，眼下我看不出你怎样能把她找回来。今晚我得知她在尼斯。但我不要你去找她讲和，因为你没法不把事情全弄糟。你将不得不说，你是作为我的代理人去的贝拉焦，去查她的钱出了什么事。我已依照你的报告，把她的事务彻底理清了。她的损失已全数收回。但为在不冒犯她的前提下完成这场物归原主而设计的这套机关，是脆弱的。我相信它、也盼它转得起来；可它经不起任何人往齿轮里扔扳手。
“你信末那段附言，把我整个人击碎了。你说她四点钟前后离开贝拉焦……倾盆大雨……心碎，遭背弃……被你和我——两个肯为她去死的人！……憋闷的小轮船去科莫……多半在那儿过的夜——兴许又赶上了去米兰的车……不知去哪儿……下一步怎么办！亲爱的——还有比这更凄惨的境地吗？……这事同杰克商量。问他看没看出别的出路。倘若你和他都想不出一个法子，既能同她联络、又不危及我们为她做的一切，那就宁可别沾边！我为这局面五内俱焚，可是——事已如此！”
然后他给海伦写了一封信——一封没打算寄的信——把它撕成碎片，脱衣上床，试着读了几行，关灯，放松。
许久以来，他有个习惯：入睡前向内看一眼。他的那条“走廊”——他这样称呼它——当然只是个幻觉，由他自己的追寻养成、喂大。他早就认定，走廊不过是他想象力的一个古怪产物，坐落在意识消散与睡眠之间的那片无人区里。
寻它，于他是一桩乐事；凭着练习，他已能把意识的转暗恰好定住，停在那个奇异的幻影栖身的相位上。
它的清晰与否，系于他的心境；而他关于走廊的心境，又系于他当下在人格投射一域操持的课题。
通常，一道极细极淡、大有指望的黄色光束，会顺着走廊粗糙的石板路——那路面永远是粗的，像鹅卵石铺的——正中划过。只有一瞬。大双开门会分开一线，光透出来……刚够养活一个大希望。
今夜——也许因为他刚做成一笔投资，因为他全力向布伦特阐明那个题目的专注，以及两件事各带着的情绪张力——他发觉，自己的心境异常地利于走廊显形。
当他临近意识的灰蒙蒙的暮色 时，它锋利地成了焦。那两扇门，不再试探地、迟缓地开一线——正敞开来！走廊灌满了粼粼的光。
此后，事态迅疾得叫人眩晕。走廊骤然像客观化了——一件脱开他自身的东西——他走了进去！一声轰鸣震得他发聋……大门口那片炫目的强光，刺得他不敢正视，他便把注意力转向墙边的物件，眨着眼，努力让眼睛适应那雪亮。
事后想精确回忆他究竟在那里看见了什么，全是徒劳。那些东西属于半意识的一个极窄相位，到别处无法重建。留给他的只有一点极模糊的印象：他看见了自己的实验室——烘炉、黑色配电盘、拧在桌上的小虎钳。他那台小小的熔炉正烧到顶温。白色的火苗顺着门缝喷吐。那轰鸣想必由此而来。还有一段朦胧的记忆：那只五英尺高的柜子，柜里装着他费了几个月心血制造的整套装置——柜门大敞着……几天前他几乎已经决定，把它拆掉运走，免得院里哪位好事的同僚发现他打的是何等狂妄的主意，拿他打趣……
呢——醒也罢睡也罢，清明也罢疯癫也罢——它就在那儿！
最底下一格里有一只装真空管的盒子；可那些管子的排列次序，与他柜里的不一样！
他拼尽全力把注意力聚在那只幻影管盒上；这番用力，把他唤回了全然的清醒。
他掀开被单，跳下床，汗透的身子抖得几乎站不住。一个钟头，他坐在书桌前，画着真空管另一套试验接法的图。他甩不掉这个感觉：他正站在发现的边上。一种奇异的昂扬攥住了他。
他机械地穿上街衣，下楼，穿过空无一人的大堂，走到外头的空气里。他走了几里又几里，既不知也不在乎走到哪里；大步流星，目中无物；整个人被那场仍粘在身上如衣裳般的奇遇吸干了……天破晓时，他发觉自己到了轮渡码头。
回到旅馆，他沐浴，用早饭，驱车去车站。包下一个包房，倒头便睡，一整日无梦。醒来天已黑，一时想不起身在何处。随后记起了，他咧嘴大笑。一种奇异的掌控之感扬着他。他笑出声，想起兰道夫。兰道夫当年笑过。兰道夫当年发觉草更绿了；一切都调高了一个调门；每一样感觉都更浓。他像兰道夫当年那样笑了！
“我还一度当他疯子！”
他坐在铺位边上，死死盯着包房亮闪闪的红木壁板，双眼圆睁，饶有兴味地自我剖析着。他又笑了。
“我还一度当哈德森疯子！”
车轮碾过轨节的颠簸、铁链的铿锵、轮缘在急弯上受刑般的尖啸，把他从忘形里搅醒。自己的笑声还在脑子里回荡。他拿手腕背狠狠揉了揉前额，咽了一口干唾沫。
“我的天！”他呻吟，“我不会是疯了吧！”

## XIV / 十四
===Original===

Young Merrick discovered, within a week, that when a man begins to suspect he is slipping mentally his disorder fattens on itself.
He became morbidly introspective, exaggerated the significance of his little tricks of manner, caught himself doing things automatically and wondered what else he might have been doing of which he had no recollection.
Then, in the course of two hours, one Friday morning, Pyle had said, “You’re not quite par these days. Something bothering you?﻿ ﻿…” Watson had said, “I’ll look after that Weber case, Merrick. She had a notion you’re too young. Silly nonsense, but we’ll have to humour her﻿ ﻿…” Nancy Ashford had said, “What is it, Bobby? Tired?”
That settled it. At noon he told Pyle he was going out to the country for a couple of weeks. He spent the afternoon gutting his little laboratory, assisted by an orderly who packed the apparatus into boxes. His first intention was to store the stuff in his suite of rooms near the hospital. On second thought, he shipped the whole of it out to Windymere. Perhaps he might amuse himself if time dragged.
Farmers along the roads near Lake Saginack grew accustomed to the sight of a tall, slender chap, in knickers and white sweater, walking rapidly on the highway; learned who he was; vainly speculated about the cause of his leisure. One tale had it that he was discharged from the hospital for drunkenness, another that he had decided to give up medicine and loaf. Meggs’ curiosity, reaching that state of compression which demanded that he either blow off or blow up, ventured to inquire of Bobby why he had come home, and was informed that his young master was recovering from “a slight touch of leprosy.”
Old Nicholas aged markedly during the first week, but made a gallant effort to disguise his worry, a well-intended deceit which added to his grandson’s anxieties. The old man’s excessive solicitude annoyed him. He reproached himself for making wild remarks for the sole purpose of seeing to what ridiculous lengths he might lead his Grandpère in assenting solemnly to his nonsense.
“Believe I could persuade him a cloud resembled a camel,” thought Bobby, “and then talk him into the notion that it looked like a hawk.”
More than two weeks had passed before he had any inclination to rig his laboratory. Somehow it seemed related to his mental dishevelment, and the thought of it had been repugnant. One morning at breakfast, he announced impulsively that he wanted the use of an attic room for a workshop.
Nicholas was delighted. Carpenters, plumbers, and electricians were in the house before noon, taking orders from a young scientist who obviously knew exactly what he wanted, surprising them with the breadth of his practical information about their trades.
That night at dinner, Bobby was more like himself than he had been at any time since his homecoming.
The farmers who lived near the highway missed him; presumed he had finished his vacation, or he had been reinstated at the hospital, or had gone “gallavanting to furrin parts.”
Old Nicholas worried more about him now than before; feared his close confinement in the attic would do him harm.
Bobby rarely came down to the first floor. Most of his meals were sent up to him, and as often as not the tray was returned almost untouched.
---
It was on Thursday about nine. The lights in the laboratory had burned all Wednesday night. Bobby was haggard and stubbly with three days’ beard. Meggs had tried the door, found it locked; had knocked and been told to go away.
Taking in his left hand the tiny knife, attached to the end of a long green cord, Bobby reached up and slowly moved the lever along the dial of the rheostat.
The little scalpel came alive!
For a long time he sat there on his laboratory stool with the dynamic thing in his hands, too deeply stirred to make a sound, trembling with ecstatic happiness.
Then he switched off the current, put down the scalpel on the bench, rose, stretched his long arms until every fibre was at top torsion, and laughed boyishly.
---
Old Nicholas was quite swept off his moorings when Bobby strode into the library, shaggy as a tramp, hollow-eyed, pasty from lack of sleep, and said he wanted to use the telephone.
“Is anything the matter, Robert?” he quavered, rising hurriedly, and taking him by the arm.
Bobby shook his head and smiled. The operator was in the process of giving him his connection.
“I want to talk to Doctor Pyle, Nancy﻿ ﻿… No! Everything’s quite all right!﻿ ﻿… Yes﻿ ﻿… That you, Doctor Pyle?﻿ ﻿… I wish you would come out here!﻿ ﻿… Yes﻿—very urgent!﻿ ﻿… That’s fine! Thanks! Bring your bag along and we’ll put you up!”
“What’s it all about, son? Feel all right?” Nicholas had dropped into a chair and his face was twitching.
“Very much all right!” shouted Bobby, patting him on the shoulder. “I’ll tell you about it after a little! I want to run up and shave first. Then I want my breakfast﻿ ﻿… Meggs!﻿ ﻿… I’ll be down again in a half-hour for a thick slice of ham, two eggs turned, a pair of flapjacks, and a pot of strong coffee.”
---
Doctor Pyle was consumed with curiosity when he arrived. Nicholas could not tell him exactly what was wanted. He was to go up to the attic when he came. Robert wished to see him there.
“You can come along, Grandpère!” called Bobby from the head of the stairs.
Nicholas trudged wheezily after Pyle, and they entered the laboratory.
“Hello, Doctor Pyle,” greeted Bobby radiantly. “Got something to show you! Wanted you to be the first to see it!”
He held up the gleaming little scalpel, dependent from yards of green-clad wiring, leading to a tall cabinet.
“Take it in your hand!﻿ ﻿… Now look!” He stepped to the switchboard and drew a lever.
“Look out!” he warned, as Pyle lifted the knife for closer inspection. “Don’t let it burn you!﻿ ﻿… Know what it is, don’t you?”
Pyle slowly nodded his head, eyes still intent upon the glowing blade.
“Humph!﻿ ﻿… Cuts and cauterizes instantly, eh?﻿ ﻿… Hummm!﻿ ﻿… Takes care of the haemorrhage as it goes, eh?﻿ ﻿… Hummm!﻿ ﻿… Well﻿—that means we’re to have some new brain surgery, doesn’t it?”
He reached out a hairy hand.
“I needn’t tell you what you’ve done, Merrick!﻿ ﻿… Thank you for letting me be the first to congratulate you!”
Then, turning to old Nicholas, who had been standing by, his face puckered with baffled curiosity, he also extended his hand to him.
“Mr. Merrick, your grandson has invented a device that will completely revolutionize brain surgery, and make a new science of it! Operations which have never yet been successfully performed will now be comparatively safe. Within the next thirty days, his name will be as familiar in the clinics of Europe as yours is among manufacturers of motor cars!”
Old Nicholas’ chin vibrated spasmodically. All he could say was “Indeed!﻿ ﻿… Indeed!”
He threw an arm around his grandson’s broad shoulders, and mumbled,
“Why, Bobby!﻿ ﻿… Indeed!”
---
Pyle could not stay the night, but consented to remain for dinner which was called earlier than usual for his convenience. When he had gone, old Nicholas and Bobby, deep in their chairs in the library, talked of the invention.
To the latter’s pleased surprise, the old man asked questions which showed with what tenacity he had retained his interest in physics; for there had been a time when Nicholas Merrick had had to know a great deal about electricity.
Bobby was so delighted with the lucidity of his grandfather’s queries and the comments, that he drew the small coffee-table between their chairs and proceeded to make a detailed diagram of his coagulation cautery, Nicholas following with keen attention.
“It was the vacuum tubes that had you stumped, eh?﻿ ﻿… And the success of that came, you say, as a sort of bolt out of the blue﻿ ﻿… How do you mean?”
“Did you ever go to bed, Grandpère, with a problem on your mind, and find in the morning that you’d worked it, somehow, in your sleep?”
Nicholas rubbed his jaw.
“I’ve heard of such things. Can’t say I ever had that experience myself﻿ ﻿… Was that what happened to you?”
Bobby pushed the table away, and shifted his chair until their knees touched.
“Grandpère,” he said, soberly, “I’m going to tell you something that you may have trouble believing. It’s a long story, and I’ll have to begin at the beginning.”
Nicholas’ contribution to the conversation, during the next hour and a half, was limited to an occasional “Indeed!﻿ ﻿… Incredible!﻿ ﻿… You don’t tell me!”
When finally Bobby had made an end of it, the old fellow sat for a long time in deep meditation.
“I had never suspected, Bobby, that you were interested in religion.”
“Not sure that I am, Grandpère.”
“But that’s what this is! You’ve been talking about this ‘Major Personality’ that supplies our personalities with added energy, as we ask for it and obey the rules for getting it﻿ ﻿… Well﻿—that’s God, isn’t it?”
“Doubtless﻿ ﻿… Just another way of saying it, maybe.”
“I’ve always shied off from the subject, Bobby. But, of late, it has been much on my mind. I’m quite disturbed, these days. I’m in a mental revolt against death. It’s sneaking up on me, and there’s nothing I can do about it. Death holds all the trump cards﻿ ﻿… It takes me a little longer to get out of bed in the morning than a month ago. It is just a bit harder to climb the stairs than it was last week. The old machine is running down. I don’t want to die. I understand that when a man actually faces up to it, nature compounds some sort of an anaesthesia which numbs his dread and makes it seem right enough; but that thought brings me small comfort. I have been accustomed to meeting all my emergencies with my eyes open, and I don’t get much consolation out of the thought that I’m to be doped into a dull apathy﻿—like a convict on the way to execution﻿—as I face this last one﻿ ﻿… I wouldn’t mind so much if there was anything﻿—after that﻿ ﻿… Bobby, do you believe in immortality?”
“I wish I was as sure of a few other things that bother me,” replied Bobby, instantly, “as I am of the survival of personality. Once you’ve experienced a vital contact with the Major Personality, Grandpère, you become aware that the power of it is quite independent of material things﻿ ﻿… To my mind, that’s clear. Personality is all that matters! The roses in that vase have no meaning for each other; no meaning for themselves. A tiger doesn’t know he is a tiger. Nothing in the world has any reality except as it is declared real by our personalities. Count personality out of the scheme, and there’s no significance left to anything! Include personality in the scheme, and the whole business is automatically explained!
“I’ve thought a good deal about the soul lately, Grandpère. It strikes me that the things one reads about souls are frightfully misleading. They inquire, ‘What are you doing to, for, and with your soul?’ as they might ask ‘When are you going to turn in your old car?’﻿ ﻿… I can’t say ‘my soul,’ as I would say ‘my hat,’ or ‘my canoe,’ or ‘my liver.’﻿ ﻿… I *am* a soul! I *have* a body! My body is wearing out, and when I can’t tinker it back into service any more, I’ll drive it out to the junk-pile; but I don’t have to be junked with it! I’m tied up to the Major Personality!﻿ ﻿… like a beam of sunshine in the sun!﻿ ﻿… I’ll not lose my power unless He loses His!﻿ ﻿… If that’s religion, Grandpère, I’m religious! But I’d rather think of it as science!”
“Bobby﻿—are you a Christian?”
“That’s what I’d like to know myself, Grandpère﻿ ﻿… For some time I have been very much absorbed by the personality of Christ. Here was the case of a man who made an absolutely ideal adjustment to his Major Personality. He professed to have no experience of fear. He believed he could have anything he wanted by asking for it﻿ ﻿… The story interests me at the point of his bland assurance that anybody else could do the same thing if he cared to. I’m amazed that more people aren’t interested in that part of it﻿ ﻿… Now﻿—if that’s being a Christian, I’m a Christian.”
“Is that what the churches teach, Bobby?”
“I’m sure I don’t know; for I never go to them. From what I gather, they approach this whole subject sentimentally. They regard the soul as a sort of congenital disease that ought to be cured. The soul has been passed along, from one common carrier to another, like a trunk with a bent lock and a broken hinge, labelled ‘Received in Bad Order.’﻿ ﻿… And as for the things you read in the papers about the churches, either they’re campaigning for money to build something, or helping to elect a new prosecuting attorney, or stopping a prizefight, or panning some other sect’s belief, or raising hell with one another inside their own bailiwick﻿ ﻿… Maybe you and I had better start a church; eh, Grandpère?”
“Very good,” approved Nicholas, grinning. “I’ll build it and you be the parson.”
“It would be just like all the rest of ’em﻿ ﻿… Nobody would want to go to the bother and expense of making his own connections with his Major Personality﻿ ﻿… He’d decide to sing about power﻿ ﻿… Fancy!﻿—singing about power! Watt didn’t sing for his! And Faraday didn’t produce the dynamo by reciting ‘I believe in Volta, Maker of the dry battery and Father of the Leyden jar, and in his successor Ampere, who codified the formulae for electrodynamics, and in Ben Franklin who went at it with a kite.’﻿ ﻿… No, sir! By the Great Horn Spoon﻿—No!﻿ ﻿… Faraday did his in an attic, alone, on an empty stomach!” He rose with a prodigious yawn and sauntered toward the door.
“I’m off to Brightwood early in the morning﻿ ﻿… Think I’d better turn in!”
Old Nicholas struggled heavily to his feet.
“Bobby, I’m not physically able to go around trying to nose out some opportunity to experiment with your theory. Keep your eyes open and let me know if there’s anything I can do. You arrange for it: I’ll furnish the money.”
“That wouldn’t get you anywhere﻿ ﻿… You can’t do this with a cheque book!﻿ ﻿… By the way﻿—did you know that old Jed Turner, up the road here, had to kill seventeen of his Holstein cows, last week? The State Vet condemned them as tuberculous﻿ ﻿… Jed’s all broken up about it.”
“I wonder if he has a telephone.”
“Oh, you can easily send for him to come over.”
Nicholas’ eyes brightened. He rubbed his hands.
“Thanks for telling me, Bobby. I’ll let you know how it comes out!”
“That you won’t! I never want to hear about it again!”
“Maybe you were thinking of doing it yourself,” said Nicholas. “If so, I’ll not get into it.”
“No, Holsteins are not in my line. That’s your job﻿ ﻿… And Grandpère﻿—while you’re over in that neighbourhood﻿—I noticed, the other day, that Jim Abbot’s ten-year-old boy is dragging a leg in a brace that didn’t look right to me﻿ ﻿… Why don’t you hop into your car tomorrow, and have Stephen drive you about through the district? You’ll be amazed what it does to you to make connections with people who need you!﻿ ﻿… Oh, I know you’ve done a lot. It was big stuff when you contributed a hundred thousand to the hospital in Axion; but you couldn’t do it without getting your name on a bronze tablet in the main hall. You drop in at Jim Abbot’s and inquire all about the boy. If they ask you to stay for noon dinner﻿—corned beef and cabbage﻿—you stay! I know you can’t eat boiled cabbage at home, because it isn’t good for you; but you’ll be able to eat it at Abbot’s, and it won’t hurt you a bit. I’ll guarantee that on my honour as a medic!”
“Run along to bed!” Nicholas slapped him vigorously on the back. “Glad we had this talk! Glad your worries are all over! Now you can be happy again!”
“I’m not looking for happiness, Grandpère﻿ ﻿… She’s out of my reach!”
“Since when was happiness a she?”
“Mine is!”
“Going to tell me about that, too?”
“Sometime, maybe﻿ ﻿… Good night, Grandpère.”

===Chinese===

不出一个礼拜，小梅里克便发觉：一个人一旦疑心自己的脑子出了毛病，这毛病便自己养肥自己。
他变得病态地内省，把那些小小的举止习惯夸张成凶兆，撞见自己在机械地做事，便疑心还有多少做过的事，他已全无记忆。
于是，某个星期五早晨，短短两个钟头里：派尔说：“你近来欠着火候。有心事？……”沃森说：“韦伯那案子我接手吧，梅里克。她嫌你太年轻。糊涂话，可咱们只好顺着她……”南希·阿什福德说：“怎么了，博比？累了？”
这就定了。中午他告诉派尔，他要下乡两个礼拜。一下午，他在勤杂工的帮助下掏空了小实验室，把装置装箱。他起初想把这些东西存到医院附近的寓所。转念一想，整批运去了温迪米尔。日子若闷，兴许可以拿来消遣。
萨吉纳克湖附近路边的庄稼人，渐渐看惯了一个穿灯笼裤、白毛衣的高挑小伙子，在公路上快步疾走；打听到他是谁；又猜不透他何以这样清闲。一种说法是他因为酗酒被医院开了，另一种说法是他决意弃医闲居。梅格斯的好奇心憋到了那个临界点——不吐就要炸——斗胆问博比为何回家；得到的答复是：少东家染过“一点轻微的麻风”。
头一个礼拜，老尼古拉斯明显见老，却英勇地遮掩他的忧心——这份好心的欺骗反倒加重了孙子的不安。老人过分的关切惹恼了他。他责备自己，曾故意说疯话，专为了看看能把爷爷郑重其事地点头附和的荒唐程度，引到多远。
“我看我能说跟他一朵云像骆驼，”博比想，“再接再厉，说跟他其实像只鹰。”
过了两个多礼拜，他才起意搭实验室。不知怎么，它仿佛同他心绪的凌乱连着，一想起就发怵。一天早饭时，他冲动地宣布：要一间顶楼房间做工场。
尼古拉斯大喜。不到中午，木匠、管子工、电工进了宅子，听一位年轻科学家调遣——他显然知道自己要什么，而且对他们各行当的门道懂得之宽，叫他们愕然。
那天晚饭桌上，博比自回宅以来头一回有了几分本色。
住公路近的庄稼人想他了；猜他假期满了，或是回了医院，或是“浪到外国地界去了”。
老尼古拉斯如今比先前更替他悬心；怕他闷死在顶楼里，熬坏了身子。
博比难得下一楼。饭大多是送上去的，而且十回有九回，托盘端下来时几乎没动。
---
那是星期四，九点来钟。实验室的灯烧了个通宵星期三。博比胡子拉碴，一脸三天没刮的憔悴。梅格斯推过门，锁着；敲了敲，被轰走了。
博比左手拈起那把小刀——拴在一根长长的绿线末端——举手向上，缓缓把变阻器度盘上的拨杆推过去。
那把小手术刀，活了！
他久久坐在实验凳上，手里捧着这有生命的东西，激动得发不出一点声音，因狂喜而发抖。
然后他切断电流，把刀搁上台面，起身，抻开两条长胳膊，抻到每一根纤维绷到极处，孩子气地笑了。
---
博比大步闯进书房时——蓬头垢面像个流浪汉，眼窝深陷，熬夜熬得一脸惨白——说要用电话，老尼古拉斯登时慌了神。
“出什么事了吗，罗伯特？”他颤声问，慌忙起身挽住他的胳膊。
博比摇头笑了。接线员正在给他接线。
“我找派尔医生说话，南希……不！一切都好！……是……是您吗，派尔医生？……我想请您到这边来一趟！……是——十万火急！……太好了！谢谢！带上您的出诊包，我们留您住！”
“这是怎么回事，孩子？没不舒服吧？”尼古拉斯跌坐进椅子，脸皮直抽。
“舒服得很！”博比大喝一声，拍着他的肩，“回头细说！我先上楼刮个脸。然后我要早饭……梅格斯！……半个钟头后我下来，要一大块火腿、两个单面煎蛋、两张热饼、一壶浓咖啡。”
---
派尔医生到时，好奇心熬得他冒烟。尼古拉斯说不清究竟要什么。只说他来了就上顶楼。罗伯特要在那儿见他。
“您也一起来，爷爷！”博比在楼梯口喊。
尼古拉斯喘吁吁地跟着派尔蹭上楼，进了实验室。
“您好，派尔医生，”博比满面春风，“有样东西给您看！要您做头一个看它的人！”
他举起那把寒光闪闪的小手术刀，垂在几码长的绿皮接线下面，线通向一座高柜。
“拿在手里！……现在看！”他走到配电盘前，拉下一根拨杆。
“当心！”派尔举刀细看时，他提醒，“别烫着！……您认得这是什么，对吧？”
派尔缓缓点头，眼睛仍钉着那发光的刀刃。
“哼！……切开即灼合，嗯？……嗯！……一路走一路把血也止了，嗯？……嗯！……好哇——这是要有新的脑外科了，是不是？”
他伸出一只毛茸茸的手。
“你干成了什么，不必我说，梅里克！……谢谢你让我做头一个道贺的人！”
随后他转向一旁的老尼古拉斯——老人立在那里，满脸被好奇心折磨出的皱纹——也向他伸出手去。
“梅里克先生，令孙发明了一件器械，它将彻底刷新脑外科，替它翻出一门新科学！从没做成过的手术，今后将相当安全。不出三十天，他的名字在欧洲的诊所里，会同您的名字在汽车制造商里一样响亮！”
老尼古拉斯的下巴痉挛地颤动。他只说得出：“当真！……当真！”
他一把搂住孙子宽阔的肩膀，喃喃道：
“哎呀，博比！……当真！”
---
派尔留宿不成，答应吃了晚饭再走；为迁就他，晚饭比平日提早了。他走后，老尼古拉斯和博比深深陷在书房的椅子里，谈这项发明。
让博比又惊又喜的是，老人问的问题，显出他对物理的兴味保有多牢——当年有一段，尼古拉斯·梅里克可是不得不精通电学的。
祖父的提问与点评那样清明，博比高兴极了，把小茶几拖到两椅之间，动手画他那台凝灼刀的详图，尼古拉斯一路紧盯不舍。
“绊住你的是真空管，嗯？……而它这一成，你说，像青天一个霹雳……怎么讲？”
“爷爷，您可曾带着一道难题上床，一早醒来，发现不知怎么的，睡梦里把它解了？”
尼古拉斯摸着下巴。
“听说过这种事。不敢说自己赶上过……你赶上的是这个？”
博比把茶几推开，挪椅子挪到两人的膝盖碰上膝盖。
“爷爷，”他郑重地说，“我要告诉您一件事，您未必肯信。说来话长，我得从头讲起。”
接下来一个半钟头，尼古拉斯对谈话的贡献，不外偶尔一声“当真！……难以置信！……竟有此事！”
博比终于讲完，老头久久坐在那里出神。
“我从没想到，博比，你对宗教有兴趣。”
“我自己都不敢说有，爷爷。”
“可这不就是宗教嘛！你讲的那位‘至大人格’，我们一求告、一守规矩，他就给我们的人格添能量……那不就是上帝吗？”
“想必是……也许只是另一种说法。”
“我一向躲着这个题目，博比。可近来，它老压在我心上。这些日子我很不安生。我在同死亡较着一股心劲。它正一步步摸上来，我一点办法没有。死亡手里全是王牌……如今我早晨下床，比一个月前多磨蹭好一阵。上楼梯也比上礼拜沉些。这部老机器在走下坡。我不想死。我听说，人真到直面它的那天，天性会配出一副麻药，麻掉恐惧，让人觉得也挺自然；这话给我的是小得可怜的安慰。我这辈子遇上急难，习惯睁着眼睛对付；如今想到要被灌成一滩麻木——像押赴刑场的囚徒——去应付这最后一桩，实在得不着多少慰藉……倘若那之后还有什么——我倒不至于这么计较……博比，你信不信灵魂不死？”
“但愿我拿得准旁的几件烦心事，”博比应声答道，“像拿得准人格不灭这样。爷爷，人一旦同至大人格起过一场活的接触，便知道那力量全然不系于物质……在我看来，这是明摆着的。要紧的只有人格！花瓶里那几朵玫瑰，彼此之间没有意义；对它们自己也没有。老虎不知道自己是老虎。世上万物，唯有经我们的人格宣布为真，才算真。把人格从这套宇宙里拿掉，一切便再无意义可言！把人格放回这套宇宙，一切便不解自明！
“近来我想了许多关于灵魂的事，爷爷。我看人们读到的那些关于灵魂的话，误人不浅。他们问：‘你对你的灵魂，做了什么、为了什么、拿它如何？’——好像问‘你那辆旧车几时折价送来？’……我说不出‘我的灵魂’，像说‘我的帽子’‘我的独木舟’‘我的肝’……我本身就是灵魂！我拥有的是一副身体！我的身体在磨损，等再也修不好使了，我就把它开去废铁堆；可我不必跟它一起报废！我拴在至大人格上！……像太阳里的一道日光！……祂不失他的力，我就不失我的！……若这就是宗教，爷爷，我有宗教！不过我宁可当它是一门科学！”
“博比——你是基督徒吗？”
“这正是我想弄明白的，爷爷……有一阵子了，基督的人格叫我入了迷。这是一个同他的至大人格达成绝对理想调和的人。他自称从无恐惧的体验。他相信凡他求的，必能得……这故事最叫我着迷的一点是：他泰然担保，谁肯学，谁也能办到。叫我惊诧的是，对这一节感兴趣的人竟这样少……呢——若这样算基督徒，我就是基督徒。”
“教会讲的是这一套吗，博比？”
“我说不好；我从不进它们的门。就我拾掇来的，他们是从感伤一路接近这个题目。他们把灵魂当作一种胎里带来的病，该治。灵魂被一程接一程地转运，像一口锁弯了、铰链断了的皮箱，挂着的标签是‘收件即残’……至于报上读到的那一桩桩：不是募款盖楼，就是帮着选个新检察官，或者拦一场拳赛，或者骂别家的教义，再不然就在自家院墙里头闹个天翻地覆……没准儿您我该自立一个教会；嗯，爷爷？”
“好得很，”尼古拉斯咧嘴赞成，“我出钱盖，你来当牧师。”
“那也同其余各家一个样……谁也不肯费那个事、花那个钱，自己去接通他的至大人格……他们会决定唱一唱力量……想想看！——唱歌颂力量！瓦特的力量不是唱来的！法拉第造出发电机，也不是靠背诵‘我信伏打，干电池的制造者、莱顿瓶之父；我信他的继任者安培，他厘定了电动力学的公式；我信本·富兰克林，他放风筝干这营生’……不，先生！指着老天爷的大勺子起誓——不！……法拉第那一件，是在一间顶楼里、独自一人、饿着肚子干出来的！”他打个惊天动地的哈欠，起身向门口踱去。
“我一早要赶去布莱特伍德……看来得睡了！”
老尼古拉斯费力地撑起身。
“博比，我这身子骨，是没本事挨家挨户去嗅你那理论的实验机会了。你替我留心，有什么我能做的，言语一声。你去安排：钱我来出。”
“那可走不通……这种事，支票本办不来！……对了——您可知道，路上头的老杰德·特纳，上礼拜不得不宰了他十七头荷斯坦奶牛？州里的兽医判了结核……杰德为这个垮了。”
“不知道他家里有没有电话。”
“哦，打发人唤他过来，还不容易。”
尼古拉斯的眼睛亮了。他搓起手来。
“多谢你告诉我，博比。办得如何，我告诉你！”
“您才不许告诉我！这事我永远不要再听！”
“没准儿你原想自己去办，”尼古拉斯说，“那样我就不插手。”
“不，荷斯坦牛不在我的行当。那是您的差事……还有，爷爷——您既要去那一片——前两天我瞧见吉姆·阿博特家十岁的男孩，拖着一条上着支具的腿，那支具瞧着不对劲……明儿您何不跳上车，让斯蒂芬拉着您在那一区转转？同需要您的人接上头，那滋味，您要大吃一惊的！……哦，我知道您做过大事。当年您捐十万给阿克西恩的医院，那是大手笔；可您办不到不把自己的名字刻上大厅的铜牌。您上吉姆·阿博特家坐坐，把孩子的事问个仔细。人家若留您吃晌午饭——腌牛肉炖白菜——您就留下！我知道您在家里吃不得煮白菜，忌口；可在阿博特家您吃得下，一点不碍事。我拿我医士的名誉担保！”
“睡觉去！”尼古拉斯在他背上重重一拍，“这场谈话痛快！你的心事全过去了，痛快！这下你又该快活了！”
“我没在找快活，爷爷……她够不着我！”
“几时起，快活成了个‘她’？”
“我的快活是！”
“这个也要讲给我听么？”
“往后吧，兴许……晚安，爷爷。”

## XIX / 十九
===Original===

Maxine Merrick, pouring their coffee in the sunny breakfast room of her over-Louised apartment in Boulevard Haussmann near the Etoile, glanced up shyly at her distinguished guest, finding it difficult to identify him as her son.
His mouth was somehow different. Ungifted with any capacity for character analysis, she was unable to define the change, but some dormant instinct told her it was other than merely functional; it was organic, structural.
It was not an austere mouth, neither was it pessimistic; but it had put off its adolescent wistfulness. It no longer entreated or anticipated, or even inquired; it accepted. The mouth had none of the tight-buckled smugness of self-imputed infallibility; none of the haughty protrusion of authority in repose; but it looked as if it were concerned only with facts, and had learned to be very particular about them.
