Their weary wives sagged in every available chair, conscious of their redundant knees, pecking at fresh marcels with nervous fingers. A few of the more intrepid attempted a languorous indifference toward their unaccustomed cigarettes which, however, they regarded gingerly and at arm’s length, as foolhardy urchins hold sputtering firecrackers﻿—nonchalantly, but with secret concern at the tail of the eye.
Joyce had impetuously taken Nancy’s arm and led the way.
“Keep close, you two,” she shrilled, over her shoulder, “and don’t get lost. We must hurry, so we will not be late for the theatre!”﻿—and had swept Nancy along into the squirming pack.
Bobby had offered his arm to Helen and she had taken it; not perfunctorily, but as if she wished to do so﻿ ﻿… She really needn’t have done it﻿ ﻿… Neither Joyce nor Nancy could observe them﻿ ﻿… She might have ignored his gesture﻿ ﻿… It was not necessary she should play a part at that moment.
They were jostled in the crowd. He had drawn her closer to him, and she had responded﻿ ﻿… No﻿—it was not merely that she had been pressed against him from without﻿ ﻿… She had responded﻿ ﻿… There was a difference. He had drawn her closer to him, and she had responded!﻿ ﻿…
He relighted his pipe, mechanically, and absently held the match until it nipped his fingers.
She had responded so generously that he could feel the warm, soft contours of her against his arm﻿ ﻿… She needn’t have done that﻿ ﻿… It was not in the book of her play﻿ ﻿… Yet, it was what she would have done if there had been no estrangement between them; or would she?﻿ ﻿… Probably not﻿ ﻿… It was hard to think straight about this affair.
Until now, no words had passed between them except their brief salutation at meeting, and Joyce’s patter had sent even that into eclipse. He felt he should be saying something to her. He despised dull commonplaces, but the silence must not be permitted to grow any longer.
Of a sudden, he had become audacious.
“I led you this way, once, through a very dark lane,” he heard himself saying.
“Oh﻿—was it?” she laughed. “I thought we went hand in hand. I felt like a little girl being led to her first day in the kindergarten.”
“So﻿—you do remember!”
“Rather! I don’t know what I should have done that night without you.” And she had looked up into his face and smiled. He wondered if she could feel the pounding of his heart﻿ ﻿… They were entering the dining-room﻿ ﻿… Joyce waved a hand from a table halfway the length of the room.
“Tell me something, while we’re thinking about that,” continued Helen, confidentially. “Why didn’t you let me drive you home, that night, or at least put you down as we passed your gate?”
“Because I preferred you shouldn’t know me. I thought that if you knew, you might﻿—” He had broken off, lamely, groping for a word.
“That was a very appropriate way to begin a friendship like ours,” she said crisply, “seeing it was destined to be full of little deceits and riddles.”
“I am sorry,” he said. He must have appeared appallingly so.
“Well﻿—don’t be, then!” she commanded hotly, with a savage little tug at his arm. “You look like Hamlet! Grin, I tell you! Your Nancy Ashford knows well enough we have been quarrelling! I saw it in her face this afternoon. I’m not going to do this farce all by myself!”
He had looked down into her big blue eyes, amazed at this outburst so startlingly out of keeping with her serene expression, and laughed aloud. As he reviewed it, now, he laughed again. It had been absurd﻿ ﻿… beyond belief!
---
“What’s the joke?” demanded Joyce, as the waiters drew their chairs.
“Long story,” said Helen briefly, “and the good man laughed at it once. I can’t have him hearing it over again.”
Nancy looked puzzled. He was secretly pleased over her bewilderment, and amused to see that the little episode had put a crimp in her omniscience.
“Your curriculum is all prescribed,” Joyce was saying, as the waiters hurried away, “all but the dessert. That’s an elective. Otherwise, you take what the institution thinks will be good for you. No, darling,” she added, turning to Helen, “it isn’t veal. I remembered that you had already worked off your credits, majoring in veal.”
“Do tell us some of your other experiences abroad!” Nancy had begged. “I’m hoping to go over, for a summer, presently, and I’m awfully keen on travel tales.”
How graciously she had complied! And how charmingly she talked of her impressions. Most of the larger cities he knew almost as he knew his own, but she had seen things that he had missed﻿—intimate glimpses up narrow streets and into quaint shops where, it seems, she had frequently made the acquaintance of a whole family﻿ ﻿… How tenderly she talked about little children!﻿ ﻿…
---
“In Assisi, I once made some wonderful friends that way, in a little shop,” she was saying. “I’m afraid I began visiting the Bordinis, at first, to improve my colloquial Italian. Of course, I always bought some trifle to pay for my tuition, or brought along something for the children; but after a while, I found myself going there because I liked them and really needed their friendship. And, one day, little Maria, about three, took dreadfully ill. For three weeks, she just hung on to the mere edge of life. They were all so terribly worried. And, not having anything very important to do, I was in and out, frequently, through those days﻿—”
She interrupted her story to unfasten from within the neck of her gown a little silver cross.
“Maria’s mother insisted on giving me this when I left Assisi.”
The trinket was passed around the table. When it came to him, he had inspected it, with a feeling of reverence. It was holy﻿—for many excellent reasons.
“I did not want to take it,” pursued Helen, “for I am sure it was the most treasured thing she owned. It had been blessed by the Holy Father himself, when, as a young girl, in nineteen hundred, she had accompanied a pilgrimage to Rome.”
“So that’s why you’re wearing that cheap little cross!” exclaimed Joyce. “Does it bring you good luck?”
Helen smiled.
“Perhaps,” she answered. “At least, I like it better than any other jewellery I have.”
“Very naturally,” commented Nancy understandingly.
Joyce was quite attentive.
“You must have been the family’s main prop, during their trouble, to earn their enchanted cross. Let’s have the rest of the story. What all did you do for them while Maria was sick? Help keep store? Were you the nurse? Go on, darling! Tell us all about it!”
At that point, he had been unable to restrain himself. Quite to his own amazement, he had held up a protecting hand.
“No, no, Joyce! We really daren’t ask Mrs. Hudson to tell us that!” Instantly he had felt embarrassed by his own remark.
“How funny! Why shouldn’t she tell us?”
He had turned to Helen, at that, and asked soberly, “Did you ever tell anybody that story?”
“No! Now that you ask﻿—I don’t believe I ever did.”
“Then I wouldn’t, if I were you. This is a very valuable little keepsake, and its chief charm is in the fact that nobody knows but you what you did to earn it.”
“How perfectly ridiculous, Bobby!” shouted Joyce. “Did you know he was so superstitious, Nancy?”
“I had suspected it﻿—a time or two.”
Helen was regarding him with perplexed, wide eyes as he put the little fetish into her palm with a gesture that had been perilously close to a caress, for his fingertips had lingered there.
“Sometimes I have thought of sending it back to her. You seem to have ideas on this subject, Doctor Merrick. Perhaps you will tell me; should I?”
“By no means. It wouldn’t be valuable to her any more if she accepted it. She really can’t take it back now, you see, because﻿—because﻿ ﻿…”
Helen’s lips were parted, and she was a bit breathless as she urged, insistently, “Yes?﻿—because﻿—because what?”
“Well﻿—because﻿—by this time she has possibly﻿—probably﻿—used it all up, herself.”
She stared at him steadily for a moment, as if she had seen a ghost. Then, half-articulately, and for his ears only, she murmured, “So﻿—that’s﻿—what﻿—that﻿—means!”
“Yes﻿—exactly! That’s what it means!”
Her eyes were misty and her fingers trembled as she refastened the little cross inside the neck of her gown.
“I’m glad you told me,” she said, under her breath. “I have so often wondered.”
---
Joyce put down both hands with a sudden gesture of impatience.
“What on earth are you people talking about!﻿ ﻿… Do you know, Nancy?”
“Oh﻿—vaguely, I think,” she replied﻿ ﻿… “You liked the little towns best, didn’t you, Helen? Let’s hear some more about them. There was Bellagio. Tell us about that.”
“Oh, do!” echoed Joyce. “You wrote such wonderful letters from there. What was the name of that little hotel﻿—on the top of the hill?”
“The Villa Serbelloni?” Helen grew moody. “Yes﻿—I quite liked it, at first; but I grew very lonely there. I became so unhappy I left, one afternoon, on a moment’s impulse﻿—in a drenching rain.”
“Why﻿—what was the matter, darling?” inquired Joyce solicitously.
“Just sheer loneliness! The season was over, really, and almost everybody had gone away. There was a young woman I had found congenial, but she turned out to be a writing person, and seeing that was what she was there for I couldn’t impose myself on her when she needed all her time for writing; so, one dreadfully lonely, stormy day, I left.”
“Ever see any of her work?” Nancy wanted to know.
Helen shook her head.
“Perhaps you should make some inquiries,” suggested Joyce. “Maybe you figured in some of her tales, yourself. Wouldn’t it be odd to pick up a story and find oneself cavorting about in it?”
During all this Bellagio talk, Helen had addressed herself chiefly to the others. As she replied to Joyce’s comment, however, she turned her eyes slowly in his direction.
“It’s quite possible I may have qualified for some minor part in a story; for I had been as garrulous as a high school girl before I discovered her occupation.”
“I am sure you were the heroine of the piece,” he had declared stoutly. “I would swear to that!”
“You seem as certain as if you really knew.” She had leaned slightly toward him. Her nearness gave him a chance to mutter, in an undertone, “I do!”
---
The talk had drifted, then, to ships. Nancy was anxious to know all about voyages; what to wear, how much and whom to tip, how long in advance one should book passage to insure good space.
“Helen had hers on a day’s notice, coming back,” remembered Joyce.
“But it’s not always that way,” Nancy argued. “I recall a quite hectic experience we had in getting accommodations for some friend of Bobby’s who was suddenly required to go to Buenos Aires.”
He had glanced apprehensively at Helen, and found her staring into his eyes, her brows knitted in perplexity. Quickly collecting herself, she said:
“Perhaps the season had something to do with the congestion. When was it?”
“When was it, Bobby?” queried Nancy. “You ought to remember. You were no end excited over getting him off by that boat. It must have been about a year ago; possibly a little earlier than this.”
“Something like that,” he had agreed disinterestedly.
The waiters had handed them menu cards. Joyce and Nancy had their heads together in consultation over parfait flavours. Helen had raised her card until it screened her face from them.
“That was very good of you,” she said softly. “I never guessed﻿—until now.”
“I didn’t intend you should. I hope you will never give it another thought. I’m sorry the matter inadvertently came up.”
Her face was studious for a moment; then brightened, suddenly, with illumination.
“Oh﻿—I see!” she murmured.
“I wonder if you do.”
She nodded her head vigorously.
“It is something like﻿—like my Bordinis﻿—and my little cross, isn’t it?”
“Yes﻿—exactly like that!”
Joyce had put an end to their cryptic byplay with a demand for light on the dessert problem﻿ ﻿… It had been a very tender moment. As he mulled it over now, analytically, it occurred to him that had he been called away, on some emergency duty, at that juncture, he might at this moment be exulting in the hope that their misunderstanding had been definitely cleared﻿ ﻿…
---
He rose and paced the room, digging his fingertips into his temples, paused at the little table, refilled his pipe, replenished the grate, and sank again into his chair. A small cathedral clock on the mantel wearily tolled the first quarter.
Those four strokes, when, on occasion, they caught his attention, invariably sent a momentary cloud drifting across his mood. It was not so at the half. The clock seemed to have cheered up, noticeably, by that time. It was almost reassuring when it came to the third quarter. But, always, that jaded, resigned, mocking, Amarish da-de-di-dum at the first quarter impressed him with the solemn asininity of whatever he happened to be doing and the futility of everything he was planning to do. It was exactly as if Eternal Destiny stretched its long arms and yawned. He could never be sure precisely what it said. Sometimes the strokes were but four gradations of an articulate sigh of inexpressible fatigue.
The vibrations still lingered. He glanced up. It was fifteen minutes past two﻿ ﻿… He resumed his reflections, moodily, realizing that his memories, from this point, would be disturbing.
---
The short trip to the nearby theatre had been uneventful, made in his own car. As it drew up at the kerb, he had heard Helen exclaim to Nancy, “What a beautiful car! What is it?”﻿ ﻿… He had not caught Nancy’s reply; but she knew.
They were very late, having lingered long at dinner﻿ ﻿… Gropingly following the usher’s little electric torch, they had dodged guiltily into their seats which fortunately were on the aisle.
A nimble chorus marched mincingly across the stage, single file, in close formation, like a garish caterpillar; coquetted, shrieked a piercing blast, broke ranks, and were joined by the male contingent which sauntered in from the wings. There was a stormy repetition of the theme song, a final deafening screech, with arms aloft, and the lights burst on as the curtain fell.
Joyce, who had insisted on leading the way, leaned to the left, across Nancy and Helen, to hand him the seat checks﻿ ﻿… How vividly every trivial incident stood out now, chiselled in high relief﻿ ﻿… He had reached for the ticket-stubs, his movement pressing him close against Helen’s bare shoulder. His hand had lightly brushed her arm. Every chance contact swept him with a suffocating surge of emotion. It was only by the sternest resolution that he resisted the urge to touch her.
He could not remember what the chatter was about at this first entr’acte. Joyce seemed to have provided most of it﻿—some amusing incident of *Jasmine*’s opening night in New York. Nancy was her best listener; Helen smilingly half-attentive, half-preoccupied.
The orchestra trailed in, twitched its E-string; the director raised both hands, swept his crew fore and aft with a final inspection; and they were off at full gallop in the descending darkness.
He had wished he was not quite so acutely conscious of her beside him, fearing she might sense the physical out-groping of himself toward her. Nancy’s experienced observation recurred to him. He had told her how keenly aware he had been of the girl in the car beside him, that night in the country. Nancy had pooh-poohed his naive notion that Helen was, of course, ignorant of his sensations.
“Nonsense!” Nancy had scoffed. “Do you imagine she could have that effect on you without sharing it?﻿ ﻿… How little you know about women!”
It was near the close of the second act that the catastrophe occurred.
He had not been following the silly, threadbare plot with enough attention to realize what, if anything, it was aiming at. His mind had been concentrated on the magnetic presence beside him, what time he was not daydreaming of the happiness he would find in surrounding her with the things she ought to have. It was not until he had irretrievably blundered that he came awake to the fact that he had unwittingly insulted her.
The dashing ingénue had returned﻿—it was a colourful scene of a country house party﻿—in a luxurious limousine. The fact that she was penniless; that the car was the property of the brazen broker who had been pursuing her throughout the play with gifts and attentions obviously to be credited on account; that the imported gown she wore was his by purchase﻿—all this was of no significance to him﻿ ﻿… At that moment, the only fact of any interest to him was the quite good-looking limousine.
Impetuously, he had turned to Helen﻿—their heads had touched, lightly, for an instant﻿—and whispered, “I heard you say you liked my new car. I’m not using it much. I’d like to lend it to you for the time you’re here.”
Perhaps, even then, the most serious blunder of all might have been avoided had she been quick with an emphatic refusal﻿ ﻿… Unsuspecting that her silence meant nothing more encouraging than that she had been stunned by his raw audacity, and heartened by that misinterpreted silence, he had groped shyly, his heart pounding, for the hand that he knew lay, palm up, very white against the black velvet.
Perhaps she had intuitively divined his intention﻿ ﻿… Perhaps the slight movement of his arm gave her warning﻿ ﻿… Or, did she choose that exact second to toy with her strand of pearls?﻿ ﻿… He would probably never know how it had happened﻿ ﻿… The warm velvet stirred uneasily under his brief caress.
The curtain was falling. The house was flooded with light. He glanced apprehensively toward her. Her cheeks were flushed, and her little fist, tightly clutching her handkerchief, was pressed hard against her lips.
---
On Joyce’s suggestion, they strolled in the lobby. On the way up the aisle, Helen had taken Nancy’s arm, and Joyce, observant, had tarried until he fell into step with her. Animatedly, she had carried the full responsibility for their desultory talk. He was glad, for his mind was in chaos.
When the signal summoned to the last act, they returned in the order in which they had made their exit, and when their seats were reached, Helen led the way in, leaving Joyce beside him.
What a beastly cad she must think him!﻿ ﻿… But﻿—surely her own good sense would tell her he had not meant it!﻿ ﻿… Not that way!
He hadn’t the faintest idea what the last act was about; sat through it suffering every imaginable torture. After a few eternities, the wretched thing was done.
Their parting was brief, conventional, without one single understanding look into each other’s eyes.
In the morning, he would find her and attempt an explanation﻿ ﻿… The clock tolled the four quarters and struck three﻿ ﻿… He had an operation to do at nine.
Wearily he flung off his clothes and went to bed. As he relaxed on his pillow, sick at heart, the chimes offered a cynical comment on his adroit handling of the evening’s complicated problem.
---
Upon his arrival at the hospital, in the morning, the desk notified him he was requested to call Mr. Randall at the Fourth National Bank, to which he paid no attention.
Having finished his operation, he called the Statler and asked for Mrs. Hudson. She had checked out.

===Chinese===

午夜过后不久，梅里克回到寓所，脱下晚宴服，套上晨衣，吩咐马楚往火里再添一块松柴，然后去睡。
这一晚的余兴，是一场狂风骤雨的交响曲，什么调门什么拍子都齐了：几小节说不出的温柔，几小节短暂的指望，大段大段枯燥的转调与铺垫，间或爆出几声惊心的渐强，沿着半音阶尖利地扶摇直上，载满摇摇欲坠的焦虑。可惜终曲收在一个极不安定的减和弦上……总观这一晚，它的心绪起落无常，赛过西贝柳斯的《忧伤圆舞曲》。
他本想带着运动员上场角逐的那股洒脱来赴它。他亲爱的对手许诺过：谁也——连他也在内——觉察不出她的恼怒与怨气；他知道她说到做到。她的戏难演，他的更难。她只需演出热络。但凡有点社交历练的人，谁没学过：陪着不喜欢的人周旋时，怎么装出一团和气？至于他，他抽到的角色要求一种冷静的随意；不是割舍者的僵硬克制，也不装苦行僧的无动于衷，而是一个男人对一个仅识一面女子的从容礼节。要演得叫人信服——顶着这颗烈焰烧灼的心——这便是他的课题。
下山进城之前，他在穿衣镜前匆匆自照，郑重地向镜中人立约：今夜对这位女士，他必持庄重的骑士风度——她那应许下来的、为时一晚的友情假扮，多半近于苦刑。他差一点——就几乎英勇地——撑到了底。
然而，尽管他痛悔那一次短暂却彻底的失守——它使两人的关系雪上加霜——那几分钟后销魂的时刻，仍叫他彻骨酥麻：她分明只是在履约，可那份情谊，显得那样真。
他怀着守财奴的心境，急着独享他的黄金，不耐烦地打发走恋恋不舍的马楚，点起烟斗，陷进噼啪作响的炉火前那张深椅，决意把这一晚一桩桩重过一遍，掬回其中最惊心的悸动。
---
奉乔伊斯的吩咐，他先去接南希·阿什福德。一身绛红，雍容夺目；年轻的脸，雪亮的白发，曼妙的身段，轻捷的步子——南希当得起他全部的骄傲。他这么告诉她；她道了谢——为这句，也为那束花。
什么也逃不过她的眼睛。他扶她进轿车时，她说，理查德换了新帽子和绑腿。
“您从前没叫他穿过制服吧？”她问，“我记得您在这个问题上，可是有些民主见解的。”
“见解是有的，”他认了，“可我改了主意。他一穿上制服，就是这机构的一部分，免得他惹事。至少，理论如此。再说，他自己喜欢。”
这一路尽是些漫无边际的闲话，他也答得分外起劲；可南希很快从这场琐碎话的弹雨底下溜出来，一心要讨个明白。
“博比，”他那辆漂亮的新车汇入大道车流、迈开大步时，她说，“有什么告诉我：今晚这一场，怕是相当难熬。”
“您从来眼尖，南希。”他认了。
“用不着特异功能也看得出来：今天下午布莱特伍德的空气，带足了电。”
“打岔一下——您穿这个颜色好看极了。今晚您真美，亲爱的。”
“意思是：这事您不想讲给我听？”
“呢——也许……差不多吧。”
“很好。那我就闭上眼、堵上耳、封上嘴。我做那三只智慧的猴子。我假装不知道你们俩吵过架。”
“真乖！”
“我还假装不知道：你们这俩傻东西爱得这么深，深到不敢对视，生怕露了底。”
“我还是觉得您美，南希。”
“可惜是个哑巴！”
“不，不！不是哑巴！……正相反，叫人急得要命！”
听了这个，她饶了他，捏捏他的手，唤他好孩子；余下的一路，便纯然公事公办地谈院务。
---
在布克-凯迪拉克饭店门前，他们一脚跨进一场大片大雪，匆匆钻进旋转门。乔伊斯和海伦按约在夹层候着；乔伊斯几乎闹过头地欢快，一身绿塔夫绸的甜俏玩意儿窸窣生姿，胸前佩着他送的花束；而且，若他没大看错，在他的兰花底下，她还掖了一两杯鸡尾酒——她那金丝雀式的手势里绷着一股紧张的神经，嗓音里多出一股尖细的泛音，不灌下大约三杯杜松子酒是浇不出来的……海伦若看出了，也显然决意视而不见。既然这是个成批无视不快事实的场合，她大约也没理由单挑这一件来挑眼……她多么可爱……这一身黑丝绒……这串珍珠……还有他的兰花。她竟真的肯戴了！……敬爱的对头，失一分！
---
说来也怪，乔伊斯从第一刻起，就存心把他俩往一块儿撞，撞得几乎暴力……兴许是杜松子酒……兴许是她的本能告诉她，两人之间有一根没说出口的线，理当由她来把它挑明……猜乔伊斯在想什么、怎么想、甚至想不想，从来都是难题……但不管她动机何在——若有动机——她毫不掩饰她的意图：要让这一小席，成为他俩初萌的交情飞速发展的契机……仿佛那是一朵蘑菇，现在不熟，就永不熟了。
的确，她干得全无怜悯。吃沙拉时，她趁着谈话一时的空隙，拿腔拿调地自言自语：“哈——德森太太！……梅——里克医生！……天哪！”——耸耸肩，叹口气——“我还指望到这会儿，他们已经改口叫博比和海伦了呢！”海伦闻声朝她倾过身去，用抱歉的、母亲式的低声答：“把你的牛奶喝了，小宝贝。乖！”……两人都笑了；这一乐，性质近于喝彩。
---
旅馆里挤得透不过气。正赶上某大型行业年会，公共休息厅和门厅里人满为患，全是大惊小怪的胖子，翻领上别着蓝金色的长条徽章，脑门上沁着汗珠。几十个在人堆里打转，各奔各的方向，横着身子在人流里蹭，左一声右一声“借光”，那口气便是“让开！”他们紧锁的眉头作保：这一挤若挤不过去，这一番辛苦这一番花费，全都要打水漂。
他们疲惫的太太们，塌在每一把坐得上的椅子里，自觉膝盖多余额外，紧张的手指拨弄着新烫的卷发。几个胆大的，摆出慵懒的世故，对付那支不习惯的香烟——却终究小心翼翼，隔着一臂之遥端详它，像莽撞的顽童捏着咝咝作响的爆竹——满面无所谓，眼角却暗暗提心吊胆。
乔伊斯一时兴起，挽起南希的胳膊，领头开路。
“跟紧了，你们俩，”她回头尖声喊，“别走丢了。得赶紧，别误了戏！”——说着把南希裹进了蠕动的人团。
博比把胳膊递给海伦，她挽住了；不是敷衍的一挽，倒像她心甘情愿……她本不必的……乔伊斯和南希都望不见他们……她本可以不理会这个手势……那一刻，她原不必演戏。
两人在人群里被挤。他把她往自己身边拢了拢，她响应了……不——绝不止是外力把她压向他……她是响应了……这中间有分别。他把她往身边拢了拢，她响应了！……
他机械地点了点烟斗，火柴举着忘了扔，直到火苗咬了手指。
她响应得那样大方，他连她贴着他手臂的温软轮廓都觉了出来……她本不必那样的……她的戏本子里没有这一条……可这，恰恰是假如两人之间没有那道隔阂，她便会做的样子；抑或，不会？……多半不会……这件事，理直了想，难。
到此刻为止，两人除见面时那一声简短的招呼，再没交一语；乔伊斯的呱啦，连那声招呼也淹了。他觉得该对她说点什么。他鄙夷干巴巴的客套，可这沉默再不许它长了。
一时冲动，他放开了胆。
“我这样领过您一回路，穿过一条很黑的小巷。”他听见自己说。
“哦——是吗？”她笑，“我记得咱们是手拉着手的。我觉得自己像个小女孩，头一天上幼儿园，被人领着。”
“这么说——您记得！”
“记得很清楚！那晚没有您，我真不知怎么办。”说着她仰起脸望他，笑了。他不知她可觉出他胸腔里擂鼓……他们正进餐厅……乔伊斯在半道上的一张桌子边挥手。
“趁记着，再告诉我一件事，”海伦推心置腹地接下去，“那晚您为什么不肯让我送您回家？哪怕路过您家门口，让我放您下来也好？”
“因为我不愿您认出我。我想，您若认出来，兴许会——”他讷讷地收住，找不着词。
“像咱们这样的交情，这开头倒也贴切，”她干脆地说，“反正注定要充满小小的欺瞒和谜语。”
“对不起。”他说。他那副样子一定懊丧得吓人。
“那——就别对不起！”她发着狠，在他胳膊上狠狠拽了一把，“您活像哈姆雷特！笑，我说！您的南希·阿什福德早看透咱们吵架了！今天下午我从她脸上瞧见的。这出闹剧我可不一个人唱！”
他低头望着她那双湛蓝的大眼，惊异于这场爆发同她平静的神情全不相衬，放声笑了。此刻回想，他又笑了。荒唐……荒唐得离了谱！
---
“笑什么？”侍者拉椅落座时，乔伊斯追问。
“说来话长，”海伦简短地说，“而且这位好人已经笑过一回了。不能让他再听第二遍。”
南希一脸迷惑。她的迷惑使他暗暗得意；又乐见这一小插曲，在她的一贯全知上掐了个折。
“你们的课全是指定的，”侍者们匆匆退下后，乔伊斯正说，“只有甜食是选修。其余的，人家给什么你受什么。不，亲爱的，”她转向海伦补一句，“不是小牛肉。我记得您主修小牛肉，学分早修满了。”
“再给我们讲讲您在国外的别的经历吧！”南希恳求，“我盼着哪天去消一个夏，最爱听游记。”
她讲得多么雍容！谈她的观感，又多么动人！那些大城，他多半也熟得像自家门口；可她见了他错过的东西——窄街深处的一瞥，古怪小铺的一堂，在那儿，看来她常同人家一家子结成朋友……她谈起小孩子，多么温存！……
---
“在阿西西，我就这样交过一回极好的朋友，在一家小铺子里。”她正说着，“起初我去博迪尼家串门，恐怕是为练我的意大利会话。当然，我每回总买点小东西抵学费，或者给孩子们捎点什么；可过了些时候，我发觉我去，纯粹因为喜欢他们，当真需要他们的友情。后来有一天，小玛丽亚——三岁上下——病得凶险。整整三个礼拜，她吊着命的边儿。一家人急得不行。我呢，手头没什么要紧事，那几日便常进常出——”
她中断了故事，从礼服领口里解下一只小小的银十字。
“我离开阿西西时，玛丽亚的母亲执意把这个给了我。”
这小玩艺儿在桌上绕了一圈。传到他手里时，他怀着敬意细看。它是圣洁的——有许多极好的理由。
“我本不想收，”海伦接着说，“因为我确信，这是她最心爱的物件。一九零零年，她还是个姑娘，随朝圣团去罗马，教皇亲自给它祝过圣。”
“原来您戴这便宜的小十字，为的是这个！”乔伊斯喊，“它给您带来好运吗？”
海伦微笑。
“也许，”她答，“至少，我喜爱它胜过我其余所有的首饰。”
“那太自然了。”南希体谅地评论。
乔伊斯听得专心。
“他们遭难那阵，您准是这家的顶梁柱，才挣来这有魔力的十字。把故事讲完。玛丽亚生病时，您全为他们做了些什么？帮着看铺？当护士？讲下去，亲爱的！全讲给我们听！”
说到这里，他再也拴不住自己。大出他本人的意外，他举起一只护卫的手。
“不，不，乔伊斯！咱们真不敢请哈德森太太讲那个！”话一出口，他立刻为自己这句话窘住了。
“多可笑！为什么不能讲？”
他当即转向海伦，正色问：“这个故事，您对人讲过吗？”
“没有！您既问起——我相信从没讲过。”
“那么我要是您，就不讲。这是件非常珍贵的纪念品；它最要紧的魔力，正在于：您为挣得它所做的事，天底下只有您一个人知道。”
“荒唐透顶，博比！”乔伊斯嚷，“南希，你知道他这么迷信吗？”
“我疑心过——一两回。”
海伦睁大迷惑的眼睛望着他；他把那小小的灵符放回她掌心，那姿势险些就是爱抚——他的指尖在那一带流连了。
“我几回想把它寄还给她。这方面您似乎有见解，梅里克医生。您肯告诉我：该吗？”
“万万不该。她若收下，它便不再珍贵。您看，她如今实在收不回了，因为——因为……”
海伦唇微启，微微喘着，紧追不舍：“嗯？——因为——因为什么？”
“呢——因为——到如今，她多半——多半——自己把它用得精光了。”
她定定望了他一瞬，像见了鬼。然后，半出声地，只说给他一人：“原来——那句话——是这个——意思！”
“是——正是！就是这个意思！”
她两眼蒙雾，指尖发颤，把小十字重新系回礼服领口里。
“幸亏您告诉我了。”她低声说，“我纳闷过多少回。”
---
乔伊斯忽然不耐烦地把两只手往桌上一放。
“你们这些人到底在讲什么！……南希，你知道吗？”
“哦——依稀知道一点吧。”她答……“你最喜欢那些小城，是不是，海伦？再讲讲。贝拉焦怎么样。讲讲那个。”
“哦，讲！”乔伊斯帮腔，“你从那儿寄来的信多精彩。山顶上那家小旅馆，叫什么来着？”
“塞尔贝洛尼别墅？”海伦的情绪沉了下来。“是——起先我挺喜欢；后来寂寞得厉害。我难过得受不了，一个下午，一时冲动就走了——冒着瓢泼大雨。”
“哎呀——怎么了，亲爱的？”乔伊斯关切地问。
“就是寂寞！季节本来就完了，人几乎走空。有位年轻太太跟我投契，后来发现她是个写字的；她既是为写字去的，人家写作要工夫，我总不好赖着打扰她；于是，一个寂寞透顶的暴风雨天，我走了。”
“见过她写的东西吗？”南希想知道。
海伦摇头。
“你该打听打听，”乔伊斯出主意，“没准儿你自己就进了她的小说呢。拿起一本故事，发现里头有你本人蹦得正欢，那多有趣！”
贝拉焦这一席话，海伦多半是对着旁人说的。可回答乔伊斯那句时，她的眼睛慢慢转向了他这边。
“没等我发现她的职业，我已经说得跟个中学生一样多了；所以我进哪本故事、当个什么小角色，倒真有可能。”
“我担保您是那一篇的女主角。”他斩钉截铁地宣告，“我敢起誓！”
“您说得这么笃定，倒像真知道似的。”她朝他微微倾了倾身。这份贴近，给了他一个机会，他低声咕哝：“我是知道！”
---
话头随后漂到了船上。南希急想知道关于航行的种种：穿什么，小费给多少、给谁，要保好舱位得提前多少日子订。
“海伦回程那张票，是一天之内弄的。”乔伊斯记得。
“可未必回回这样，”南希反驳，“我记得有一回，为博比的一位朋友忽然要去布宜诺斯艾利斯，订舱订得好一场忙乱。”
他惶惶瞟了海伦一眼；她正盯着他的眼睛，眉头紧锁。她飞快定住神，说：
“兴许是旺季，舱位挤。那是什么时候？”
“什么时候，博比？”南希追问，“你该记得。为了送他上那条船，你张罗得不可开交。总有一年了；兴许比这稍早。”
“差不多吧。”他淡淡地附和。
侍者递上甜品单。乔伊斯和南希头挨头商量巴菲的口味。海伦把卡片抬起来，正好挡住自己的脸。
“您心肠真好。”她轻声说，“我从没猜到——直到刚才。”
“我本不打算让您猜到。望您永不再想它。抱歉，这桩事无意间冒了出来。”
她的脸凝重了片刻；随即忽然一亮。
“哦——我明白了！”她低语。
“不知您是不是真明白。”
她用力点头。
“它有点像——像我的博迪尼一家——和我的小十字，对不对？”
“对——一模一样！”
乔伊斯一声令下，终结了他俩这段哑谜，逼着大家给甜食问题拿主意……那曾是一个极温柔的时刻。此刻他冷静地反刍，忽然想到：倘若那一关口他被急诊唤走，此刻他多半正欢天喜地，以为两人的误会确凿地冰释了……
---
他起身踱步，指尖抠着太阳穴，在小桌前驻足，重新装斗，给壁炉添了柴，又陷回椅中。壁炉架上一座小教堂钟，倦倦地敲了一刻。
这四响，偶尔引人注意时，总在他心头掠过一小片阴云。敲半点时便不这样：到了半点，那钟像是缓过劲来了，明晃晃的。敲三刻时，几乎令人心安。可第一刻那声疲惫的、认命的、讥诮的、阿马里式的“嗒-得-嘀-咚”，永远向他昭示：他手头无论做什么，都愚蠢得庄严；他打算做的一切，都属徒劳。恰似永恒的命运伸长了胳膊，打了个哈欠。他从来拿不准它究竟说了什么。有时那四响，不过是一声说不出的疲惫的叹息，分出四个层次。
钟的震颤还悬在空气里。他抬眼。两点一刻……他郁郁地续上回想，知道从这一点起，记忆要折磨人了。
---
去附近那家戏院的一小段路平平常常，坐他自己的车。车靠路缘时，他听见海伦对南希赞叹：“多漂亮的车！这是什么牌子？”……南希答了什么他没听清；可她知道。
他们到得极晚，晚饭磨蹭了太久……跟着领座员那支小电筒一路摸索，愧疚地缩进座位——幸而靠走道。
一支轻捷的合唱队碎步横过舞台，一路纵队，队形紧凑，像一条花哨的毛毛虫；卖弄一阵，尖啸一声，散了队形，同从侧翼晃出来的男班合流。主题歌风暴般地重演一遍，末了一声震耳的尖叫，双臂高举，大幕落下，灯光骤亮。
执意领头的乔伊斯向左倾过身，隔着南希和海伦，把座号牌递给他……此刻，每一桩琐事都刻得棱角分明，高高地凸着……他伸手接票根，这一动把他紧紧压上海伦裸露的肩。他的手轻轻擦过她的手臂。每一次偶然的接触，都把他卷进一阵窒息的情绪。全凭最狠的自持，他才按住了碰她的冲动。
第一次幕间休息时谈的什么，他记不起了。多半是乔伊斯包办的——《茉莉》纽约首演之夜的一桩趣闻。南希是她最好的听众；海伦笑着，一半在听，一半出神。
乐队零零落落进场，拨了一下 E 弦；指挥双手一举，前后一扫，作最后的检阅；灯光沉下去，他们全速飞奔。
他但愿自己不至于这样尖锐地意识到身边的她，生怕她觉出他的身体朝她那边探。南希那番老练的议论又浮上来。他讲过：那晚乡路上，他对身边车座上的姑娘，意识得何等尖锐。他当时天真地以为海伦对他的感觉浑然不觉，被南希嗤之以鼻。
“胡说！”南希当年哂笑，“你想，她能在你身上起这等作用，而自己分毫无感？……你多不懂女人！”
灾祸降临，是在第二幕将近结尾处。
那出愚蠢陈旧的戏，他并没怎么跟，不清楚它到底想干什么。他的心思全在身侧那团磁力上；没出神畅想“把她该有的种种围着她、他便能寻得的幸福”时，全在那上头。直到大错铸成，他才醒悟：他已经无心得罪了她。
那位俏丽的少女主角回来了——正是乡间宅会的一场热闹戏——坐着一辆华贵的轿车。她身无分文；车是那个厚脸皮的掮客的——整出戏里他捧着礼物和殷勤追她，显然全记账上；她那身进口礼服，也是他掏钱买的——这一切，于他毫无意义……那一刻，他唯一有兴趣的事实，是那辆相当气派的轿车。
他一时冲动，转向海伦——两人的头轻轻碰了一瞬——低声说：“我听您说喜欢我这辆新车。我用得不多。您在这儿的日子，我想借给您用。”
倘若她当时快口给一个斩钉截铁的回绝，兴许连最重的那记失着也躲得过……他没疑心她的沉默，只当她被这生猛的唐突震住了；这份被错解的沉默又给他壮了胆，他心跳如鼓，怯生生地伸手，去够他知道正摊放着的那只手——掌心向上，衬着黑丝绒，很白。
兴许她凭直觉窥破了他的意图……兴许他胳膊那一点轻动报了信……又或者，她偏偏挑了那一秒去拨弄她的珍珠链？……事情究竟怎么发生的，他大概永远无从知晓……温软的丝绒在他短短一抚之下不安地动了。
幕正落下。满场灯亮。他惶惶侧顾。她双颊绯红，攥紧手帕的小拳头，死死抵在唇上。
---
依乔伊斯的提议，四人在门厅散步。上走道的一路，海伦挽住了南希；乔伊斯眼尖，磨蹭着等他同自己并了肩。这一路漫无边际的谈话，全凭她一人撑着生气。他求之不得，他脑子里一锅粥。
铃召最后一幕，四人按出场时的次序折回；到得座前，海伦领头进，把乔伊斯留在了他身边。
她一定把他当成多下作的无赖了！……可是——凭她的明白，她一定知道他不是那个意思！……不是那样！
最后一幕演的什么，他一无所知；一坐到底，受尽了想得出的各样酷刑。熬过几个永恒，那劳什子总算完了。
道别短促而客套，两人没有交换过一丝心照的眼神。
明早，他去找她，试着解释……钟敲了四刻，又报三时……九点他有一台手术。
他倦倦地甩掉衣裳，上了床。心口发沉地陷进枕头时，壁炉的钟声对今晚这道难题被他如何“漂亮”地料理，发表了一通冷嘲。
---
早晨到了医院，服务台通知他，第四国民银行的兰道尔先生来电话请回——他没理会。
做完手术，他挂电话到斯塔特勒饭店，找哈德森太太。她已经退房走了。

## XVIII / 十八
===Original===

The Bruce McLarens were entertaining Dr. Robert Merrick at luncheon in their cosily furnished apartment. It was Sunday, and the three of them were just back from Grace Church where the appearance of the distinguished young surgeon in the minister’s pew, in company with Mrs. McLaren, had excited a genial buzz of pride and satisfaction.
Bobby Merrick’s recent spectacular contribution to the cause of brain surgery had been made much of by the press, somewhat to his own dismay; for he had the honest scientific worker’s shyness of publicity. It had been quite embarrassing to see his invention described in the argot of journalistic ballyhoo, and he was not nearly so grateful as he would have liked to be for the well-intended eulogies on the editorial pages of the dailies, and the sentimental twaddle which embellished his biography in the digests and reviews.
Of course, it really had been a corking story, well worth its two-column head on page one. The scribes had left nothing out. Young Doctor Merrick’s utter abandonment of the leisure to which he had access by virtue of his large fortune, to give himself tirelessly to the most difficult and discouraging speciality known to surgery, was played up for all that the traffic would bear. Had he not promptly barricaded himself against the fleet of feature writers who bore down upon him, the matter would unquestionably have been worse.
“You really owe something to your public!” one of them had twittered, as if she were talking to some movie-struck flapper who had won *The Times*’ beauty contest.
It was even recalled that Doctor Merrick’s life had been saved, some years ago, at the same hour when another eminent brain surgeon, the late Dr. Wayne Hudson, had drowned in Lake Saginack. One paper (pink) had broadly wondered if the wealthy young Merrick’s immediate decision to enter a medical school where he trained to espouse brain surgery might have been influenced, if not indeed directly caused, by that tragedy; but, lacking the details, and unable to twist them out of the unhappy hero or his associates, it had been content to toss out the hint and let the public draw its own conclusions.
Within eighteen hours after the news broke, Bobby had decided that if the liabilities of front page publicity were pitted against the assets accruing therefrom, his account with Fame was already in the red. It was obvious that a new star was better off for a low visibility. His mail was crammed with importunities from every known species of beggar; appeals from alleged philanthropies ranging all the way from foundations guaranteeing international understanding to wildcat altruistics for the relief of hectored blue-jays. He was the recipient of home-brewed poetry extolling his merits, slobbering songs hymning his praise and hopeful of publication at his expense, saccharine love letters, many of them enclosing photographs. He was besieged for luncheon talks. He became a fugitive, darting from cover to cover.
Even out at Windymere, where he sought seclusion for a weekend, shortly after the persecution set in, he was exasperated to find his grandfather proudly and﻿—for him﻿—garrulously accommodating a severely tailored young woman who required some intimate knowledge of Bobby’s boyhood to adorn a magazine story.
“Ah﻿—Robert﻿—surprised to see you!” exclaimed old Nicholas. “We were just speaking of you. This young lady﻿ ﻿…”
“Yes, I see,” Bobby had responded icily. “I dare say she will pardon us if we change the subject.”
“That I will not!” giggled the visitor.
Nicholas had looked very foolish and helpless over the situation until Bobby came to his relief by summoning Meggs.
“Tell Stephen to drive this lady down to the station, Meggs. She is anxious to make the 4:16.”
---
As for his colleagues in the profession, their gratitude and generous felicitations had been a source of much pleasure. Every day brought dignified encomiums from well-known men of his own speciality, thanking him for the unselfish manner in which he had promptly made his find available to his fellows. He had had letters from every civilized country of the world.
Now that sufficient time had elapsed for his sudden fame to jellify, Bobby had shyly crept out into the open to resume his normal schedule of activities and recreations. He had not yet become accustomed to the stares, whispers, and nudges, which singled him out in public places; but, seeing he couldn’t sneak about forever like a hunted thing, he masked his self-consciousness the best he could and took his punishment with an assumption of nonchalance. Today he had even risked going to church.
---
Doctor McLaren had preached a scholarly sermon to a large audience of good-looking people﻿—fully half of whom were under forty﻿—on a topic he hoped would be of special interest to his important guest.
Indeed, what Dr. Robert Merrick was going to think of that sermon had loomed so large in the popular young preacher’s mind, while preparing his discourse, that it was with much difficulty he had restrained himself from the use of a scientific phraseology quite beyond the ken of his customers﻿—albeit, as church audiences went, they intellectually registered A-plus; and freely admitted it. Grace Church was quite conscious of its modernity.
“Really, the most forward-looking﻿—indeed the only forward-looking church in town!”﻿—Mrs. Sealback was remembered to have said in prefacing her suggestion of Doctor McLaren as the proper person to invoke the divine blessing on that session of the Social Congress which had programmed a discussion of Birth Control.
“As to what?” President-of-the-Social-Congress Mrs. Cordelia Kunz of Grand Rapids had inquired drily, tapping her notes with a cunning little lorgnette. “Forward-looking on economic questions, social problems, political issues﻿—or merely posing as the last outpost of orthodoxy?”
Mrs. Sealback, slightly dizzied and not a little nettled, had replied that she was sure she didn’t know exactly how far or in what direction Grace Church led the way to freedom﻿—and snapped her purse several times, quite noisily, to emphasize her disclaimer of further interest in the matter﻿—unaware that the brusque gavel-swinger from up state had indeed touched a mighty live wire.
---
Obedient to the necessary precautions, however, Doctor McLaren had made a few last-minute substitutions for certain erudite terms he feared might overshoot his congregation; but, even with these begrudged alterations in the cause of clarity, the address was as one scientist to another, and the people who heard it were at once flattered and befuddled by its charming inexplicability. They, too, wondered what Dr. Robert Merrick thought of it, and were glowingly proud of their wise young pastor.
And they had every right to be proud. The Rev. Bruce McLaren, Ph. D., was by no means an intellectual coxcomb or a solemn blatherskite with a fondness for big words and an itch to achieve the reputation of a savant. His scholarship was sound, and the sermon that morning was a credit to it.
Deacon Chester, warmly gripping his pastor’s hand, shouted above the shrill confusion of the metal-piped postlude that he guessed it was the most profound sermon ever delivered in Grace Church! The statement was entirely correct; nor was the word “guess” used in this connection a mere colloquialism. Had Mr. Chester been a painstaking stylist﻿—he was a prosperous baker of cookies by the carload lot, and not averse to admitting that he had left school at thirteen﻿—he could not have chosen a word more meticulously adequate than “guess” to connote his own capacity to appraise the scholarship disclosed by that homily. Had a photographic plate been exposed to Mr. Chester’s knowledge of the subject which Doctor McLaren had treated, it could have been used again, quite unimpaired, for other purposes.
---
The warm friendship which had arisen between Doctor McLaren and Doctor Merrick dated from a raw March evening when the rangy, bronze-haired preacher had been brought into Brightwood, unconscious and breathing stertorously, with an ugly and dangerous smash in the right squamous temporal. He was muddy, bloody, and limp. It had looked black for him, that night; and the only recess Doctor Merrick had permitted himself, from the time he finished the necessary repairs at nine until the next morning at seven, were brief pacings up and down the corridor in front of his important patient’s door, tugging nervously at cigarettes, and disinterestedly accepting the sandwiches and milk brought up by a nurse at three.
Bobby Merrick had liked McLaren from the first moment; liked the length and strength of him as he lay on the operating table, subconsciously making his good fight for life; liked the shape of his broad forehead, the cut of his ears, the cleft of his chin, the hardness of his well-tennised right forearm, the texture of his hide, the elliptical curve of his thumb. All these things were significant. Had Doctor Merrick been required, he could have written a two thousand word paper on the character of Doctor McLaren before ever he had heard him speak.
He had liked his patient, next day, for the inherent poise he exhibited when, rousing for the first time to a vague consciousness of his surroundings, he had taken in the situation at a glance, and, apparently considering it as all in the day’s work, had dropped off to sleep, at the nurse’s suggestion, without bothering to ask questions. Bobby had liked him even more, a few days later, for his quite superior capacity to take his punishment﻿—and there was a lot of it﻿—without flinching or grousing. And, finally, he had liked Doctor McLaren’s state of mind when, a week after the accident, he had spoken calmly and without rancour of the drunken insolvent who had run him down in a safety zone.
“He probably feels bad enough about it,” remarked McLaren, in his deep bass, mellowed by an ancestral Scotch burr. “Anyway, I’m not going to press the matter, or make myself wretched by mulling it over.”
“That’s amazingly good sense!” Merrick had commented, privately resolving to see more of this man when he was up and had his boots on. He had never known a preacher before. His rather nebulous opinion of the clergy had been collected from cartoons, the quips and jibes of paragraphers, and satirical sideswipes at the profession roughly projected from the stage and screen. He had lately thumbed, scowlingly, a nasty novel vilifying men of this vocation. He was not conscious of an active dislike for them, but shared what seemed to be the unchurched public’s general belief that preachers were﻿—to put the matter laconically﻿—a bunch of saps.
Every day, the young surgeon found himself more and more pleasantly attracted by his patient, enjoyed his droll comments, offered in moments when drollery came high, admired the adroitness with which he parried the friendly raillery of doctors and nurses﻿—chaff invited by his own whimsical humour. Almost everybody in the organization at Brightwood was in to see him, at one time or another, during his convalescence; and it was quite unanimously held that he was a good sport.
And no less cordially interested had been the Brightwood household in the brown-eyed, deep-dimpled Mrs. McLaren who had appeared, anguished but admirably controlled, a half hour after her husband’s arrival. They had telephoned her that Doctor McLaren was seriously injured, suggesting that she come at once. When she came, there was no hysteria﻿ ﻿… Would Doctor McLaren recover? They couldn’t say. It was too soon to tell. He was very, very badly hurt﻿ ﻿… She took the blow standing, and they rejoiced in her pluck. She was invited to stay the night, and they were at pains to give her every scrap of available information about her husband﻿—both good and bad.
From the first, Mrs. McLaren was adopted at Brightwood on unusual terms. When Doctor Merrick told her, at noon, that her husband was putting up a very encouraging resistance, and had at least an even break in the decision, she did not stage a scene. There was a momentary tight closing of her eyes, a quick breath of relief, and a misty smile; but no theatricals. She had herself well in hand. Bobby liked her for that. He was glad to have her about. Sometimes, when her husband was asleep, she would read to other convalescents. On several occasions she offered first aid to hysterical next-of-kins, who waited while operations of interest to them were in progress. One day, upon invitation of Doctor Pyle, she went into the operating-room to see “some interesting surgery”﻿—from which entertainment she hastily excused herself, however, when a diminutive saw began making noises that played the deuce with her digestion.
The McLarens were, by popular suffrage at Brightwood, “all to the good.”
---
One afternoon, the Reverend Bruce said to Doctor Merrick, as the latter sat by his invalid chair, visiting﻿—not very professionally, but with a hope of hearing a few more Scotch stories: “Beloved, I’ll soon be out of here, and I’m just a bit anxious about my bill. My income is small, and my present balance at the bank, if there is one, would amuse you. Of course, I know what the hospital charges are, and I can manage to pay them. But I have been afraid to ask about your fee, thinking perhaps the shock wouldn’t be good for me. Speaking of the Scotch﻿—what are you going to charge me?”
“Well﻿—I’ll make you a proposition, dominie. You have given me a chance to patch your head. I’ll give you a chance to do something for my soul. And we’ll call it square. I’ll take it out in trade. How’s that?”
“It’s mighty generous,” rumbled McLaren, in a tone at least three added lines below the bass clef. “I’ll expect you to come in and attend my church, soon as I am in the running again.”
“Oh﻿—do I have to go to your church for this treatment?”
“Well﻿—I came to your hospital, didn’t I, for mine?”
“You win!” said Bobby submissively. “I’ll be there!”
---
Pursuant to his promise, he had gone to Grace Church on that fine May morning, after having telephoned the McLarens he was coming, and having accepted their invitation to return with them to their apartment, afterwards, for luncheon. Betty McLaren had quite enjoyed the sensation of presenting him to many of their friends. She had been very proud of Bruce’s performance in the pulpit. She was having a good day﻿ ﻿… Would Doctor Merrick have two lumps or three; cream or lemon; and wasn’t he surprised to see so many young people in the congregation?﻿ ﻿… Doctor Merrick would have one lump; neither cream nor lemon; and was it anything to be surprised at that young people went to church?
“Oh﻿—quite!” replied Doctor McLaren, helping his guest to a portion of a delicious omelette. “That’s our one great satisfaction! You see﻿—the students and the young business and professional people have outgrown the old traditions and are eager for an﻿—shall I say an intellectual approach to religion. We have been trying to give it to them.”
“I noticed that,” said Bobby. “Your sermon was very scholarly; and they liked it, I am sure.”
“Well, doctor﻿—if you don’t mind being helpfully candid with me, exactly how did it strike you﻿—as a scientist?”
“Oh, I’m not much of a scientist. A surgeon doesn’t have to be a scientist﻿—just a good mechanic.”
Betty McLaren protested with a laugh.
“Come now, Doctor Merrick! The very idea! You﻿—not a scientist? We know better than that!”
“At all events, you have the scientific outlook﻿—the scientific approach,” insisted Doctor McLaren. “Perhaps you noticed at what pains I was to avoid the old stock phrases of theology.”
“I fear I wouldn’t recognize them as such,” confessed Bobby. “But﻿—what’s the matter with the old terminology?”
“Obsolete! Misleading! We’ll have to evolve a new vocabulary for religion, to make it rank with other subjects of interest. We’ve got to phrase it in modern terms; don’t you think so?” Doctor McLaren was eager for his guest’s approval.
“Perhaps,” agreed Bobby tentatively. “I don’t know. Whether people could learn any more about religion by changing its names for things of concern to it, I’m not sure. It just occurs to me﻿—casting about at random for a parallel case﻿—that the word ‘electricity’ means ‘amber.’ All that the ancients knew about electricity was that a chunk of amber, when rubbed with silk, would pick up a feather. Now that it has been developed until it will pick up a locomotive, electricity still means amber. They never went to the bother to change the name of it. Maybe they thought there was at least a pleasant sentiment in retaining the name. More likely, they never thought about it, at all. Too busy trying to make it work, I suppose.”
“Humph! That’s a new idea. Then you think it doesn’t make much difference about the phraseology of religion?”
“It wouldn’t﻿—to me,” replied Bobby, hoping he had not too ardently objected to a pet theory of his host.
“Well﻿—there seems to be a demand for a more adequate interpretation of theology. We are trying to be a little less dogmatic in our assertions and a little more honest. For instance﻿—I think it’s ever so much better to say frankly that God is an hypothesis than to attempt to offer proofs which fail to stand up under their own ponderosity.”
Bobby was tardy with a rejoinder, and both McLarens silently quizzed him out of the tails of their eyes. Surely he ought at least to believe in the Deity as an hypothesis!﻿ ﻿… He observed that they waited.
“I’m afraid I don’t accept that,” he said at length, rather shyly.
“Oh﻿—Doctor Merrick!” reproved Betty disappointedly. “You don’t mean to say that you do not believe in God, at all!”
“I mean that I do not think of God as an hypothesis.”
“But﻿—my dear fellow,” exclaimed McLaren, “we really have no hard and fast proofs, you know!”
“Haven’t you?” asked Bobby quietly. “I have.”
The two forks in use by the McLaren family were simultaneously put down upon their plates.
“Er﻿—how do you mean﻿—proofs?” queried his host.
---
Bobby wished then that he had smilingly deferred to the minister’s theory. He had no relish for controversy. And this was no place for it, had he been no end a debater. Moreover, he knew he was not in a position to explain what he had called his proofs. Lamely he admitted that what he had considered sufficient evidence for the existence of God might satisfy no one but himself. He privately hoped the conversation might soon find safer going.
“You’re probably arguing from ‘design,’ ” McLaren suggested bookishly.
“Oh﻿—probably,” said Bobby, with a gesture of dismissal.
“The whole business of institutionalized religion,” resumed McLaren, didactically, “demands reappraisal! It appals me to contemplate what must be the future of the Church when all the people who are now fifty and up are in their graves! This oncoming generation, now in its adolescence, is not in the least way concerned about organized religion. Religious enough, instinctively, I dare say; but out of sorts with the sects; weary of their bad-mannered yammering at one another over matters in which one man’s guess is as good as another’s, and no outcome promised either in faith or conduct, no matter whose guess is right!”
“Is it that bad?” commented Bobby. “I hadn’t known the churches were losing ground. There seems to be such a lot of them.”
“Yes﻿—too many!” grumbled McLaren. “Too many﻿—of the wrong kind!﻿ ﻿… Take so important a matter, now, as the nature and mission of Christianity’s author, himself. A Christ who can help us to a clearer perception of God needs to be a personality confronted with problems similar to ours, and solving them with knowledge and power to which we also have access﻿—else he offers us no example, at all.
“But here we have a majority of the churches trying to elicit interest in him because he was supernaturally born, which I wasn’t; because he turned water into wine, which I can’t; because he paid his taxes with money found in a fish’s mouth, which﻿—for all my Scotch ingenuity﻿—I can’t do; because he silenced the storm with a word and a gesture, whereas I must bail the boat; because he called back from the grave his friend who had been dead four days, while I must content myself with planting a rosebush and calling it a closed incident! What we want is a Christ whose service to us, in leading us toward God, is not predicated upon our dissimilarities, but upon our likenesses!
“In our church, we’re trying to offer a Christ who is not a mere prestidigitator﻿—a magician who feeds an acre of people from a boy’s lunch-basket﻿—but a great prophet and an understanding friend! Don’t you think a man can accept that, and still be a sound scientist?”
Bobby accepted a light from his hostess’s hand, and slowly nodded.
“I know very little about the conflict between the traditional estimate of Christ and the more recent theory. Viewing it superficially, I should say that neither system would appeal very strongly to this age. Isn’t the modern school just substituting a new metaphysic for the old one? Our generation is doing all its thinking in terms of power, energy, dynamics﻿—the kind you read about, not in a book, but on a meter! Why not concede the reality of supernormal assistance, to be had under fixed conditions, and encourage people to go after it?”
“That sounds a little as if you believed in prayer, Doctor Merrick,” said Betty wistfully.
“You mean﻿—getting down on your knees to wish you had something?”
“Oh, it’s more than that!﻿ ﻿… Asking God to give it to you!”
“Well, that depends on your credit.”
“Agreed!” nodded McLaren.
“I’m afraid I don’t quite understand,” said Betty.
“Why, he means that unless one has been living up to one’s best ideals, it’s useless to ask for God’s approval and assistance. That’s obvious.”
“No,” said Bobby. “That is not what I meant. If you’re interested, I’ll tell you a story.”
---
For the next two hours﻿—they had moved to the library on Bobby’s hint that they had let themselves in for an extended tale﻿—the McLarens sat scarcely daring to trust their own hearing.
Eager to give them all the steps of his spiritual progress, in their exact sequence, he had begun back in Randolph’s studio. Carefully picking his way with a care to the avoidance of any act of his own, performed in pursuance to the Galilean theory of availing prayer, he laid the facts before them in a dispassionate recital.
He finished as he had begun. He could hardly expect them to believe it, he said. He hadn’t believed it; had been disgusted by it; intellectually offended by it; in violent revolt against it; but﻿—well, there it was!
“How utterly trivial,” said McLaren humbly, “my whole programme of preaching seems in the face of such astounding possibilities! Why﻿—we’ve been trying to teach religion without﻿—without knowing what it’s about!”
“Oh, I shouldn’t go so far as to think that!” consoled Bobby. “You’ve inspired people to take stock of themselves. They can’t help being better for every serious thought you’ve given them about life and duty. That’s ethics. And ethics is decidedly important. This thing I’ve been talking about is not in the field of ethics. It belongs rather to science. We have been at great pains to construct devices and machinery to be energized by steam and electricity and sunshine; but haven’t realized how human personality can be made just as receptive to the power of our Major Personality.”
“I feel, today,” said McLaren, “as if I’d been doing nothing﻿—exactly nothing!”
“By no means! You have been doing some highly necessary work in clearing away the old superstitions; the old irrelevancies. That’s not labour lost, you may be sure! Only﻿—as I listened, this morning, I couldn’t help wishing that this new interpretation of religion which you are so splendidly equipped to offer might go further and show how soundly scientific religion is. You counselled us, today, to accept the evolutionary hypothesis. You said﻿—if I recall correctly﻿—that we could explain everything we have and are by that theory﻿ ﻿… Now﻿—I don’t agree with you. Perhaps our bodies derive from some pre-human type of life. Perhaps all romantic literature is but an elaboration of the animal’s urge to reproduce itself. Perhaps our brains are but refinements of elementary nerve ganglia that used to respond, automatically, to the necessity for food and shelter﻿ ﻿… It hasn’t been proved. You were ever so much more sure of it, in your pulpit, than my biology professor was, in his classroom﻿ ﻿… But﻿—assuming a physical evolution, biology has no explanation to offer for human personality. You ask old man Harper how he accounts for aspiration, penitence, inquisitiveness about our origin, concern about our future﻿—and he’ll say, ‘I’m not a theologian, sir! I’m a biologist!’ ”
“And I suppose you’re hinting,” smiled McLaren, “that my chief business is to account for aspiration, penitence, and man’s passion to be a time-binder﻿—and if anybody inquires what I think about evolution, I’d better reply, ‘I’m not a biologist, sir! I’m not a theologian!’ ”
“Something like that,” agreed Bobby.
“I wonder if we modernists,” said McLaren, after a considerable pause, “are not somewhat in the predicament of Moses, who had enough audacity to lead the slaves out of their bondage, but lacked the ingenuity to take them on into a country that would support them. We’ve emancipated them; but﻿—they’re still wandering about in the jungle, dissatisfied, hungry, making occasional excursions into paganism and experimenting with all manner of eccentric cults, longing for the spiritual equivalent of their repudiated superstitions﻿—sometimes even wishing they were back in the old harness!”
“It’s worth while to have fetched them out of that,” said Bobby. “It ought to be equally interesting to lead them on. They mustn’t go back! But they will﻿—if they’re not pointed to something more attractive than the jungle you say they’re in.”
---
As he left the house at four, McLaren followed him out to his big coupé parked at the kerb.
“Merrick,” he said rather timidly, “would it be asking too much of you to come to my church again next Sunday? I’m going to have something a little more constructive to offer﻿—and I’d like your reaction.”
“I would gladly, but I shall be on the briny deep. Sailing Saturday to France, en route to Vienna to see a colleague. I’ll be happy to come when I return.”
He stepped on the starter and the powerful engine hummed.
McLaren gripped his hand.
“Merrick﻿—just a minute!﻿ ﻿… We modernists have been trying to show how religion is not at odds with science. What we’ve got to do now is to show how religion *is a science*! Isn’t that what you mean?”
“Exactly! Nothing less or else than that! You have it! More power to you! See you in September!”

===Chinese===

布鲁斯·麦克拉伦夫妇正在他们陈设温馨的公寓里，设午宴款待罗伯特·梅里克医生。这天是星期天；三人刚从格雷斯教堂回来——那位杰出的年轻外科医生同麦克拉伦太太一道出现在牧师席上，激起了一片亲切的骄傲与满足的低语。
博比·梅里克近来对脑外科事业的那份辉煌贡献，被报界大炒特炒，炒得他本人颇有些狼狈；因为他怀着正经科学工作者对出风头的那份羞怯。眼看自己的发明被用新闻吹嘘的黑话来描写，实在难堪；日报社论版那几篇好意的颂扬，还有各类文摘评论给他传记添的脂粉，他领情也领得远不如希望的痛快。
当然，这实在是一条顶呱呱的新闻，头版双栏大标题当之无愧。笔下诸位一样没漏。青年梅里克医生放着大笔家产供养的清福不享，义无反顾地献身于外科最艰深、最令人气馁的专科，这一点被渲染到了无以复加。若不是他及时筑垒自守，挡住压过来的特写作家大军，事情无疑还要糟。
“您对您的公众，实在欠着一笔债呢！”其中一位曾喳喳地说，那口气像在同哪个迷电影的轻佻姑娘说话——人家刚赢了《时报》的选美。
甚至有人翻出旧事：几年前，就在另一位杰出的脑外科医生、已故的韦恩·哈德森溺亡萨吉纳克湖的同一时刻，梅里克医生的生命获救了。一家（桃色的）报纸大胆猜测：这位阔少梅里克立即决意进医学院专攻脑外科，是否受那场惨剧的影响，甚或正是它直接促成；只是细节无着，又从不幸的主角及其亲友嘴里撬不出半句，只好把暗示抛出去，任公众自下结论。
消息见报十八小时之内，博比便已断定：倘若把头版声名的负债同它带来的进益放在天平上，他跟“名声”的这本账早已透支。显然，一颗新星还是行踪隐些好。他的信箱塞满了各色已知乞儿的苦苦纠缠；所谓慈善团体的呼吁，从“保障国际了解的基金会”一路排到“救济受欺蓝樫鸟”的野鸡博爱事业。他收到自家酿造的颂诗，颂他之德；黏黏糊糊的赞歌，盼他自费出版；甜腻腻的情书，不少还附有玉照。请他去午餐演讲的请帖围攻他。他成了逃犯，从一道掩体窜向另一道掩体。
就连温迪米尔也不得清静。围剿开始后不久的一个周末，他去那里躲清静，懊恼地发现祖父正得意洋洋地——就他而论，简直是滔滔不绝地——招待一位剪裁严肃的年轻女子；此人为了给一篇杂志特稿添彩，需要一些博比童年的私密细节。
“啊——罗伯特——想不到你回来！”老尼古拉斯喊，“我们正说你呢。这位年轻小姐……”
“我看见了。”博比冷冷地应，“我想换个话题，她会原谅我们的。”
“那可不行！”来客咯咯地笑。
局面尴尬，尼古拉斯又窘又无措；博比唤来梅格斯，替他解了围。
“梅格斯，叫斯蒂芬把这位女士送去年站。她急着赶四点十六的车。”
---
至于同行们，他们的感激与慷慨的道贺，是一大乐源。每天都有着本专科名家庄重的赞辞来函，谢他慨然把成果公诸同侪的无私。文明世界的每个国家，他都收着了信。
如今，骤然的名声总算凝了冻，博比羞怯地从藏身处探出头来，恢复了正常的作息与消遣。公共场所那些把他认出来的注视、耳语和拐肘，他还不能习惯；可既然不能永远像猎物似的潜行，他只好尽量掩住那份自我意识，装出满不在乎领受他的刑罚。今天，他甚至冒险进了教堂。
---
麦克拉伦牧师向一大群体面的会众——足有一半不满四十岁——讲了一篇渊博的证道，题目他指望能特别投他这位贵客之所好。
其实，罗伯特·梅里克医生会怎样看待那篇证道，在年轻的牧师备讲时的心头，占了那么大的位置，以至他好不容易才忍住，没用上一整套科学术语——那远在他羊群的悟性之外；虽说以教堂会众而论，他们的智识评级得优加，而且自己也大方承认。格雷斯教堂对自己的摩登，颇为自觉。
“真的，全城最有远见的——不，唯一有远见的教堂！”——人们记得，西尔贝克太太举荐麦克拉伦牧师为社会大会那一届祈求神佑时，就是这样开场的；那一届的大会议程里，编有一场节制生育的讨论。
“在哪方面有远见？”社会大会会长、大急流城的科德莉亚·孔茨太太干巴巴地问，拿她那柄精巧的长柄眼镜点着议事单。“经济问题上有远见，社会问题、政治问题上有远见——还是仅仅摆出一副正统最后堡垒的姿态？”
西尔贝克太太有点晕，也不免有点火，答道：格雷斯教堂究竟把自由引到多远、引向何方，她实在说不上——接着把钱包啪啪按了好几声，响得很，强调自己不再奉陪这个话头——浑然不知北边州里这位风风火火的女议长，恰恰摸着了一条火线。
---
不过，麦克拉伦牧师顺从了必要的审慎，赶在末一刻换掉了几个学理词，唯恐超出会众的射程；可即便为晓畅计做了这几处忍痛的改动，那篇讲词依旧是一个科学家对另一个科学家的，听的人既受宠若惊，又被它迷人的莫测高深弄得晕头转向。他们也想知道罗伯特·梅里克医生对它怎么看，也为他们这位睿智的年轻牧师 灼灼地骄傲。
他们的骄傲全然有理。布鲁斯·梅克拉伦牧师兼博士，绝非爱用大字的浮夸学究，也不是一心挣一个哲人名声的庄重废话篓子。他的学问是扎实的；那天早晨的证道，为它增了光。
执事切斯特热情攥住牧师的手，在金属管风琴尾奏的尖啸中大嗓喊道：他猜这是格雷斯教堂有史以来最深刻的一篇证道！这话全然正确；而且此处的“猜”字，绝非口头禅。倘使切斯特先生是个讲究字眼的人——他是位以整车皮为单位烤饼干的殷实饼商，也不讳言自己十三岁离开学校——那么要表达他自己鉴定这篇讲章所显学问的能力，再没有一个字比“猜”更熨帖的了。倘若拿一块感光底片去曝一曝切斯特先生对麦克拉伦所讲题目的了解，那底片用过后还能完好的另作他用。
---
麦克拉伦牧师与梅里克医生之间这段热络的交情，始自一个阴冷的三月之夜：这位瘦长条、古铜色头发的牧师被送进布莱特伍德，人事不省，鼾声粗重，右颞鳞部一道又险又丑的破口。他满身泥污血污，浑身瘫软。那晚他的形势凶多吉少；从晚上九点完成必要的修补起，到次日早晨七点，梅里克医生只给自己批了这样一点空闲：在病人门前走廊里来回踱几步，神经质地猛抽香烟；三点钟护士送上三明治和牛奶，他心不在焉地受用了。
博比·梅里克从第一眼就喜欢麦克拉伦；喜欢他躺在手术台上那副身量与筋骨——他正下意识地打着他的求生之仗；喜欢他宽阔的额形，耳朵的轮廓，下巴的凹槽，打惯了网球的右前臂的坚实，皮肤的质地，拇指的椭圆曲线。这一切都有讲究。若要梅里克医生来写，不等听此人开口，他就能就麦克拉伦牧师的性格，写出两千字的论文。
第二天，他更喜欢他的病人了：他头一回从朦胧中醒来，对周遭有了含糊的意识，一眼看清了处境，显然只当是分内的寻常事，在护士的提议下便睡去，懒得发问——这份天生的镇定。几天后，他更喜欢他了：他承受苦楚——那苦楚委实不少——的功夫高人一等，不皱眉，不哼哈。末了，出事一个礼拜后，牧师平平静静、毫无怨毒地谈起那个把他撞倒在安全区里的酗酒的破产者，他更喜欢他了。
“他心里想必已经够难受了。”麦克拉伦用他那深沉的男低音说，一口苏格兰祖传的卷舌把它泡软了，“反正，我不打算追究，也不打算翻来覆去地嚼它，把自己嚼苦了。”
“这见识好得惊人！”梅里克评论道，心里打定主意：等这人下了地、穿上靴子，要多同他来往。他从没结识过牧师。他对教士这个行当那点影影绰绰的见解，采集自漫画、专栏作家的俏皮话与挖苦、还有舞台和银幕上朝这行当甩过来的冷枪。前些天他还皱着眉头翻完一本诋毁此行的恶毒小说。他谈不上积极地厌恶他们，只是同不进教堂的公众持着同样的笼统看法：牧师们——长话短说——是一窝傻子。
日子一天天过去，年轻的外科医生发觉自己越来越愉快地被这病人吸引，喜欢他在“俏皮昂贵”的时刻说的俏皮话，佩服他招架医生护士们善意打趣时的机敏——那打趣正是他自己古怪的幽默招来的。布莱特伍德上上下下，在他康复期间，差不多都先后进来看过他；众口一词，这人够意思。
布莱特伍德上下，对那位棕色眼睛、酒窝深深的麦克拉伦太太，也同样热络。丈夫送到后半小时她就到了，肝肠寸断，却控制得叫人敬佩。他们给她去了电话，说麦克拉伦牧师伤势危重，请她即刻过来。她来了，没有歇斯底里……麦克拉伦牧师能康复吗？说不好。为时尚早。他伤得非常、非常重……她站着受住了这一击，众人为她的胆气欢喜。请她留下过夜；他们尽心把关于她丈夫的一切可得的音讯——好的坏的——都给她。
从第一刻起，麦克拉伦太太就被布莱特伍德以破格的条件收养了。中午，梅里克医生告诉她：她丈夫的抵抗非常令人鼓舞，至少胜负各半；她没有上演晕厥。眼睛紧紧闭了一瞬，一口气松下来，一个朦胧的笑；没有戏。她把自己拿捏得很好。博比为此喜欢她。有她在跟前，他高兴。丈夫睡着时，她有时给别的康复病人念书。好几回，她主动救护那些歇斯底里的至亲——手术进行中，他们在门外候着。有一天，应派尔医生之邀，她进了手术室看“一场有意思的手术”——只是当一把小锯子开始发出搅得她消化不良的动静时，她匆匆告退了。
依布莱特伍德的公议，麦克拉伦夫妇“没得挑”。
---
一天下午，布鲁斯牧师对坐在病椅旁的梅里克医生说——此来不算很职业，只是巴望再听几个苏格兰故事：“亲爱的，我不久就能出院了，只是有点为账单发愁。我进项微薄，我在银行的存款——若还有存款——定能博您一笑。医院的收费，我自然知道，也付得出。可您的诊费，我一直不敢问，怕那打击于养病不宜。就着苏格兰人这个话头——您打算收我多少？”
“呢——我给您出个价，牧师。您给过我一个机会修补您的脑袋；我也给您一个机会，照看照看我的灵魂。两清。我拿劳务抵账。如何？”
“真是大方极了。”麦克拉伦瓮声瓮气，那音调至少比低音谱号又沉了三线。“那我就指望您上我的教堂了，等我重新开张。”
“哦——治我这个病，非上您的教堂不可？”
“呢——治我这个病，我上的可是您的医院，对不对？”
“您赢了！”博比乖乖地说，“我一定到！”
---
依着许诺，五月那个晴朗的早晨，他先给麦克拉伦夫妇挂了电话说要来，又应了他们的邀请，做完礼拜随他们回公寓用午餐，果然去了格雷斯教堂。贝蒂·麦克拉伦把他介绍给那么多朋友，那阵风光她受用极了。她为布鲁斯在讲坛上的表现骄傲得很。她这天过得真好……梅里克医生的茶，加两块糖还是三块？加奶油还是柠檬？还有，会众里这么多年轻人，他不惊奇吗？……梅里克医生加一块糖；奶油柠檬都不加；年轻人上教堂，有什么可惊奇的？
“哦——可不！”麦克拉伦牧师应道，给客人又添了一份可口的煎蛋卷。“这是我们最大的满足！您看——学生和年轻的企业界职业界人士，早长出了旧传统，巴望有一种——我该怎么说——对宗教的智性进路。我们一直想给他们的，就是这个。”
“我注意到了。”博比说，“您的证道很有学养；他们爱听，我敢担保。”
“呢，医生——您若不介意对我有益地直率一句：以一个科学家的眼光看，它到底给您的印象如何？”
“哦，我可算不上科学家。外科医生不必是科学家——做个好技师就行。”
贝蒂·麦克拉伦笑着抗议。
“得了，梅里克医生！瞧您说的！您——不是科学家？我们心里有数！”
“至少，您有科学的视野——科学的进路。”麦克拉伦牧师坚持，“您或许留意到，我费了多大的劲避开神学那些老掉牙的套话。”
“只怕我认不出它们来。”博比招认，“不过——旧术语又有什么不好？”
“过时了！误导人！我们得给宗教演化出一套新词汇，让它同其他学问平起平坐。得用现代的字眼来表述；您说是不是？”麦克拉伦牧师巴望着客人的首肯。
“也许吧。”博比试掂着同意，“说不好。把宗教关切的那些东西换个名字，人们就能多懂一点宗教吗？我拿不准。我恰好想起——随手找一个类比——‘电’这个词，本义是‘琥珀’。古人关于电的全部知识，就是一块琥珀拿丝绸一蹭，能吸起一根羽毛。如今电发展到能吊起一整列火车，它还是叫‘琥珀’。谁也没费那个事给它改名。兴许他们觉得留着这个名字，至少有几分雅意。多半呢，压根儿没人想过。我猜，都忙着让它转起来呢。”
“哼！这是个新想法。这么说，您觉得宗教的措辞无关紧要？”
“于我——无关紧要。”博比答，暗暗希望自己没有太起劲地驳了主人的得意理论。
“呢——看来，人们要求一种更充分的解释神学。我们正努力把断语少下得武断些，多几分诚实。譬如——我想，坦率地说上帝是一个假说，总比端出一套压垮自身的证明要好。”
博比迟迟没有接话；麦克拉伦夫妇各自用眼角悄悄打量他。他总该至少把神明当一个假说来信吧！……他们等着他。
“只怕这一点我不能同意。”他终于说，颇有些腼腆。
“哦——梅里克医生！”贝蒂失望地责怪，“您总不是要说，您压根儿不信上帝吧！”
“我是说，我不把上帝当一个假说。”
“可是——亲爱的朋友，”麦克拉伦喊道，“我们实在拿不出铁证，您知道！”
“拿不出？”博比平静地问，“我拿得出。”
麦克拉伦家正用着餐叉的两只手，同时放到了盘子上。
“呃——您所谓证据——怎么讲？”主人问。
---
博比这时但愿方才笑着顺着牧师的理论便罢。他不爱论战。就算他是个一流的辩士，这里也不是论战的地方。何况他清楚，他所谓证据，眼下自己也没法解释。他讷讷地承认：被他当作上帝存在之充分证据的那些，也许除他之外谁也满足不了。他私心里巴望话头快些踏上安全地带。
“您多半是从‘设计论’立论。”麦克拉伦书卷气地提点。
“哦——多半是。”博比说，摆手撂开。
“建制化宗教的这一整套，”麦克拉伦接着说，教导腔又起，“必须重估！一想到如今五十岁往上的人统统入土之后，教会的前途，我就不寒而栗！正在到来的这一代，眼下正当少年，对有组织的宗教毫不挂心。论宗教感，本能地，我料想是有的；可同各宗各派闹了别扭；腻烦了它们彼此间那副没教养的聒噪——吵的全是一个人的瞎猜同另一个人的瞎猜一般高的话头，而且无论谁猜中，无论信仰还是行为，都不见个下文！”
“当真糟到这地步？”博比评论，“我还不知道教会在失地。瞧着处处是教堂嘛。”
“是——太多了！”麦克拉伦咕哝，“太多——而且全不对路！……就拿顶要紧的一件事来说：基督教创立者本人的性质与使命。一个能领我们更清晰地看见上帝的基督，须得是一个面对着与我们相似难题的人格，用我们同样够得着的知识与能力去解开它们——否则他给我们立的榜样，就是空的。
“可眼下，多数教会招人对他起兴，靠的是：他超感降生，我不会；他把水变酒，我不能；他拿鱼嘴里的钱完税——凭我一身苏格兰的机巧，也办不到；他一言一挥手就平了风浪，我却只能往船外戽水；他把死了四天的朋友从坟里唤回来，我却只好栽一株玫瑰，宣布结案！我们要的基督，是他对我们的用处——领我们走向上帝——不建立在我们的相异上，而建立在我们的相同上！
“在我们的教堂里，我们想给出的基督，不是一个变戏法的——不是一个拿男孩子的午餐篮喂饱一英亩人的魔术师——而是一位大先知、一位善解人意的朋友！您说，一个人接受了这个，还能不能做个健全的科学家？”
博比就着女主人的手点了烟，缓缓点头。
“关于基督的传统评估同较新学说的这场争执，我知之甚少。皮相地看，恐怕两套都拴不住这个时代。现代派岂不也只是拿一套新玄学换掉了旧玄学？我们这代人，一切思维都用量、能、动力来说——是在表上读出的那一种，不是书上读来的！何不承认超常之助的真实，承认它按固定的条件可得，进而鼓励人们去求取呢？”
“听这话，您有点像信祈祷了，梅里克医生。”贝蒂神往地说。
“您是说——跪下去，许愿自己有个什么？”
“哦，不止于此！……是求上帝把它赐给您！”
“呢，那得看您的信用。”
“说得对！”麦克拉伦点头。
“我怕没听懂。”贝蒂说。
“他是说，人若没有一直活在自己的最佳理想里，去求上帝的嘉许与扶助，也是枉然。这不是明摆着的。”
“不，”博比说，“我不是这个意思。你们若有兴，我讲个故事。”
---
此后的两个钟头——依着博比的暗示，三人挪进了书房，既然听的就是一部长篇——麦克拉伦夫妇坐着，简直不敢相信自己的耳朵。
他巴望把灵性进境的每一级台阶，按本来的次序交代给他们，便从兰道夫的工作室讲起。他小心择路，只述事实，不偏不倚；凡他自己循着那位加利利人的“有效祈祷”论所行的任何一举，都小心避开不提。
他有始有终。他说，他不敢指望他们信。他自己就不曾信；曾对它恶心；智识上被它冒犯；起而猛烈反叛过它；可是——呢，它就在那儿！
“面对这样的惊人可能，”麦克拉伦谦卑地说，“我整个的布道纲领显得何其琐屑！……天哪——我们一直想教宗教，却——却不知道宗教讲的是什么！”
“哦，我可不敢那么想！”博比宽慰道，“您启发人们自省。您给他们的每一句关于人生与责任的严肃思考，都必叫他们受益。那是伦理学。伦理学极其要紧。而我方才讲的这一件，不在伦理学境内。它属于科学。我们费尽心血造出机器，用蒸汽、电力、阳光驱动；却没有意识到，人的人格，同样可以变得那样易于领受我们至大人格的力量。”
“我今天觉得，”麦克拉伦说，“自己仿佛什么也没干——恰恰是什么也没干！”
“绝不是！您一直在做一件极必需的工作：清扫旧迷信、旧枝蔓。这功夫没有白费，您放心！只是——今早我听着，不免想：您这么装备精良，正可献出的这套宗教新解，何不再往前走一步，指明宗教何其合乎科学。您今天劝我们接受进化论假说。您说——我记得不错的话——凭那一套，我们能解释我们所有的一切、我们所是的一切……呢——这一点我不同意。我们的身体，也许源自某种前人类的生命形态。也许一切浪漫文学，不过是动物繁衍冲动的一番铺陈。也许我们的脑子，不过是初级神经节的一点精炼——那神经节从前自动应对觅食觅巢之需……这都没有证实。您在讲坛上，可比我的生物学教授在课堂上笃定多了……可是——就算承认身体的进化，生物学对人类的人格，拿不出任何解释。您去问老哈珀：向往、悔恨、对来历的好奇、对前程的挂心，他怎么解释？他必答：‘先生，我不是神学家！我是生物学家！’”
“我猜您是在提点我，”麦克拉伦笑道，“我的本业，是去解释向往、悔恨，以及人做‘时间黏合者’的激情——若有人问我对进化论怎么看，我最好答：‘先生，我不是生物学家！——也轮不到神学家来答！’”
“差不多是这个意思。”博比同意。
“不知我们这些现代派，”隔了好一阵，麦克拉伦说，“是不是也陷在摩西的窘境里：他有胆量领奴隶们出牢笼，却缺那份机巧，领他们进一片养得活他们的地土。我们解放了他们；可是——他们还在丛林里流浪，不满，饥饿，时时窜进异教的领地，什么古怪的教门都要试一试，巴望着一样东西，抵得上他们丢掉的迷信——有时甚至巴不得套回旧轭里去！”
“把他们从那里面领出来，已是一功。”博比说，“把他们领向前去，该同样有味。不能让他们退回去！可他们会的——若没人指给他们一样比您所说的丛林更有吸引力的东西。”
---
四点钟他告辞，麦克拉伦送他到路缘停着的大轿车旁。
“梅里克，”他颇为羞怯地说，“下个礼拜天再请您来一趟，会不会太打扰？我要献上一点更有建树的东西——想听听您的反应。”
“乐意之至，只是我将在碧波之上。星期六启航去法国，转道维也纳看一位同行。回来我一定来。”
他踩下启动踏板，强劲的引擎嗡嗡响起。
麦克拉伦攥住他的手。
“梅里克——稍等！……我们现代派一直在证明，宗教同科学并不相悖。如今我们该做的，是证明宗教本身就是一门科学！您说的，是不是这个意思？”
“正是！非此无他！您抓住了！愿力量与您同在！九月见！”

## XX / 二十
===Original===

Yes﻿—Doctor Ardmore was in, admitted the desk in the lobby, but it was to be doubted if he could be seen﻿ ﻿… Yes﻿—so very busy today﻿ ﻿… Mrs. Hudson?﻿ ﻿… She was still living﻿ ﻿… Would he sit down?﻿ ﻿… The desk was sympathetic.
Bobby nervously scribbled a message.
“Take that card to Doctor Ardmore,” he commanded, “and make sure he gets it!”
In a few minutes a stocky, greying man of forty-five came quickly down the corridor and reached out his hand.
“Yes﻿—I’m Ardmore. Only today we were speaking of you, Doctor Merrick. This is indeed a great pleasure﻿ ﻿… Now﻿—you have come about your countrywoman, Mrs. Hudson﻿ ﻿… My friend, I fear we are not to save her﻿ ﻿… No﻿—nothing has been done yet﻿ ﻿… Too soon﻿—you will understand﻿ ﻿… Cerebral concussion﻿ ﻿… Donelli must wait a little﻿ ﻿… He thinks tonight it may be safe to try﻿ ﻿… but he has no hope﻿ ﻿… Conscious? Oh﻿—fitfully; partially. We have had her quieted, you know﻿ ﻿… She is aware that she is totally blind﻿—I am sure of that.”
“Then the contusion covers the occipital lobes!”
“Exactly! Squarely on the back of the head! Very deep lesion!﻿ ﻿… Then there are the fractured ribs. That’s bad too. Lowers the resistance﻿ ﻿… Donelli had decided at noon that it was useless to attempt the head operation, but now he’s going into it﻿ ﻿… He does not see too many of these cases﻿ ﻿… I could wish﻿—but﻿—my God, man! *You’re* here! You shall do it! Donelli will be very grateful!”
Bobby’s heart was pounding.
“You really think he might want me to?”
“Want you? He’ll say you’re a godsend! Donelli’s an excellent surgeon, but he does not specialize. You’ll win his everlasting appreciation!﻿ ﻿… Come﻿—we will go to your patient﻿ ﻿… I shall be responsible for Donelli’s full approval﻿ ﻿… Come﻿—please.”
“Just a moment, Doctor Ardmore,” said Bobby, tugging loose from the arm that was propelling him down the corridor. “I have something to say to you before I see Mrs. Hudson. Let us go some place where we may talk privately.”
Ardmore led the way into a small parlour.
“I think you ought to know,” said Bobby, “that this young woman’s life is more to me than of professional concern; more than might be implied by the fact that we are fellow countrymen and acquaintances﻿ ﻿… I have had hopes of making her my wife.”
“My word!﻿—What a situation!”
“Yes, isn’t it? Now, I’ll tell you the rest of it. We have had a very serious misunderstanding. That is to say﻿—she is misunderstanding me. That being the case﻿—perhaps it would be much better, in the event she has an interval of consciousness, that she be left unaware that I am her surgeon.”
“But,” objected Ardmore, “might not that fact put a little more resistance into her?”
“Not the kind we want.”
“Well﻿—you ought to know﻿ ﻿… I shall tell the nurse and endeavour to keep your identity secret.”
---
It was the droop of her mouth that brought the scalding tears to Bobby’s eyes. For a moment he thought he couldn’t bear it.
Ardmore, observing how deeply he was affected, felt some concern. This was not an affair into which an operating surgeon dared to fetch his emotions. He gripped the visitor’s shoulder with five vice-like fingers and muttered, “Steady! You’re her doctor; not her lover!”
He motioned the nurse to follow him out into the hall and left Bobby alone with his patient. She roused slightly, drew a short, painfully interrupted sigh that made her wince; the lips tightened momentarily, and relaxed again into the pensive droop.
Booby took the hand that lay on the white counterpane and held it in both of his. There was the very faintest imaginable pressure in her fingers. Dear little chap! She was at least vaguely aware of a friendly handclasp, no matter where it came from. That was that much﻿—anyhow!
The door opened and Ardmore returned with the nurse whose eyes sparkled. She shared an important secret.
“Perhaps you would like to make an examination, doctor,” said Ardmore.
Bobby nodded. The nurse led him to a wash room and found him a smock and gloves.
The patient was placed in position and the temporary bandages were removed. Bobby stood stunned at the sight. It was, as Ardmore had said, very, very deep. He gave a quick, almost articulate intake of breath as he touched it﻿ ﻿… Again the English doctor’s strong fingers gathered up a large handful of his young colleague’s shoulder and growled, “Remember! She’s your *patient*!”
“There’s nothing to be gained by waiting,” said Bobby. “Send for Doctor Donelli, please. We will go into this as soon as he arrives.”
---
It was six o’clock when Helen was laid upon the operating table, and seven-thirty when she was transferred again to the wheeled bed on which she had come.
During that hour and a half, Bobby Merrick, by a supreme effort of his will, was a brain surgeon, and Helen Hudson was a patient﻿—a case﻿—a precariously difficult occipital contusion.
When they had wheeled her in, he feared for a moment he would be unable to achieve that professional attitude. He hesitated, before making his first incision, as he might have hesitated had the scalpel been aimed at his own heart. Once that deft stroke had been accomplished, he was all surgeon.
Donelli stood by, attentively, gently sponging away the blood; marvelling at the uncanny accuracy with which the veins were clipped with forceps almost before they had a chance to signal their location; regarding with envious admiration the swiftness and sureness of those slim, strong, experienced fingers.
Twice Merrick had glanced up anxiously into the eyes of the anaesthetist﻿—it was no small matter to be conducting ether into those pummelled lungs﻿—but, apparently satisfied with what he read there, applied himself with renewed concentration to his task.
It was a terrific strain, and the little group in the operating room was strangely silent. By common consent it was understood that a moving drama was being enacted here﻿—a tragedy, perhaps. Any breath might be her last. That would depend upon the skill of the surgeon. Life and death here were to be determined by the promptness and accuracy of decisions in the removal of the clots. Too deep meant death; not deep enough meant blindness for life!
When the operation was all but finished, Donelli inquired, with an entreating look and a hand outstretched for the needle, if he might not do the scalp stitches; but Merrick shook his head.
The little procession crowded through the door. Bobby walked shakily into the adjacent dressing room; sat down on a white enamelled stool, his shoulders slumped; and contemplated his hands﻿ ﻿… Her blood!﻿ ﻿… Donelli and a nurse helped him off with his rubber gloves and out of his smock. The impulsive Italian himself insisted on mopping his guest’s face with a cold towel, eager to show his sympathy. It had been the most stirring event of his experience in a vocation singularly exposed to dramatic situations.
They tried, a little later, to persuade Merrick to eat something, but his dinner consisted of a stiff drink of brandy which he gulped greedily as a toper. It was useless to argue with him. He was determined to go at once to his patient’s bedside and wait results.
“But﻿—there’s nothing you can do,” expostulated Ardmore. “It will be hours before you can determine anything more than you know now, unless﻿—of course﻿ ﻿…”
“Exactly!” muttered Merrick. “It’s the *unless* that concerns me!﻿ ﻿… That﻿—and the threat of a quick pneumonia.”
Donelli and Ardmore went to dinner. As they parted at the hospital gate, the Italian said, “It’s too much to hope for. Our young friend will be deeply grieved. But﻿—it was a masterful piece of work!”
---
Her room was in semidarkness, but Merrick’s eyes, once they were accustomed to the gloom, caressed the loveliness of her face. He had not bandaged her eyes. They were closed; and the long black lashes﻿—incredibly long lashes﻿—made heavy shadows on her darkly flushed cheeks. Her breathing was regular, quiet﻿—almost too quiet, at times, and he would rise from his chair by the window and move anxiously toward the bed, his nerves keyed to the breaking point.
Mostly he sat resting his elbows on his knees with his chin in his hands, staring at her face, occasionally rousing when a longer breath, exhaled shudderingly, would bring him to her side, his stethoscope intent.
About midnight, he took a turn up and down in the corridor, and upon reentering the room, whispered into Julie Craig’s ear:
“Is her clothing in that closet?”
“Yes, doctor﻿ ﻿… Can I help you?”
He shook his head, stepped to the wardrobe, and after some search brought out a soiled and torn blue gown, fumbled at the neck of it, and, having found what he sought, restored the garment to the closet and closed the door.
Julie Craig watched him interestedly as he sat toying with the bit of jewellery he had abstracted from his patient’s clothing. Doubtless it was one of his gifts to her. There was some delicious secret connected with it. She wished she knew.
After a while, he rose, and, bending over her, whispered:
“You may go out and take a bit of exercise. I shall call you if you are needed.”
---
Dawn was breaking. The little clock ticked energetically on the bureau. Birds twittered sleepily, outside the window. Bells tolled matins.
There was a weary sigh from the bed. Julie Craig bent over it, solicitously.
And then, in that dear voice, curiously like a muted cello, between hysterical little sobs, Bobby Merrick’s patient murmured:
“Oh﻿—Blessed God﻿—*I can see!*”

===Chinese===

是——阿德莫尔医生在，大堂服务台答道；能不能见上，就难说了……是——今天实在忙……哈德森太太？……她还活着……请坐好吗？……服务台满含同情。
博比神经质地草草写了一行字。
“把这张卡片送给阿德莫尔医生，”他吩咐，“务必送到！”
几分钟后，一位四十五岁、头发花白的壮实男人快步穿过走廊，伸出手来。
“是——我是阿德莫尔。就在今天，我们还说起您呢，梅里克医生。真是莫大的荣幸……呢——您是为您的同胞哈德森太太来的……朋友，只怕我们救不了她……不——还没有动手术……还太早——您会懂的……脑震荡……多内利还得再等等……他认为今晚或许可以试了……但他不抱希望……有知觉吗？哦——时有时无；断断续续。我们让她安静着，您知道……她知道自己全瞎了——这一点我敢肯定。”
“那么挫伤正压着枕叶！”
“正是！端端正正在后脑！病灶很深！……还有那几根折断的肋骨。那也糟糕。降低了抵抗力……多内利中午已经断定头部手术无谓；可现在他又打算做了……这样的病例他见得不多……我方才还在想，要是——可是——我的天，伙计！您在这儿！该您来做！多内利会感激不尽！”
博比的心擂了起来。
“您真觉得他会愿意让我上？”
“愿意？他会说您是上天派来的！多内利是位出色的外科医生，可不搞专科。您会赢得他永久的感激！……来——我们看您的病人去……多内利那边，包在我身上……来——请。”
“稍等，阿德莫尔医生。”博比说着，从那条推着他往走廊去的手臂里挣脱出来，“见哈德森太太之前，我有话对您说。找个能私下谈的地方。”
阿德莫尔领他进了一间小客厅。
“我想您应当知道，”博比说，“这位年轻女子的性命，于我不止是职业上的关切；也不止是同国之人、相识之人那层意思……我一直存着娶她为妻的希望。”
“我的天！——好一个局面！”
“可不正是？下面还有呢。我们之间有一场很深的误会。就是说——她误会着我。既然如此——倘若她有一段清醒的间隙，还是别让她知道，她的医生是我。这样或许好得多。”
“可是，”阿德莫尔异议，“这个事实，兴许能给她多添几分抵抗力呢？”
“不是我们要的那种。”
“呢——您最清楚……我会嘱咐护士，尽力替您瞒住身份。”
---
是她嘴角那道垂落的弧线，把滚烫的泪逼上博比的眼眶。有一刻，他以为自己撑不住了。
阿德莫尔见他这样动情，倒有些担心。这不是一桩可以把感情带进来的手术。五指如钳，扣住来客的肩，他低声道：“稳住！您是她的医生；不是她的情人！”
他示意护士随他出去到走廊，留下博比单独对着他的病人。她微微动了动，抽了一口短促的、痛得断续的气，痛得她一缩；嘴唇一时收紧，又松开，回到那副怔忡的垂落。
博比拿起白床罩上摊着的那只手，双手握住。她的指头里，有一丝想象中最微弱的回握。亲爱的小家伙！至少她朦胧地知道有一只友好的手在握——不管这只手从哪里来。这也就算是一分了——不管怎样！
门开处，阿德莫尔同护士回来；护士的眼睛亮晶晶的。她共享着一桩重大的秘密。
“您也许愿意做一次检查，医生。”阿德莫尔说。
博比点头。护士领他去盥洗室，替他找了手术衣和手套。
病人摆好体位，临时绷带解开了。博比一见，呆住了。正像阿德莫尔说的，很深、很深。指尖触上去时，他猛吸了一口气，几乎成了声……英国医生那双有力的手指，又一次攥起年轻同僚肩头的一大把，咆哮：“记住！她是您的病人！”
“等无益处。”博比说，“请去请多内利医生。他一到，我们立刻进手术。”
---
六点钟，海伦被安上手术台；七点半，又移回她来时那张带轮的病床。
在那一个半钟头里，博比·梅里克凭意志的绝大努力，做着一个脑外科医生；海伦·哈德森是一个病人——一个病例——一处险象环生的枕部挫伤。
推她进来时，有一刻他怕自己摆不进那份职业姿态。下第一刀之前，他踌躇了——仿佛那刀要落在他自己的心上。那一记利落的手势一完成，他便整个是外科医生了。
多内利侍立在旁，轻轻地蘸去血；静脉几乎还来不及报出位置，止血钳便已夹上——那份莫测的精准叫他惊叹；那双纤长、有力、老练的手指的速度与笃定，他看着眼热。
有两回，梅里克焦虑地抬眼探问麻醉师的眼睛——往那两叶被打伤的肺里导乙醚，绝非小事——但显然，他在那里读到的使他满意，便重新凝神于手上的活。
这是场骇人的鏖战；手术室里这一小队人出奇地静。不约而同，人人明白：这里正演着一出动人的戏——兴许是一出悲剧。任何一口气都可能是她的最后一口。那要看外科医生的手艺。这里的生与死，系于清除凝血块时决断的快与准。太深即是死；不够深，便是一世盲！
手术全部但收未收之际，多内利眼光恳求着，伸手要针，问头皮的缝合可否赏他；梅里克摇了头。
小小的一列人挤出手术室的门。博比脚步发飘地走进隔壁更衣室；在一只白搪瓷圆凳上坐下，双肩塌陷；端详着自己的双手……她的血！……多内利和一位护士帮他褪下橡皮手套、脱去手术衣。这位意大利人按捺不住，亲自拿冷毛巾替客人擦脸，急于表达他的敬意。在他这一行当——天生多戏剧行当——的经历里，这是最惊心动魄的一件。
稍后，他们劝梅里克吃点什么；可他的晚饭只有一盏烈性的白兰地，像酒鬼似的仰脖灌了。同他争是无用的。他拿定主意立刻守到病人床边，等结果。
“可是——您什么也做不了呀。”阿德莫尔劝，“没有几个钟头，您探不出比现在更多的一分，除非——当然……”
“正是！”梅里克咕哝，“叫我挂心的就是那个‘除非’！……还有——突来一场肺炎的威胁。”
多内利同阿德莫尔去吃晚饭。在医院门口分手时，意大利人说：“希望是奢求了。我们的年轻朋友要大大伤心一场。不过——那是一件大师的活儿！”
---
她的房间半明半暗；待眼睛习惯了幽暗，梅里克的目光便爱抚起她脸庞的秀美。他没有给她包扎眼睛。眼阖着；长长的黑睫毛——长得叫人难以置信——在深红的两颊上投下浓影。她的呼吸匀净、安静——有时安静得过了头，他便从窗边的椅子里惊起，惶惶地趋近床边，神经绷到断弦的边缘。
多半时候他坐着，肘支膝上，下巴搁在掌心里，凝望她的脸；偶尔一声较长的、颤着呼出的息，把他唤到她身边，听诊器屏息以待。
午夜前后，他去走廊里踱了一个来回；再进屋时，对着朱莉·克雷格的耳朵低声问：
“她的衣裳在那口柜子里？”
“是的，医生……要我帮忙吗？”
他摇头，走到衣柜前，翻寻了一阵，取出一件又脏又破的蓝裙，在领口摸索，找到要找的东西，把衣裳归回柜里，掩上柜门。
他坐下，把那件从病人衣裳里取出的小饰物在指间摩挲；朱莉·克雷格看得有趣。多半是他送她的一件礼物。这里头连着什么甜美的秘密呢。她巴不得知道。
过了一阵，他起身，俯向她，低语：
“你出去活动活动吧。需要你时，我会叫。”
---
天破晓了。五斗柜上的小钟起劲地走。窗外鸟儿睡意惺忪地啁啾。钟声报着晨祷。
床上传来一声疲惫的叹息。朱莉·克雷格关切地俯下身去。
随后，在那副亲爱的嗓音里——奇异地像一把加了弱音器的大提琴——夹着歇斯底里的细碎呜咽，博比·梅里克的病人喃喃道：
“哦——慈悲的上帝——我能看见了！”

## XXI / 二十一
===Original===

Julie Craig was a romantic little thing, and the drama in which she had been assigned a role was quite to her liking. It was not a fat part as to voluminosity of lines, but it kept her almost continuously on the stage in the thick of action.
Very thrilling it was to feel herself the chief custodian of so valuable a secret. She had resolved to safeguard it against any hazard until the impressive moment arrived for its sensational release.
As for Doctor Ardmore’s attitude toward the matter, he was so hilarious that morning, to learn of the complete success of young Merrick’s operation that it mattered little to him when or how his attractive patient should discover the identity of her benefactor. Ardmore was British. It occurred to him that whatever misunderstandings might have estranged these two interesting young Americans, the machinery of reconciliation was now in good running order. If they couldn’t execute some kind of a treaty in the face of this theatrical lifesaving event, they deserved to go their separate ways without expectation of sympathy.
Donelli, by race and temperament cordial toward the grand opera aspects of the situation, rather hoped the fair patient’s identification of Merrick might not be brought about until she was at least sufficiently clear of mind to get a real emotional wallop out of the occasion, and sincerely hoped he might find some good reason to be present when it happened.
Julie was for postponing the great moment as long as possible. To her active imagination it was a situation to be nibbled at appreciatively; toyed with﻿—cat and mouse fashion; savoured, rolled about on the tongue. She could not conceive anything more bitterly disappointing than a prosaic anticlimax, now that the dramatic materials were all in hand for a perfectly whopping curtain! It made her shudder to contemplate the possibility of his popping into the room while his idol was in the throes of nausea, as she was almost sure to be, a dozen times before the day was out. The whole affair, Julie decided, should be insured against a commonplace outcome, and dedicated herself to that movement with almost as much concern as if she were one of the principals.
Pursuant to that determination she had left word at the office that she wished to communicate with Doctor Merrick as soon as he returned from breakfast and the brief nap he had promised himself. When the message came up that the young American surgeon was in the building, Julie went to the stairs to meet him. He greeted her warmly, told her she had been very faithful, suggested she would be better off for a few hours’ sleep. He would see to it that she was relieved at once.
She shook her head.
“I am not sleepy. I shall wait until this afternoon. Doctor Ardmore told me my patient was not to know who was treating her. So﻿—I have restored the bandage to her eyes. I told her they should not encounter the strong light for several hours. I hope I haven’t done wrong, sir.”
“You are very resourceful,” said Bobby, endeavouring to keep a straight face. “Is there, by any chance, something else I ought to know before looking in?”
She coloured with conscious pride.
“Yes, sir. If you don’t mind, I have told her that since she does not speak Italian fluently, whatever conversation she may have with you is to be interpreted by me﻿ ﻿… And Doctor Donelli can be asked in, this morning, while you make the examination. Is that agreeable?”
“Miss Craig,” said Bobby solemnly, “you’re wasting your talents here. You should belong to the diplomatic corps.”
“I hope you’re not making fun, sir﻿ ﻿… Doctor Ardmore said she wasn’t to know﻿—and I saw no other way of keeping it from her.”
“You have done very nicely,” commended Bobby. “I’ll wash up and change my coat.”
---
Helen had been creeping out of her etherized torpor, at closer intervals of full consciousness, since seven; had even smiled gratefully under Julie’s little tendernesses.
“So glad!” she had murmured when Julie assured her she was going to be well and sound as ever.
“And﻿—I can see!”
She raised a white hand and tugged weakly at the edge of the bandage over her eyes. Julie hastened to restore it.
“Tomorrow, perhaps,” she promised. “He wants them protected today.”
“Very well,” with an obedient sigh, “he ought to know, I suppose.”
Julie was a tense little figure when Doctor Merrick came in, arrayed in his borrowed surgical gown, accompanied by the volatile Donelli who greeted her with a bombardment of praise for having contributed so much to this happy eventuality. She returned a volley warning him against an accidental disclosure of their secret. Donelli nodded vigorously and chuckled.
Doctor Merrick stepped at once to the bedside table, consulted the chart, and proceeded to verify the latest pulse-count.
“Please tell the doctor,” said Helen slowly, “that the bandage around my chest is so very tight. Could he make it more comfortable?”
Julie dutifully relayed the request in a swift Italian sentence composed of one word of two hundred syllables﻿—mostly vowels.
Seeing that the surgical bandage around her chest had been Donelli’s affair, it was only a natural courtesy for Doctor Merrick to permit his colleague to decide whether or not he wished it changed. Stepping back, he silently signed to Doctor Donelli that this matter was up to him. But Donelli vehemently protested, with outspread fingers, that he was only too eager to discover his guest’s technique.
The opportunity for a debate not being favourable, Bobby turned down the sheet, examined the broad bandage, unpinned it, unfolded it down to the creamy-white satin skin, carefully inspected the discoloured field of the fractures, and expertly replaced the bandage.
“Oh﻿—that is ever so much better,” sighed his patient, gratefully.
Julie shook loose a cascade of musical Italian, and the doctor grunted his receipt of it.
“He has very gentle hands,” murmured Helen sleepily.
“Shall I tell him that, madame?” asked Julie, her eyes brightly searching his face.
“No﻿ ﻿… Tell him I am thankful he has made me see again.”
Impulsively Bobby entertained a foolhardy notion. In a clumsy Italian phrase, remembered from boyhood, he mumbled something about the pleasure being all his own﻿—and dismayed by his own audacity, walked to the window to write a prescription.
Julie’s eyes were intent upon her patient’s half-covered face.
