She was about to interpret the doctor’s remark when she noted that the full lips parted; then, that the lower lip was gently pinioned between an even row of white teeth, while the dimples deepened ever so little and a slow flush crept across her cheeks.
With agitated fingers, Julie refastened the smock at the throat, her sentimental little heart beating wildly﻿ ﻿… She knows!﻿—thought Julie﻿ ﻿… Bending over her, she said gently:
“But perhaps you understood what the doctor was just saying. Is it not so?”
There was no reply. She had drowsed off again. But the smile lingered on her lips, and the flush lingered on her cheeks.
As they went out, Doctor Merrick beckoned Julie to the door and said, in an undertone:
“You might remove that bandage from her eyes while she sleeps. It will be more comfortable.”
---
Two hours later she roused again. Having soberly regarded Julie for some minutes, she fumbled at the neck of her smock and drew out the little silver cross. Holding it tightly in both hands and pressing it to her heart, she inquired:
“How did you know I wanted it?”
“I did not know, madame; or you should have had it from the first.”
There was a long pause.
“When did you put it on me? I just now discovered it.”
“I did not put it on you, madame.”
There was another long silence.
Observing that her patient was dabbing clumsily at her eyes with the corner of the sheet, Julie hastened to restore her handkerchief; then turned and walked thoughtfully to the window.
“Did you see him do it?”
Julie did not turn from the window as she replied, unsteadily:
“No, madame. He invited me to leave the room.”
“The﻿—poor﻿—dear!”
---
At nine that evening Marion Dawson arrived and Bobby met her at the station.
Immediately upon reading the news of the accident, she had been fortunate in making connections with the best westbound train of the day. Her frantic telegram of inquiry, addressed to the hospital when en route, had been handed to Merrick who had wired reassuringly to her train. She had received the message at ten. It did not greatly surprise her to have a telegram from him, knowing that he would have been likely to see the account of the disaster while in Paris.
“Oh, Bobby﻿—how wonderful!” she exclaimed tearfully, when he told her that everything was favourable to a prompt recovery. “Can I see her?”
“Better not tonight. She will be brighter in the morning.”
“And I suppose you two blessed things have found that you’re necessary to each other, haven’t you?”
“Well﻿—not yet,” he said, hesitatingly. “You see, I have her at a rather awkward disadvantage. I performed the operation myself. I don’t wish to make capital of any obligation she might feel toward me. In fact﻿—she doesn’t know I’m here, at all.”
Marion was pink with indignation. The taxi-driver stood by the open door of the cab waiting for them to step in; but she ignored the gesture and blazed at her compatriot angrily.
“Bobby Merrick﻿—I think that’s simply disgraceful! You’ve always kept the dear soul in the dark and made her feel irresponsible. And here you are again with some more of your wretched secrets! Well﻿—we’ll see if you’re going to use her that way, this time! When I see her in the morning, I mean to tell her! You don’t need to think I’m a party to any more of these mysteries!”
He rather suspected that she meant it. On the short ride over to the Quirinal where he had reserved a room for her, he told her as much as he could explain of the operation, attempting to divert her attention from her annoyance over his attitude towards Helen.
“I am leaving in the morning for Vienna,” he said, after making sure she was receiving proper attention at the hotel desk. “Doctor Donelli can do the dressings as well as I. There is no danger now, and no reason for my staying on; especially since you seem bent on informing her.”
“It’s the only decent thing to do,” retorted Marion obdurately. “She has a right to know﻿ ﻿… Well﻿—give my love to Jack and tell him I’ll be back when Helen doesn’t need me any more. I wonder if she’ll forgive me!”
“I hope so, my dear; but I wouldn’t bet much on it﻿ ﻿… Goodbye!”
---
Later in the evening he had a consultation with Donelli and felt confident he was leaving his patient in competent hands. He stepped into Helen’s room, found her sleeping, took her hand and held it for a moment, and walked out with only a nod for the nurse who had relieved Julie. At the desk he scribbled a note to her, thanking her for “exceptional faithfulness and originality,” and enclosed a substantial tender of gratitude (part of which she used to defray a three months’ vacation in Switzerland).
Reconciliation was effected promptly and silently, the next morning, when Marion called. They kissed each other and wept a little. Julie excused herself and left them alone together.
“Helen, dear,” whispered Marion, the moment the door had closed, “you could never guess who did your operation?”
“Oh, yes, I could,” drawled Helen, smiling.
“Good! He thought he had such a secret! When did you find out?”
She laughed﻿—with a little wince of pain.
“He talked some Italian for me, yesterday.”
“And you recognized his voice?”
“Instantly.”
“He doesn’t know it; I am confident.”
“Well, he probably will before the day is over.”
Before Marion had a chance to reply, Doctor Donelli came bustling in, trailed by Julie, and smilingly approached the bedside.
Helen looked up inquiringly.
“Isn’t Doctor Merrick coming this morning, Miss Craig?”
Julie shook her head.
“He left for Vienna at seven, dear,” said Marion. “I told him I intended to tell you he was here, so﻿—off he goes!”
“How like him!” said Helen, smiling.

===Chinese===

朱莉·克雷格是个浪漫的小人儿；派到她名下的这出戏，很合她的胃口。论台词份量不算个肥角，可她几乎始终在台上，置身戏芯里。
自觉是这么一件贵重秘密的总看守，那份刺激自不待言。她拿定主意，无论冒什么风险也要守住它，直到那个震撼揭盅的大时刻降临。
至于阿德莫尔医生对这件事的态度：那天早晨听说小梅里克的手术大获全胜，他欢喜得顾不上讲究时机和方式了——他那迷人的病人几时、以何种方式发现恩人的身份，都无所谓了。阿德莫尔是英国人。他寻思：这两位有趣的美国年轻人之间无论有过什么隔阂，和解的机器如今运转良好。面对这样一场戏剧性的救命，若还谈不成一纸和约，那就活该各奔前程，休想博人同情。
多内利呢，血统与性情都向着局面的歌剧大场面，倒盼着金发病人认出梅里克的那一刻，来得越晚越好——至少等她神志够清，好把那一记情感的闷棍结结实实吃个正着；并且诚心诚意巴望，事情发生时他能寻个正大光明的理由在场。
朱莉则主张把那伟大时刻尽量往后推。在她活跃的想象里，这是一个宜于细细咂摸的场面；宜于把玩——猫和老鼠那样；宜于品，宜在舌尖上滚。戏剧的家什已一应俱全，只待一记满堂的大幕，此刻若落进一个乏味的虎头蛇尾，她想不出比这更苦的失望。想到他也许会在偶像正呕得死去活来时闯进房来——这一天里她几乎注定要呕上十来回——朱莉就不寒而栗。这整桩事，朱莉断定，必须投保，防一个庸俗的结局；她以几乎不逊于当事人的关切，投身于这项事业。
秉着这份决心，她在办公室留了话：梅里克医生吃过早饭、打了他说好的那个小盹一回来，她就要同他联络。年轻的美国外科医生到了的信儿一传上来，朱莉便迎到楼梯口。他热络地招呼她，夸她尽心，提议她最好去睡几个钟头。他这就安排人立刻替她的班。
她摇头。
“我不困。我等到今天下午再说。阿德莫尔医生嘱咐过，我的病人不能知道是谁在给她治。所以——我把她眼睛上的绷带又缠上了。我跟她说，几个钟头内不能见强光。我没有做错吧，先生。”
“你真有办法。”博比说，努力绷住脸，“碰巧还有别的什么事，是我进去之前该知道的吗？”
她意识到骄傲，红了脸。
“有的，先生。您若不介意：我已经告诉她，既然她意大利语说得不利索，她同您之间无论谈什么，都由我来传译……还有，今早您检查的时候，可以把多内利医生请进来。这样妥当吗？”
“克雷格小姐，”博比郑重地说，“你在这里屈才了。你该进外交界。”
“您可别取笑我，先生……阿德莫尔医生说她不能知道——我想不出别的法子瞒她。”
“你办得很漂亮。”博比嘉许道，“我去洗洗手，换件外衣。”
---
七点以来，海伦从乙醚的昏沉里一步步往外爬，清醒的间隔越来越密；在朱莉的小小温存底下，甚至露过感激的笑。
“太好了！”朱莉向她担保她会像从前一样康健时，她这样喃喃，“还有——我能看见了！”
她抬起一只白手，虚弱地扯了扯眼上绷带的边。朱莉赶紧替她按住。
“明天吧，兴许。”她许诺，“他今天要它们歇着。”
“好吧。”一声顺从的叹，“想来他最懂。”
梅里克医生穿着借来的手术衣进来时，朱莉是根绷紧的小弦；同行的是喜怒形于色的多内利，一阵连珠炮的夸奖轰她，赞她为这圆满结局出了大力。她回敬一排连珠炮，警告他严防无意间泄露天机。多内利使劲点头，暗笑。
梅里克医生径直走到床头柜前，看病历卡，复核最新一次的脉搏计数。
“请告诉医生，”海伦缓缓地说，“我胸口的绷带勒得太紧了。能不能弄得舒服些？”
朱莉尽职地转达，一句意大利语脱口而出——一个词足足两百个音节，多半是元音。
既然胸口的手术绷带是多内利的份内事，梅里克医生退后一步，默然示意：这件事由他定夺。多内利却十指张开，激烈推辞：他巴不得领教领教贵客的手法呢。
辩论的机会不宜，博比翻下床单，查看那道宽绷带，解开别针，一层层展到那乳白缎子般的皮肤，细看骨折那一片淤色，又内行地把绷带原样缠好。
“哦——这可好受多了。”病人感激地叹道。
朱莉抖落一串音乐般的意大利语；医生咕哝一声，表示收到。
“他这双手真轻。”海伦睡意朦胧地喃喃。
“我转告他吗，夫人？”朱莉问，一双亮晶晶的眼睛搜着他的脸。
“不……告诉他，我感激他让我重见光明。”
一时莽撞，博比起了一个冒险的念头。他凭少年时记下的一句笨拙意大利语，含含糊糊说了句“荣幸全在我这边”——随即被自己的大胆吓着，踱到窗边开处方去了。
朱莉的眼睛钉在病人半掩的脸上。她正要传译医生那句话，忽见那两片丰满的唇分开了；继而，下唇被一排整齐的白牙轻轻衔住；酒窝极轻极轻地深了一分，一抹缓缓的红晕爬上双颊。
朱莉的手指发着颤，重新系好领口的扣子，那颗多情的小心肝狂跳……她知道了！——朱莉想……她俯下身，轻轻说：
“不过，方才医生说的话，您兴许听懂了。是不是？”
没有回答。她又睡着了。可笑意还留在唇上，红晕还留在颊上。
出门时，梅里克医生把朱莉唤到门边，低声说：
“趁她睡，把她眼上的绷带解了吧。舒服些。”
---
两个钟头后她又醒了。她端详了朱莉好几分钟，伸手到病衣领口里摸索，掏出那只小银十字。双手攥紧，按在心口，她问：
“你怎么知道我想要它？”
“我不知道，夫人；不然一开始就给您了。”
好一阵静默。
“你什么时候给我戴上的？我方才发现。”
“不是我给您戴的，夫人。”
又是一阵长长的静默。
见病人正拿床单角笨拙地按眼睛，朱莉忙把手帕递还给她；随后转身，若有所思地踱到窗前。
“你看见他戴的吗？”
朱莉背对着窗答，嗓音发颤：
“没有，夫人。是他请我出去的。”
“那——个——可怜的——亲爱的人！”
---
当晚九点，玛丽恩·道森到了，博比到车站接她。
一读到事故的消息，她便顺当赶上了当天最好的一班西行车。她在车上发往医院的急电，落到了梅里克手里；他回电到她的车上，报了平安。十点钟她收到电文。是他来的电，她并不太惊讶——她知道他在巴黎多半也见了报。
“哦，博比——太好了！”听他说一切都利于迅速康复，她含泪欢呼，“我能见她吗？”
“今晚最好别去。明早她会精神些。”
“我猜你们这两个有福的，已经发现彼此谁也离不开谁了，对不对？”
“呢——还没有。”他迟疑着说，“是这样的，我如今占着她一个相当别扭的便宜。手术是我亲手做的。我不想拿她可能生的感激做本钱。其实——她根本不知道我在这里。”
玛丽恩气得满脸粉红。出租车司机立在敞开的车门边候他们上车；她只当没看见，冲她的同胞发火。
“博比·梅里克——我看这简直丢人！你一向把那亲爱的人蒙在鼓里，叫她自觉是个不负责任的人。这会儿你又来这一套，又抱着你那些破秘密！好——这回你休想再这么摆布她！明早我一见她，就告诉她！你别指望我再入伙你们这些谜！”
他相当疑心她说到做到。去奎里纳尔饭店的短短一路——他替她订好了房——他把手术能讲的都讲了，想引开她对他那份态度的恼火。
“我明早动身去维也纳。”在饭店柜台前看着她受到妥当的接待之后，他说，“多内利医生换药不逊于我。如今没有危险了，我没有理由再留；尤其你既打定主意要告诉她。”
“本来就该这么办。”玛丽恩执拗地回嘴，“她有权知道……呢——替我问候杰克，告诉他等海伦不再需要我，我就回去。也不知她肯不肯饶我！”
“盼她肯，亲爱的；可我不敢下多大注……再见！”
---
晚间晚些时候，他同多内利碰了个头，放心地把病人留在了内行手里。他踱进海伦的房间，见她睡着，握了握她的手，对换班顶替朱莉的护士只点了点头，便走了出来。在服务台，他给朱莉草草写了张便条，谢她“格尽的忠诚与别出心裁”，附上一份可观的谢仪——其中一部分，后来被她拿去瑞士度了三个月的假。
和解在次日上午玛丽恩来探时，迅速而无声地完成了。两人拥吻，洒了几点泪。朱莉知趣地退了出去，留她们单独相对。
“海伦，亲爱的，”门一合上，玛丽恩就低声说，“你永远猜不到，你的手术是谁做的吧？”
“哦，不，猜得到。”海伦拖着腔，笑了。
“好！他还当揣着天大的秘密呢！你几时发现的？”
她笑了——笑里带着一点痛的抽搐。
“昨天，他为我说了几句意大利语。”
“你听出了他的嗓音？”
“一听便知。”
他还不知道；这一点我有把握。”
“呢，只怕等不到天黑，他就要知道了。”
玛丽恩还没来得及接话，多内利医生一阵风进来，朱莉缀在后头，笑吟吟直奔床头。
海伦探询地抬眼。
“梅里克医生今早不来吗，克雷格小姐？”
朱莉摇头。
“他七点去维也纳了，亲爱的。”玛丽恩说，“我告诉他我打算把他在这里的事告诉你，于是——他就撤了！”
“他就是这个脾气！”海伦说着，笑了。

## XXII / 二十二
===Original===

Doctor Merrick’s unusual capacity for pinpoint concentration upon problems of scientific research was not quite up to par during his collaboration with Doctor Arnstadt. The Viennese surgeon seemed every way satisfied, and rejoiced in his close association with his young American colleague, but Bobby was too restless and distracted to make the most of his opportunity.
Jack Dawson had been quick to understand his friend’s mood.
“Bobby,” he advised, one early August night, as they were finishing their dinner in the low-ceilinged grill room at Hangel’s, “I don’t want to meddle, but I believe you really ought to run over to the Imperial City and do your stuff. You’re getting to be damned poor company, and I think you have something weighty on your conscience.”
Bobby nodded soberly.
“You’re right!﻿ ﻿… I’ll go﻿—tomorrow!”
---
Marion had stayed on in Rome. Jack’s daily letter from her constituted the log of Helen’s rather tedious trip through convalescence.
It was a great day in Vienna﻿—demanding a bit of celebration that night﻿—when the news came that she had sat up for half an hour.
Now she had been wheeled out into the shady patio﻿ ﻿… Now she had taken a short walk﻿ ﻿… Now they were both at the Quirinal, and every afternoon Helen was down in the garden, in the hotel court﻿ ﻿… Now she was taking drives in the evening﻿ ﻿… You could hardly find the scar, any more﻿ ﻿… Helen was so happy.
---
On the morning of the sixth of August, Jack and Doctor Arnstadt saw Bobby off. After the train had slipped quietly out of the station, Jack went to the telegraph booth and wired to Marion. He did not tell her it was a secret that Bobby was on the way. She would not have kept it, in any event. She was all done with riddles.
“Who do you think’s coming?” she cried, romping into Helen’s room waving her telegram.
“When does he arrive?”
“Tomorrow afternoon﻿—about six!﻿ ﻿… Isn’t it wonderful?”
“I’m going, dear! I don’t feel quite up to it.”
“Why﻿—the very idea!﻿ ﻿… You can’t!﻿ ﻿… When you know he’s on the way?﻿ ﻿… He’ll be wretchedly disappointed!”
“But﻿—I don’t know he’s coming﻿—that is, not officially﻿ ﻿… He probably didn’t want me to know, or he would have informed me.”
There was no talking her out of it. She was going! That night they paid a farewell call at the hospital. Julie Craig shyly inquired of Mrs. Dawson if Doctor Merrick was still in Europe, and was appalled to learn that he was arriving in Rome tomorrow.
“But﻿—you will have gone, madame!” exclaimed Julie, looking reproachfully at her patient﻿ ﻿… Surely this affair was being badly managed by somebody.
---
All that the concierge at the Quirinal seemed to remember at first about the whereabouts of the two young American women was that they had left, that noon, for Paris. Upon further reflection, and in appreciation of the colour of the banknote which Doctor Merrick was twisting in his fingers, he recalled that Madame Hudson’s heavier baggage had been expressed directly to Le Havre. He had himself seen to it. Yes﻿—she was sailing on the *Île de France* on Thursday. At all events, her trunks were.
Bobby spent an hour, pacing up and down the flagging in the garden of the Quirinal, planning his next move. This game of hide and seek couldn’t﻿—mustn’t go on any longer! He resolved to corner her and take her by force!
He sent a long telegram to Jack Dawson, confiding the full particulars of the enormous audacity he had decided to commit. He had made his reputation with her by various acts of impudence. This would be the crowning deed which would make all his former impertinences seem bland. He was very nervous as he prepared for it.
Events moved very swiftly, once he had come to his decision. He engaged an aeroplane and flew to Paris. Between the racket of the triple engines and the furious pounding of his heart, it was a noisy trip. There was a hasty half-hour with his mother, in which he persuaded her to forgive him on the strength of a promise that he would return and be with her at Christmas.
At the down town office of the French Line, he engaged the most luxurious suite on the *Île de France*. Next morning, he flew to Havre, arriving an hour before the boat-train from Paris.
It was a long and anxious hour. Never had he been so utterly stampeded by his own emotions.
Seeing to it that his light luggage was in his quarters, and having strolled about through the commodious suite to make sure it was ready for occupancy﻿—he had retained enough presence of mind to order the place filled with flowers﻿—he went up to the captain’s cabin, renewed acquaintance, and asked a favour.
Then he went down and took up his stand at the foot of the first-class gangway, waiting with an impatience almost too urgent to be borne.
Whatever would she think?
---
Slowly the train crept into the shed, across the way, and the passengers swarmed in little groups about their hand baggage. Presently the procession began to move stragglingly towards the ship.
Bobby saw her coming, tripping smartly along with two porters attentively close behind her.
Mauve﻿—this time﻿ ﻿… Snug, saucy, cloche hat﻿—mauve﻿ ﻿… A tailored suit of mauve that sculptured every curve of her.
She sighted him waiting for her. He knew she had seen him. Now her eyes widened and her lips parted as their gaze met. She advanced steadily towards him with a tread almost martial.
As she neared him, he did not extend his hand, as she had thought he might. He held out both arms; and, to his inexpressible delight, she walked confidently into them, shyly laid her slim fingers on his coat lapels, looked up into his face, and smiled.
“Put madame’s baggage in Suite B,” said Bobby, in a proprietorial tone to the porters.
“You are travelling with me!” he explained rather unsteadily. “The captain is marrying us, this afternoon!”
“Yes, dear,” she said softly. “I know.”
Bobby’s arms tightened about her.
“How did you know?” he grinned, boyishly.
“Well﻿—let’s see. You wired it to Doctor Dawson, and he wired it to Marion, and she wired it to me﻿ ﻿… Awfully roundabout way to learn one was being married, wasn’t it?”
“But﻿—but﻿—you’re for it, aren’t you?” he pleaded, searching her eyes.
She smiled.
“Perhaps we should go aboard, Bobby. We’re blocking the traffic.”

===Chinese===

同阿恩施塔特医生合作的这段日子，梅里克医生那副把科研难题钉住了钻的非常功夫，略略欠了火候。这位维也纳外科医生样样满意，为他同年轻美国同僚的亲密共事欢欣；可博比心浮气躁，心不在焉，没能把这次机会榨到十足。
杰克·道森一眼看穿了朋友的心事。
“博比，”八月初的一个夜晚，两人正在亨格尔饭店那间低顶棚的烧烤厅里吃完饭，他进言，“我不想多管闲事，可我看你实在该奔一趟帝王之城，露一手你的绝活。你这旅伴当得越来越不够意思了；我看你心里压着一块大石头。”
博比郑重点头。
“你说得对！……我去——明天！”
---
玛丽恩留在了罗马。杰克每天收到的她的信，构成了海伦那段漫长乏味的康复之旅的航行日志。
维也纳的大日子——当夜少不了小小庆祝一番——消息传来：她坐起来半个钟头了。
这会儿她被推到荫凉的天井里了……这会儿她短距离散步了……这会儿她们都住进了奎里纳尔，每天下午海伦都下到旅馆内院的花园里……这会儿她傍晚坐车兜风了……那道疤，如今几乎找不见了……海伦快活极了。
---
八月六日早晨，杰克和阿恩施塔特医生送博比上了车。火车悄悄滑出站台后，杰克去了电报亭，给玛丽恩发电报。博比正在路上这件事，他没叮嘱她保密。反正她也保不住。她跟谜语彻底两清了。
“你猜谁来？”她挥着电报，欢蹦着冲进海伦的房间。
“他几时到？”
“明天下午——六点前后！……妙不妙？”
“我要走了，亲爱的。我自觉顶不下来。”
“哎呀——瞧你说的！……你不能走！……明知道他人在路上？……他要大失所望的！”
“可是——他来我并不‘知道’——就是说，没人正式通知我……他多半不想让我知道；不然他会通知我的。”
谁也劝不动她。她走定了！当晚两人上医院作别。朱莉·克雷格怯生生地问道森太太，梅里克医生还在欧洲吗；一听说他明天就到罗马，她吓了一跳。
“可是——那时您已经走了呀，夫人！”朱莉喊，嗔怪地望着她的病人……这场事，管保是让谁给办坏了。
---
奎里纳尔的门房，起先关于两位美国少妇的下落，只记得她们当天中午去了巴黎。再一寻思，又掂了掂梅里克医生指间捻着的那张钞票的颜色，他记起来了：哈德森太太的大件行李，是直发勒阿弗尔的。他亲自经手的。是——她星期四乘“法兰西岛号”启航。至少，她的箱子是。
博比在奎里纳尔的花园石径上踱了一个钟头，盘算下一步。这场捉迷藏不能再——绝不能再拖下去了！他决意堵住她，干脆用强！
他给杰克·道森发去一封长电，把自己决定要干的那桩泼天大胆的底细全盘托出。他早凭种种冒犯在她那里挣下了名声。这一件将是加冕之作，叫他从前的一切无礼都显得温良。备办的时候，他紧张得很。
决心一下，事态便飞转起来。他雇了一架飞机，飞往巴黎。三台引擎的轰鸣夹着心跳的狂擂，一路喧天。他同母亲匆促会了半个钟头，凭一句圣诞必回陪伴的许诺，讨得了她的饶恕。
在法国邮船公司的城里营业所，他订下了“法兰西岛号”上最豪华的套房。次晨，他飞抵勒阿弗尔，比巴黎来的船车早到一个钟头。
那是漫长而揪心的一个钟头。他从来不曾被自己的感情冲得这样溃不成军。
他看着轻便行李安置进舱房，又在宽敞的套房里踱了一圈，确认它随时可住——他还算留着点心思，吩咐把满舱布置上鲜花——然后上船长舱房叙旧，讨了一个情。
随后他下来，在头等舱跳板口站定，候着；那份急切，急得几乎担不住。
她又会怎么想呢？
---
火车缓缓爬进对面的棚站；旅客们一小团一小团围着自己的手提行李。不一会儿，队伍零零落落地朝船移动过来。
博比望见她来了，轻快地碎步走着，两名搬运工殷勤地紧随其后。
藕荷色——这一回……箍头的小钟帽，俏皮而娇俏——藕荷色……一身藕荷色的定制套裙，把她每一道曲线都雕了出来。
她望见了候着她的他。他知道她看见了他。此刻目光相接，她的眼睛睁大，双唇张开。她步步向他逼近，步子几乎带着军人的节奏。
走近了，他并没有像她以为的那样伸出手来。他张开的是两条臂膀；而叫他难以言表欢喜的是，她满怀信任地径直走进了这怀抱，羞怯地把纤细的手指搭上他的衣领，仰起脸，望着他，笑了。
“太太的行李送 B 套房。”博比用一副主人的口吻吩咐搬运工。
“您这趟同我一起走！”他微微发着颤地解释，“船长今天下午为我们证婚！”
“好的，亲爱的。”她柔声说，“我知道。”
博比的双臂把她拢紧了。
“你怎么知道的？”他咧开孩子气的笑。
“呢——我想想。你打电报给道森医生，他打电报给玛丽恩，玛丽恩再打电报给我……打听到自己要结婚了，绕这么个大圈子，是不是？”
“可是——可是——你是肯的，对不对？”他恳求，搜索着她的眼睛。
她笑了。
“咱们该上船了吧，博比。咱们把路都堵上了。”
